Groups acting on trees with Tits’ independence property ( P ) (\mathrm{P})
Colin D. Reid 1 and Simon M. Smith 2 , with an appendix by Stephan Tornier 1 1 The University of Newcastle, School of Mathematical and Physical Sciences, Callaghan, NSW 2308, Australia 2 Charlotte Scott Research Centre for Algebra, University of Lincoln, Lincoln, U.K.

Abstract
A 1970 article of J. Tits concerning groups acting on trees introduced an independence property ( P ) (\mathrm{P}) as a condition to produce the first examples of nonlinear nondiscrete locally compact simple groups, answering a question of J. P. Serre. This property has become very important in the recent development of the theory of totally disconnected, locally compact (t.d.l.c.) groups, with the majority of new constructions of compactly generated simple t.d.l.c. groups using ( P ) (\mathrm{P}) or related ideas. The primary tool for analysing groups acting on trees is Bass–Serre theory, but this theory is of limited use when constructing compactly generated simple t.d.l.c. groups. Instead, most new constructions have been inspired by ideas in a 2000 article by M. Burger and Sh. Mozes in which they describe a group 𝐔 ⁡ ( F ) \mathbf{U}(F) of automorphisms of an infinite regular tree that is ‘universal’ with respect to some specified local action F F . A generalisation, due to the second author, is a group 𝐔 ⁡ ( F 1 , F 2 ) \mathbf{U}(F_{1},F_{2}) of automorphisms of an infinite biregular tree that is ‘universal’ with respect to two specified local actions F 1 , F 2 F_{1},F_{2} ; this construction was used to show that there is a continuum of isomorphism classes of compactly generated simple t.d.l.c. groups. Both 𝐔 ⁡ ( F ) \mathbf{U}(F) and 𝐔 ⁡ ( F 1 , F 2 ) \mathbf{U}(F_{1},F_{2}) enjoy property ( P ) (\mathrm{P}) . In this paper we aim to advance the local-to-global theory of groups acting on trees by developing a ‘local action’ complement to classical Bass–Serre theory. We describe, for a closed group G G of automorphisms of a (not necessarily locally finite) tree T T something called a local action diagram: a graph decorated with the local actions of G G . A local action diagram plays a role in our theory that is analogous to a graph of groups in Bass–Serre theory. In place of the universal cover of a graph of groups, we define the universal group of a local action diagram. In this context, the groups 𝐔 ⁡ ( F ) \mathbf{U}(F) and 𝐔 ⁡ ( F 1 , F 2 ) \mathbf{U}(F_{1},F_{2}) play analogous roles to the HNN extension and amalgamated free product respectively in Bass–Serre theory. We then show how to determine whether the universal group has certain properties, such as geometric density, compact generation and simplicity, directly from the local action diagram. Our theory allows us to completely describe all closed groups of automorphisms of trees with Tits’ independence property ( P ) (\mathrm{P}) : they are precisely the universal groups of local action diagrams.



Contents

1
Introduction

Actions on trees have long played an important role in group theory. The most well-established perspective is that of Bass–Serre theory and the theory of ends of groups, in which actions on trees are interpreted as a generalisation of the free product and HNN constructions. In addition, a complementary approach has emerged based on local actions, that is, the action of a vertex stabiliser on the neighbouring vertices. In particular, two articles concerning groups acting on trees have been very important for the recent development of the theory of totally disconnected, locally compact (t.d.l.c.) groups: a 1970 article
[
20
]
of J. Tits, which introduced property
(\mathrm{P})
as a condition to produce the first examples of nonlinear nondiscrete locally compact simple groups, showing for instance that the automorphism group of a regular tree is virtually simple; and a 2000 article
[
2
]
of M. Burger and Sh. Mozes, which rediscovered the approach of Tits and used it to produce an interesting class of (virtually) simple t.d.l.c. groups acting on trees with property
(\mathrm{P})
, which moreover arise naturally in the study of lattices in products of trees. Since then, the majority of new constructions of compactly generated simple t.d.l.c. groups have used the ideas of
[
20
]
and
[
2
]
. More recently in
[
19
]
, the second named author generalised the Burger–Mozes construction to obtain a kind of product of permutation groups, often resulting in a permutation group that is both primitive and simple; this was used to show that there are
2^{\aleph_{0}}
isomorphism types of nondiscrete compactly generated simple t.d.l.c. groups.

The groups constructed by Burger and Mozes in
[
2
]
, and by the second author in
[
19
]
, are foundational constructions in a growing body of work that might now be called the
local-to-global theory of groups acting on trees
. In these two constructions, for an infinite tree
T
, some desired ‘local action’ is specified on balls of radius
1
in
T
, and the construction, subject to some mild conditions on the specified local action, then generates a subgroup
U\leq\mathrm{Aut}(T)
that is ‘universal’ with respect to the specified local action; this is,
U
contains an
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
-conjugate of all subgroups of
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
that have the specified local action. These universal groups have property
(\mathrm{P})
.

The local-to-global approach has a significant advantage over Bass–Serre theory for constructing nondiscrete groups acting on trees; that is actions
(T,G)
in which no pointwise stabiliser of finitely many vertices fixes every vertex in
T
. The reason for this is that in Bass–Serre Theory, each vertex group in a graph of groups is an entire vertex stabiliser of the group action, and consequently the graph of groups contains both local (in our sense) and global information (since a stabiliser
G_{v}
includes information about how it acts on vertices at all distances from
v
).
This issue can easily be seen for example if one tries to use Bass–Serre theory to construct a nondiscrete group
G
of automorphisms of the
n\geq 3
regular tree
T_{n}
such that
G
has some specified local action
F\lneqq S_{n}
. To use Bass–Serre one needs to know
a priori
that such a group exists, since the input data in the graph of groups includes the vertex stabiliser of such an action. This limitation disappears when using a local-to-global approach.
Conversely, given a group acting on a tree, our approach gives a more parsimonious description of the local information, since it is not necessary to specify vertex stabilisers in full, only their action on the immediate neighbours of the vertex.

Property
(\mathrm{P})
admits a natural generalisation to
(\mathrm{P}_{k})
, introduced in
[
1
]
, where
(\mathrm{P}_{1})
is just
(\mathrm{P})
. Property
(\mathrm{P})
and its generalisation provide a general tool for understanding all actions on trees: one starts by defining something called the
(\mathrm{P}_{k})
-closure of any action on a tree for all
k\geq 1
, where a group
G\leq\mathrm{Aut}(T)
is
(\mathrm{P}_{1})
-closed (equivalently,
(\mathrm{P})
-closed) if and only if it is closed in the permutation topology on
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
and has Tits’ independence property
(\mathrm{P})
. These
(\mathrm{P}_{k})
-closures give rise to a series of approximations to the original action determined by how the original action behaves on balls of radius
k
; the
(\mathrm{P}_{k})
-closures then converge to the closure of the original action (in the permutation topology of
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
).

The goal of the present article is twofold: (i) to advance the local-to-global theory of groups acting on trees by developing a ‘local action’ complement to classical Bass–Serre theory, and (ii) to describe and classify all possible closed actions of groups on trees with property
(\mathrm{P})
, or equivalently, by describing all possible

(\mathrm{P})
-closures of actions on trees. In fact our ‘local action’ complement to classical Bass–Serre theory precisely gives this classification of all

(\mathrm{P})
-closures of actions on trees.
This classification is achieved using a graph decorated with groups representing ‘local actions’, and is called the
local action diagram
; it is analogous to a graph of groups in Bass–Serre theory.
Under this analogy, the universal groups in
[
2
]
play the role of HNN extensions, and the universal groups in
[
19
]
play the role of the amalgamated free products. As in
[
2
]
and
[
19
]
, we wish to describe the group in terms of its local actions; but in contrast to these papers, we make no assumptions about the homogeneity of the local actions or the structure of the orbits of the group on vertices or arcs of the tree. As a result, we need a slightly more complicated way to describe the local actions.

Definition 1.1
.

A
local action diagram
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(G(v)))
consists of the following information:

•

A connected graph
\Gamma
. (We define graphs in the sense of Serre, except that a loop may or may not be equal to its own reverse: see Section
2.1
.)

•

For each arc
a
of
\Gamma
, a nonempty set
X_{a}
(called the
colour set
of
a
).

•

For each vertex
v
of
\Gamma
, a group
G(v)
(called the
local action
at
v
) with the following properties: write
X_{v}
to denote the disjoint union
\bigsqcup_{a\in o^{-1}(v)}X_{a}
, then the group
G(v)
is a closed subgroup of
\mathrm{Sym}(X_{v})
and the sets
X_{a}
are the orbits of
G(v)
on
X_{v}
.

There is a natural notion of isomorphism of local action diagrams. For actions on trees the natural way to define isomorphism is conjugacy, where we say
(T,G)
and
(T^{\prime},G^{\prime})
are conjugate if there is a graph isomorphism
\theta:T\rightarrow T^{\prime}
that intertwines the two actions. For a local action diagram
\Delta
arising from
(T,G)
, we then define a group
\mathbf{U}(\Delta)
that is universal among those groups acting on
T
with associated local action diagram
\Delta
.

The central theorem of this article, which we prove in Section
3
using these universal groups, is as follows.
The theorem is a classification of groups with Tits’ independence property
(\mathrm{P})
, and gives rise to a complete description of closed groups with Tits’ independence property
(\mathrm{P})
.

Theorem 1.2
.

There is a natural one-to-one correspondence between conjugacy classes of
(\mathrm{P})
-closed actions on trees and isomorphism classes of local action diagrams.

As with the Fundamental Theorem of Bass–Serre Theory, the power of the correspondence lies not just in its existence, but in the richness of the relationship between the action on the tree and the associated diagram.
A more expansive summary of Section
3
, highlighting the similarities with Bass–Serre theory for those readers familiar with it, is the following. The various identifications and isomorphisms occurring in this summary are described precisely in the section itself.

1.

For an action
(T,G)
of a group
G
on a tree
T
, we have an associated local action diagram
\Delta
(akin to a graph of groups in Bass–Serre theory).

2.

The local action diagram admits a
\Delta
-tree,
\mathbf{T}
(akin to the universal cover of a graph of groups in Bass–Serre theory).

3.

The
\Delta
-tree
\mathbf{T}
admits a universal group
\mathbf{U}(\Delta)
which acts naturally on
\mathbf{T}
(akin to the fundamental group of a graph of groups in Bass–Serre theory).

4.

The action
(\mathbf{T},\mathbf{U}(\Delta))
is in fact an action of
\mathbf{U}(\Delta)
on the original tree
T
; this latter action is
(\mathrm{P})
-closed and has associated local action diagram
\Delta
. Moreover, the action
(\mathbf{T},\mathbf{U}(\Delta))
is isomorphic to the
(\mathrm{P})
-closure of
(T,G)
(akin to the Fundamental Theorem of Bass–Serre Theory,
[
16
, §5.4]
).

5.

In particular, we have that every closed action
(T,G)
with Tits’ independence property
(\mathrm{P})
is equal to
(\mathbf{T},\mathbf{U}(\Delta))
, where
\Delta
is the local action diagram of
(T,G)
; and moreover, every local action diagram
\Delta
gives rise to a closed action
(\mathbf{T},\mathbf{U}(\Delta))
that enjoys Tits’ independence property
(\mathrm{P})
.

In this way, we obtain a complete description of all closed actions of groups acting on trees with Tits’ independence property
(\mathrm{P})
: they are precisely the universal groups of local action diagrams. All groups acting on trees with Tits’ independence property
(\mathrm{P})
can then be classified by taking the closure of their action.

It should be emphasised that this description of closed actions
(T,G)
with Tits’ independence property
(\mathrm{P})
via local action diagrams is usable, in that one can construct novel examples by drawing new local action diagrams, or analyse existing examples by examining their local action diagrams. As we describe below, important global properties of the group can be read directly from the local action diagram.

There are no surprises in how a local action diagram is obtained from a (
(\mathrm{P})
-closed) action of a group
G
on a tree
T
. The graph
\Gamma
is the quotient graph
G\backslash T
; each local action
G(v)
represents the closure of the action of a vertex stabiliser
G_{v^{*}}
(where
v^{*}\in VT
lies in the preimage of
v
) on the arcs
o^{-1}(v^{*})
of
T
originating at
v^{*}
; those arcs are partitioned into
G_{v^{*}}
-orbits, represented by the colour sets, with the result that there is a natural one-to-one correspondence between
o^{-1}(v)
and
G_{v^{*}}
-orbits on
o^{-1}(v^{*})
.

The significance of the correspondence, then, is in the following two observations.

(1)

The local action diagram exactly describes the
(\mathrm{P})
-closure of the original action up to conjugacy. In particular, any ‘large-scale’ information about the original group can be recovered from the quotient graph
\Gamma
together with the local actions.

(2)

All possible local action diagrams arise in this manner. In particular,
\Gamma
can be any connected graph in our sense, and apart from how
\Gamma
limits the number of orbits of the local actions, there are no compatibility conditions on which local actions can be combined.

For comparison, the Burger–Mozes framework corresponds to the case when
\Gamma
is a single vertex with a set of loops, each of which is its own reverse; the framework of
[
19
]
corresponds to the case that
\Gamma
has two vertices and no loops.

As an example of what this means in practice, consider the class
\mathcal{C}(n,d)
of
(\mathrm{P})
-closed actions on trees
(T,G)
such that
G
has at most
n
orbits on vertices and no vertex has degree greater than
d
. Theorem
1.2
immediately shows that for given natural numbers
n
and
d
, there are only finitely many conjugacy classes of actions in
\mathcal{C}(n,d)
; but because of all the possible graphs and decorations, the number of conjugacy classes will grow quite rapidly with
n
and
d
. Even when
n=1
, there are more than just the Burger–Mozes groups: see Section
7.1
.

As noted previously, important global properties of the action
(T,G)
can be read directly from the local action diagram. To that end, the next part of the article is concerned with characterising various natural properties of interest for groups acting on trees in terms of the local action diagram, including various properties of topological groups.

Recall that Tits’ main theorem on property
(\mathrm{P})
, ensuring that the subgroup
G^{+}
generated by arc stabilisers is trivial or simple, only applies to
(\mathrm{P})
-closed actions that are geometrically dense, meaning that there is no proper invariant subtree or fixed end. Fixed ends and invariant subtrees can be recognised in the local action diagram, since they correspond to so-called scopos. This means that sufficient conditions for the simplicity of
G^{+}
can be read directly from the local action diagram for
G
.

Definition 1.3
.

Let
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(G(v)))
be a local action diagram. A
strongly confluent partial orientation
(henceforth,
scopo
) of
\Delta
is a subset
O
of
A\Gamma
such that:

(i)

If
a\in O
, then
\overline{a}\not\in O
and
|X_{a}|=1
;

(ii)

For all
v\in V\Gamma
, if
O
contains an arc
a
originating at
v
, then
O
contains all arcs other than
\overline{a}
that terminate at
v
.

Theorem 1.4

(See Section
5.1
)
.

Given a group
G
acting on a tree
T
, then the invariant subtrees and fixed ends of the action naturally correspond to scopos of the local action diagram, with the empty scopo corresponding to
T
itself.

In particular, the action
(T,G)
is geometrically dense if its local action diagram is
irreducible
, meaning that the only scopo is the empty one. The scopos corresponding to fixed ends, and the scopos corresponding to invariant subtrees, can be described precisely (see the proof of Theorem
1.4
in Section
5.1
).

Since scopos are quite special, it is easy to write down sufficient conditions for a local action diagram to give rise to an action on the tree that is geometrically dense. One of the obstacles to a geometrically dense action is when the local action diagram
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(G(v)))
has a stray leaf: a
leaf
of a graph is a vertex
v
with exactly one outgoing edge, such that the edge is not a loop, and a
stray leaf
of
\Delta
is a leaf
v
of
\Gamma
such that
|X_{v}|=1
(or equivalently,
v
is a leaf of
\Gamma
such that
G(v)=\{1\}
). The other kinds of obstacle, which will be defined in Section
5.2
, are
focal cycle
,
horocyclic end
, and
stray half-tree
. As we shall see in Proposition
5.10
, the local action diagram
\Delta
is irreducible if and only if it is not a focal cycle and has no horocyclic ends, no stray half-trees and no stray leaves. With these we obtain the following result, allowing one to read the simplicity of
G^{+}
directly from the local action diagram of
G
.

Corollary 1.5
.

Let
T
be a tree, and let
G\leq\mathrm{Aut}(T)
have
(\mathrm{P})
. Suppose
\Delta
is the local action diagram of
(T,G)
. Then
\Delta
is not a focal cycle and has no horocyclic ends, no stray half-trees and no stray leaves if and only if
G
is geometrically dense. In particular, if
\Delta
is not a focal cycle and has no horocyclic ends, no stray half-trees and no stray leaves then
G^{+}
is abstractly simple or trivial.

In particular, for the local action diagram
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(G(v)))
, if
\Gamma
is a finite graph that is not a cycle graph (where a
cycle graph
is a finite connected graph in which all vertices have degree
2
), then one sees from the definitions that
\Delta
is not a focal cycle and has no horocyclic ends or stray half-trees, so irreducibility is equivalent to the absence of stray leaves.

Corollary 1.6
.

Let
T
be a tree, and let
G\leq\mathrm{Aut}(T)
have
(\mathrm{P})
. Suppose
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(G(v)))
is the local action diagram of
(T,G)
, such that
\Gamma
is finite and not a cycle graph. Then
G
is geometrically dense if and only if for every leaf
v
of
\Gamma
we have
|X_{v}|>1
. In particular, if
\Delta
has no stray leaves then
G^{+}
is abstractly simple or trivial.

Note that the details of the local actions are not important here, only the structure of the quotient graph
\Gamma
and the sizes of the colour sets. A trivial amount of ‘local’ information is enough to determine whether or not
G^{+}
is trivial:
G^{+}
being trivial is equivalent (see Lemma
5.11
) to all of the local actions in
\Delta
being free (i.e. semi-regular).
In the case that
(T,G)
is not geometrically dense, we can also describe which of the degenerate cases of actions on trees (if any) it falls into using the local action diagram (see Section
5.2
).

The next theorem is almost an exact characterisation of simplicity for groups with faithful
(\mathrm{P})
-closed actions, with two minor caveats: one is that we need to exclude a couple of cases that are degenerate from the perspective of local-to-global structure, and the other is that we need to ensure that the group has closed action on any invariant subtree.

Definition 1.7
.

Say that
G\leq\mathrm{Aut}(T)
is
strongly closed
if for every
G
-invariant subtree
T^{\prime}
of
T
, the action of
G
on
T^{\prime}
is closed.

In particular, every closed geometrically dense action is strongly closed. For our purposes, the requirement of being strongly closed is easily achieved: as we will see in
Corollary
6.4
, a locally compact
(\mathrm{P})
-closed subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
that acts with translation is always strongly closed.

Theorem 1.8

(See Section
5.2
)
.

Let
(T,G)
be a faithful
(\mathrm{P})
-closed and strongly closed action on a tree
T
. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

G
is a simple group,
G
acts with translation, and there is no finite set of vertices whose pointwise stabiliser is trivial.

(ii)

There is an invariant subtree
T^{\prime}
(possibly equal to
T
) which is infinite and on which
G
acts faithfully. Moreover, letting
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(G(v)))
be the local action diagram of
(T^{\prime},G)
, then
\Delta
is irreducible;
\Gamma
is a tree; and each of the groups
G(v)
is closed and generated by point stabilisers, with
G(v)\neq\{1\}
for some
v\in V\Gamma
.

Furthermore, in (
ii
) the action
(T^{\prime},G)
is
(T^{\prime},\mathbf{U}(\Delta))
.

Note that the condition that there is no finite set of vertices whose pointwise stabiliser is trivial is equivalent to saying that
G
is nondiscrete in the permutation topology on
T
.

Next, we describe some topological properties of
(\mathrm{P})
-closed subgroups of
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
with the permutation topology; these are already well-understood in the locally finite case, but in the present context we are making no assumptions about the degree of
T
. We highlight the following special case.

Theorem 1.9

(See Section
6.3
)
.

Let
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(G(v)))
be a local action diagram. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

\mathbf{U}(\Delta)
is compactly generated, locally compact and has geometrically dense action on its associated tree;

(ii)

\Delta
is irreducible;
\Gamma
is finite; and each of the groups
G(v)
is compactly generated and subdegree-finite.

Moreover, if (i) and (ii) hold, then
\mathbf{U}(\Delta)
is Polish, acting on a countable tree, and all arc stabilisers of
\mathbf{U}(\Delta)
are compact.

Combining Corollary
1.6
, Theorem
1.8
and Theorem
1.9
gives the following corollary, where
\mathscr{S}
denotes the class of nondiscrete compactly generated, topologically simple t.d.l.c. groups. This corollary allows us to construct local action diagrams that yield groups in
\mathscr{S}
. Note that the conditions in part (ii) of the corollary also imply that the local action diagram
\Delta
is irreducible.

Corollary 1.10
.

Let
(T,G)
be a faithful
(\mathrm{P})
-closed and strongly closed action on a tree
T
. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

We have
G\in\mathscr{S}
and the action does not fix any vertex of
T
.

(ii)

There is a unique smallest invariant subtree
T^{\prime}
(possibly equal to
T
) on which
G
acts faithfully. Moreover, letting
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(G(v)))
be the local action diagram of
(T^{\prime},G)
, then
\Gamma
is a finite tree, and each of the groups
G(v)
is closed, compactly generated, subdegree-finite and generated by point stabilisers, with
G(v)\neq\{1\}
for every leaf
v
of
\Gamma
.

Furthermore, in (
ii
) the action
(T^{\prime},G)
is
(T^{\prime},\mathbf{U}(\Delta))
.

We also consider the structure of open subgroups. The following should be compared with a theorem of Caprace–De Medts,
[
4
, Theorem A and Theorem 3.9]
. (Note that in
[
4
]
, unlike in the present article, it is assumed that the tree is locally finite.)
The following includes as a special case
2^{\aleph_{0}}
nonisomorphic groups in
\mathscr{S}
that can be constructed following
[
19
, Remark 40]
.

Theorem 1.11

(See Section
4.6
)
.

Let
T
be a tree such that every vertex of
T
has at least three neighbours, and let
G
be a nondiscrete
(\mathrm{P})
-closed subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
, such that
G
does not preserve any proper subtree of
T
.
Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

Every proper open subgroup of
G
has bounded action on
T
and point stabilisers in G are pairwise incomparable.

(ii)

There are adjacent vertices
v,w
of
T
such that
G_{v}
and
G_{w}
are distinct maximal subgroups of
G
.

(iii)

G
preserves the natural bipartition of the vertices of
T
and acts primitively on each part.

(iv)

Letting
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(G(v)))
be the local action diagram of
(T,G)
, then
|V\Gamma|=2
and for all
v\in V\Gamma
,
G(v)
is primitive but not regular.

Moreover, if (i)–(iv) hold then
G
is simple;
\Gamma
consists of a single undirected edge with two distinct endpoints; the action of
G
on
T
is geometrically dense; and
G
is an amalgamated free product
G_{v}\ast_{G_{(v,w)}}G_{w}
.

Tits’ main theorem from
[
20
]
imposes a restriction on the normal subgroups of a
(\mathrm{P})
-closed group. In the case of compact arc stabilisers, we find analogous restrictions on the closed subgroups that can be constructed from open subgroups.

Definition 1.12
.

Let
G
be a topological group and let
H
be a closed subgroup of
G
. Then
H
is a
RIO subgroup
if
H
is a directed union of a family of subgroups
\mathcal{O}
, such that each
O\in\mathcal{O}
is an open subgroup of
H
and an intersection of open subgroups of
G
.

When
G
is a totally disconnected locally compact group, the class of RIO subgroups has several closure properties: for example, every closed subnormal subgroup is RIO, an intersection of RIO subgroups is RIO, and if
G
acts distally (for example, by isometries) on a Hausdorff topological space
X
such that the map
g\mapsto gx
is continuous for
x\in X
, then
G_{x}
is a RIO subgroup of
G
. See
[
15
]
for more information.

Theorem 1.13

(See Section
4.5
)
.

Let
T
be a tree and let
G
be a
(\mathrm{P})
-closed subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
with compact arc stabilisers. Let
H
be a RIO subgroup of
G
. Then exactly one of the following holds.

(i)

Every compactly generated closed subgroup of
H
is compact.

(ii)

H
has a noncompact, compactly generated closed subgroup that fixes exactly one vertex
v
and
|H:H_{v}|\leq 2
.

(iii)

H
acts with translation and contains an arc stabiliser of
G
. In particular,
H
is open in
G
and
(\mathrm{P})
-closed.

Section
7
is devoted to examples. We show how the local action diagram can be used for classifying group actions on trees by classifying all 70 of the
(\mathrm{P})
-closed vertex-transitive actions on trees of degree
0\leq d\leq 5
, and give a GAP (
[
8
]
) implementation due to S. Tornier that can be used to classify vertex-transitive actions of larger degrees.

Finally, we give an example of how Corollary
1.10
can be used to produce more groups in
\mathscr{S}
, which demonstrates that within
\mathscr{S}
, the groups
\mathbf{U}(\Delta)
have a certain universality property.

Theorem 1.14

(See Section
7.3
)
.

Let
n
be a positive integer and let
G_{1},\dots,G_{n}
be nontrivial compactly generated t.d.l.c. groups, such that for each
G_{i}
there is a compact open subgroup
U_{i}
such that
G_{i}=\langle gU_{i}g^{-1}\mid g\in G_{i}\rangle
and
\bigcap_{g\in G_{i}}gU_{i}g^{-1}=\{1\}
. For example, we can take
G_{i}\in\mathscr{S}
and
U_{i}
to be any compact open subgroup. Then there exists
\mathbf{U}(\Delta)\in\mathscr{S}
acting continuously on a countable tree
T
, vertex stabilisers
O_{1},\dots,O_{n}
of
\mathbf{U}(\Delta)
and compact normal subgroups
K_{i}
of
O_{i}
, such that
O_{i}\cong K_{i}\rtimes G_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n
.

We conclude the paper with a selection of open problems.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Graphs

A
graph

\Gamma=(V,A,o,r)
consists of a vertex set
V=V\Gamma
, a set
A=A\Gamma
of arcs, a map
o:A\rightarrow V
assigning to each arc an
origin
(or
initial
)
vertex
, and a bijection
r:A\rightarrow A
, denoted
a\mapsto\overline{a}
and called
edge reversal
(or sometimes
edge inversion
), such that
r^{2}=\mathrm{id}
. The
terminal vertex
of an edge is
t(a):=o(\overline{a})
. A
loop
is an arc
a
such that
o(a)=t(a)
. If
a
is a loop, it is important that we allow both
\overline{a}=a
and
\overline{a}\neq a
as possibilities. A
leaf
is a vertex
v
such that
|o^{-1}(v)|=1
and the arc in
o^{-1}(v)
is not a loop.
We call the pair
\{a,\overline{a}\}
an
edge
between the vertices
o(a)
and
t(a)
. Two vertices are
adjacent
if there is an edge between them.

A
subgraph

\Gamma^{\prime}=(V^{\prime},A^{\prime},o^{\prime},r^{\prime})
of
\Gamma
is a graph such that
V^{\prime}\subseteq V
and
A^{\prime}\subseteq A
with
o^{\prime}:A^{\prime}\rightarrow V^{\prime}
(resp.
r^{\prime}:A^{\prime}\rightarrow A^{\prime}
) equal to the restriction of
o
to
A^{\prime}
(resp.
r^{\prime}
to
A^{\prime}
). For a subset
V^{\prime}\subseteq V
the
subgraph of
\Gamma
induced by
V^{\prime}
is the subgraph of
\Gamma
with vertex set
V^{\prime}
and arc set
\{a\in A\Gamma:o(a),t(a)\in V^{\prime}\}
.

Since graphs in this paper are not simple, the graph subtraction operation is not well behaved and so we avoid it. Instead, we define for subsets
V^{\prime}\subseteq V\Gamma
and
A^{\prime}\subseteq A\Gamma
the following graphs:
\Gamma\smallsetminus V^{\prime}
is the subgraph of
\Gamma
induced by
V\Gamma\smallsetminus V^{\prime}
, and
\Gamma\smallsetminus A^{\prime}
is the subgraph of
\Gamma
with vertex set
V\Gamma
and arc set
A\Gamma\smallsetminus(A^{\prime}\cup\overline{A^{\prime}})
.

For an interval
I\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
, let
\hat{I}=\{i\in I:i+1\in I\}
. A
path
indexed by an interval
I\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
in
\Gamma
is then a sequence of vertices
(v_{i})_{i\in I}
and edges
(\{a_{i},\overline{a_{i}}\})_{i\in\hat{I}}
such that
\{a_{i},\overline{a_{i}}\}
is an edge in
\Gamma
between
v_{i}
and
v_{i+1}
for all
i\in\hat{I}
. Where there is no ambiguity, we will sometimes specify a path simply by giving the sequence of vertices or of arcs. If
I
is finite, say
I=\{0,\dots,n\}
, we say this is a path from
v_{0}
to
v_{n}
(or between
v_{0}
and
v_{n}
) and the
length
of the path is
n
(that is, the length of the sequence of arcs). We allow paths of length
0
, that is, a single vertex with no arcs is a path.
The path
backtracks
if there is some
i\in I
such that
a_{i+1}=\overline{a_{i}}
, in other words the same undirected edge is used twice in a row. The path is
simple
if all vertices are distinct; we will sometimes regard a simple path as a subgraph of
\Gamma
. If
n>0
and
I=\{0,\dots,n\}
,
v_{0}=v_{n}
and all vertices
v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{n-1}
are distinct, then the path is called a
cycle
of length
n
. Note that a single vertex
v
together with a loop at
v
together form a cycle. The same definitions apply to
directed
paths, except that we only include the arcs
a_{i}
where
o(a_{i})=v_{i}
and
t(a_{i})=v_{i+1}
for
i\in\hat{I}
. If there is a path between two vertices
v
,
w
, then there is a (directed) path of minimal length, called a
shortest (directed) path
from
v
to
w
, and the length of this shortest path is the
distance
between
v
and
w
. If there is no path then the distance is taken to be infinite.

An
orientation
of a graph
\Gamma
is a subset
O
of
A\Gamma
such that for all
a\in A\Gamma
, either
a
or
\overline{a}
is in
O
, but not both. In particular, an
orientable graph
is a graph in the sense of Serre, in other words, there are no edges such that
a=\overline{a}
. More generally, a
partial orientation
is any (possibly empty) subset
O
of
A\Gamma
such that given
a\in A\Gamma
,
O
does not contain both of
a
and
\overline{a}
, but it could contain neither of them. Given a group
G
of automorphisms of
\Gamma
, we say the (partial) orientation is
G
-invariant
if
gO=O
for all
g\in G
.

The cardinality of
\Gamma
is defined to be that of the set
V\Gamma\sqcup A\Gamma
.
The
degree
of a vertex
v\in V
is
\deg(v):=|o^{-1}(v)|
, and the graph is
locally finite
if every vertex has finite degree. The
degree
of the graph is defined to be

\deg(\Gamma):=\sup_{v\in V\Gamma}\deg(v).

The graph
\Gamma
is
simple
if the map
A\rightarrow V\times V
by
a\mapsto(o(a),t(a))
is injective and no arc is a loop. In this case, the arc
a
is sometimes identified with the pair
(o(a),t(a))
. The graph is
connected
if there is a path between any two distinct vertices. In a simple graph
\Gamma
a
ray
is a one-way infinite simple path. The
ends
of
\Gamma
are equivalence classes
1
1

1

There are competing inequivalent definitions of an end that are all equivalent in the locally finite case. The definition we give here gives rise to so-called
vertex-ends
in the non-locally-finite case; other types of ends are edge-ends and metric ends. See
[
11
]
for more details.

of rays, in which two rays
R_{1},R_{2}
lie in the same end if and only if there is another ray
R
in
\Gamma
that contains infinitely many vertices of
R_{1}
and infinitely many vertices of
R_{2}
.
A
tree
is a nonempty simple, connected graph that contains no cycles. In a tree, there is a unique shortest path between any two vertices
v
and
w
, which we denote
[v,w]
.

A
graph homomorphism

\theta:\Gamma\rightarrow\Gamma^{\prime}
is a pair of maps
\theta_{V}:V\Gamma\rightarrow V\Gamma^{\prime}
and
\theta_{A}:A\Gamma\rightarrow A\Gamma^{\prime}
that respect origin vertices and edge reversal:
\theta_{V}(o(a))=o(\theta_{V}(a))
and
\overline{\theta_{A}(a)}=\theta_{A}(\overline{a})
. We say
\theta
is an
isomorphism
if in addition,
\theta_{V}
and
\theta_{A}
are both bijections, and an
automorphism
if it is an isomorphism such that
\Gamma=\Gamma^{\prime}
. The automorphisms of
\Gamma
form a group, denoted
\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma)
. When
\Gamma
is a simple graph, the automorphisms of
\Gamma
act faithfully as the group of permutations of
V
that respect the edge relation in
V\times V
. In this case we identify
\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma)
with the corresponding subgroup of
\mathrm{Sym}(V)
.

For
G
a group acting on a graph
\Gamma
and a vertex, arc or edge
e
of
G
, the orbit of
e
under
G
is denoted
Ge
. The action of
G
gives a
quotient graph

G\backslash\Gamma
as follows: the vertex set
V_{G}
is the set of
G
-orbits on
V
and the arc set
A_{G}
is the set of
G
-orbits on
A
. The origin map
\tilde{o}:A_{G}\rightarrow A_{G}
is defined by
\tilde{o}(Ga):=Go(a)
; this is well-defined since graph automorphisms send origin vertices to origin vertices. The reversal
\tilde{r}:A_{G}\rightarrow A_{G}
is given by
Ga\mapsto G\overline{a}
; this map is also well-defined. We will abuse notation and write
o
and
r
for
\tilde{o}
and
\tilde{r}
. We denote the quotient map of the action
(\Gamma,G)
by
\pi_{(\Gamma,G)}
.

A subset of the vertices of a connected graph is
bounded
, respectively
unbounded
, if it has finite, respectively infinite diameter in the graph metric. Given a vertex
v
of a graph
\Gamma
, write
B_{n}(v)
(the
ball of radius
n
) for the induced subgraph formed by all vertices
w
such that
d_{\Gamma}(v,w)\leq n
, and
S_{n}(v)
(the
sphere of radius
n
) for the set of vertices
w
such that
d_{\Gamma}(v,w)=n
.

A graph homomorphism
\theta:\Gamma\rightarrow\Gamma^{\prime}
is
surjective
if it is surjective on both vertices and edges, and is
locally surjective
if for each
v\in V\Gamma
, we have
o^{-1}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(\theta(v))=\theta(o^{-1}_{\Gamma}(v))
. Note that for a locally surjective map
\theta:\Gamma\rightarrow\Gamma^{\prime}
, if
\Gamma
is nonempty and
\Gamma^{\prime}
is connected (as will typically be the case throughout this paper), then
\theta
is surjective; indeed, for all edges
e
of
\Gamma^{\prime}
, if
o(e)
or
t(e)
is in
\theta(\Gamma)
, then
e
is also in
\theta(\Gamma)
.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
\Gamma
be a graph and let
G\leq\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma)
. Then
\pi_{(\Gamma,G)}
is locally surjective.

Proof.

Let
\pi=\pi_{(\Gamma,G)}
and let
\Gamma^{\prime}=\pi(\Gamma)
.
Let
v\in V\Gamma
and
a^{\prime}\in o^{-1}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(\pi(v))
. Since
\pi
is surjective, there exists
a\in A\Gamma
such that
\pi(a)=a^{\prime}
, and hence

\pi(o(a))=o(\pi(a))=o(a^{\prime})=\pi(v);

there is then
g\in G
such that
go(a)=v
, and hence
ga\in o^{-1}(v)
.

Thus
\pi(ga)=\pi(a)=a^{\prime}
; in particular,
a^{\prime}\in\pi(o^{-1}_{\Gamma}(v))
. Given the choice of
v
and
a
, we conclude that
\pi
is locally surjective.
∎

2.2
Types of action on a tree

If
T
is a tree, a
line
in
T
is a two-way infinite simple path. A
translation
of a line
L
is an orientation-preserving automorphism of
L
that does not fix any point on the line.

We recall that automorphisms of
T
come in three types.

Proposition 2.2

(
[
20
, Proposition 3.2]
)
.

Let
T
be a tree and let
g\in\mathrm{Aut}(T)
. Then exactly one of the following holds:

1.

g
fixes a vertex;

2.

g
inverts an edge;

3.

there is a unique line
L
in
T
(called the
axis
of
g
) on which
g
induces a translation.

If
g
inverts an edge, we call it an
inversion
of
T
, and if
g
translates a line we call it a
translation
of
T
(or say it is
hyperbolic
on
T
). Elements that are not translations are called
elliptic
. Notice that an inversion fixes no ends of the tree, and a translation fixes exactly two ends (namely, the ends of its axis).

The
natural bipartition
or
vertex parity
of
VT
is the partition of vertices of
T
into two classes
V_{0}T
and
V_{1}T
, such that two vertices are in the same class if and only if the distance between them is even. We say an automorphism or group of automorphisms is
parity-preserving
if it preserves each part of the parity and
parity-reversing
otherwise. In general, given
G\leq\mathrm{Aut}(T)
, the parity-preserving automorphisms form a normal subgroup of
G
of index at most
2
, called the
parity-preserving subgroup
of
G
. Note that automorphisms that fix a vertex are parity-preserving, whereas inversions are parity-reversing. A translation is parity-preserving if it translates vertices along its axis by an even distance and parity-reversing otherwise.

Given
a\in AT
, we define the associated
half-tree
to be the subgraph
T_{a}
induced on the vertices
v
such that
d(t(a),v)<d(o(a),v)
. Note that
VT=VT_{a}\sqcup VT_{\overline{a}}
and every end of
T
is an end of exactly one of
T_{a}
and
T_{\overline{a}}
, however
a
and
\overline{a}
do not lie in
T_{a}
nor do they lie in
T_{\overline{a}}
.

A group
G
acting on a tree
T
is said to act
without inversion
if there is no pair
a\in AT
and
g\in\mathrm{Aut}(T)
such that
ga=\overline{a}
. The group
G
acts
without translation
if no element of
G
is a translation; otherwise we say
G
acts
with translation
or
G

translates

T
. An action on a tree is
geometrically dense
if it does not preserve any end or proper subtree of
T
. Notice that for
G
acting on
T
, the set of leaves
L
of
T
is
G
-invariant, and
T\smallsetminus L
is connected and therefore a
G
-invariant subtree. Thus if the action of
G
is geometrically dense, then either
T
is leafless or
T
consists of two vertices connected by a single edge, and
G
contains element inverting that edge.

Geometrically dense actions are characterised by the action on the set of half-trees ordered by inclusion. The following is similar in spirit to many arguments concerning groups acting on trees; we can find no reference to this precise formulation so include a proof for completeness.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
T
be a tree and let
G\leq\mathrm{Aut}(T)
. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

G
is geometrically dense;

(ii)

For all
a\in AT
, there exists
g\in G
such that
T_{ga}\subseteq T_{\overline{a}}
;

(iii)

For all
a,b\in AT
, there exists
g\in G
such that
T_{gb}\subseteq T_{a}
.

Proof.

If
T
has no edges then all three statements are vacuously true. If
T
has only one edge, then (i)–(iii) are true if
G
flips the edge and false otherwise. If
T
has more than one edge, but has a leaf, it is easy to see that (i)–(iii) are false. Thus we may assume
|o^{-1}(v)|\geq 2
for every
v\in VT
. Given
a,b\in AT
, note that
T_{b}\subseteq T_{a}
if and only if
T_{b}
is disjoint from
T_{\overline{a}}
.

Suppose (i) holds and let
a\in AT
; we suppose for a contradiction that
T_{\overline{a}}
does not contain any
G
-translate of
T_{a}
. Then for all
g,h\in G
, the half-trees
T_{ga}
and
T_{ha}
intersect. Let
B_{a}
be the set of half-trees
T_{b}
such that
b\in Ga
and
T_{b}\subseteq T_{a}
. Given
T_{b_{1}},T_{b_{2}}\in B_{a}
, since these half-trees are not disjoint and both are contained in
T_{a}
, we see that one of
T_{b_{1}}
and
T_{b_{2}}
contains the other; that is,
B_{a}
is totally ordered by inclusion. Moreover, we see that
B_{a}
has no infinite ascending chain, by considering the length of the path between
t(a)
and
o(b)
for
T_{b}\in B_{a}
. If
B_{a}=\{T_{a}\}
, then we see that
t(a)
is contained in
T_{ga}
for every
g\in G
; in that case, the intersection
\bigcap_{g\in G}T_{ga}
is a proper nonempty
G
-invariant subtree, contradicting (i). Thus there is some
s(a)\in Ga
such that
T_{s(a)}
is maximal among elements of
B_{a}\smallsetminus\{T_{a}\}
; since
B_{a}
is totally ordered, in fact the arc
s(a)
is unique. We can then form a sequence
a_{i}
of arcs with
a_{0}=a
and
a_{i+1}=s(a)
, so that the trees
T_{a_{i}}
form a descending chain; there is then a unique end
\xi
representable by a directed ray that contains
a_{i}
for all
i\geq 0
. Since
G
is geometrically dense, it does not fix this end, so
g\xi\neq\xi
for some
g\in G
. We then see that for some
i,j\geq 0
, the half-trees
T_{a_{i}}
and
T_{ga_{j}}
are disjoint, a contradiction. Thus in fact
T_{\overline{a}}
does contain a
G
-translate of
T_{a}
, proving (ii).

Suppose (ii) holds and let
a,b\in AT
. By considering the relative orientation of
a
and
b
on a path containing both, we see that
T_{a^{\prime}}
contains
T_{b^{\prime}}
for some
a^{\prime}\in\{a,\overline{a}\}
and
b^{\prime}\in\{b,\overline{b}\}
. Then by (ii),
T_{a}
contains a
G
-translate of
T_{a^{\prime}}
, while
T_{b^{\prime}}
contains a
G
-translate of
T_{b}
. Thus
T_{a}
contains a
G
-translate of
T_{b}
and (iii) follows.

Suppose (iii) holds. Then clearly no
G
-orbit on vertices is confined to a half-tree; since any proper subtree of
T
is contained in a half-tree, it follows that
G
does not preserve any proper subtree. Given an end
\xi
, then there is an arc
a
such that
\xi
is an end of
T_{a}
and not an end of
T_{\overline{a}}
. There is then
g\in G
such that
T_{ga}\subseteq T_{\overline{a}}
, and so
g\xi\neq\xi
. Thus
G
does not fix an end, and (i) follows, completing the proof that (i)–(iii) are equivalent.
∎

Groups acting without translation have the following fixed point property.

Lemma 2.4

(See
[
20
, Proposition 3.4]
)
.

Let
T
be a tree and let
G\leq\mathrm{Aut}(T)
. Suppose
G
acts without translation. Then
G
fixes a vertex, preserves an undirected edge, or fixes an end.

For the purposes of studying groups acting on trees, it is useful to split the actions into six cases, as follows.

Theorem 2.5

(See
[
16
]
)
.

Let
G
be a group acting on a tree
T
. Then exactly one of the following holds:

(Fixed vertex)

G
fixes some vertex (not necessarily unique);

(Inversion)

G
preserves a unique undirected edge and includes an inversion of that edge;

(Lineal)

G
fixes exactly two ends and translates the line between them;

(Horocyclic)

G
fixes a unique end, does not fix any vertices, and acts without translation;

(Focal)

G
fixes a unique end and includes a translation towards this end;

(General type)

G
acts with translation and does not fix any end.

Proof.

We may suppose that
G
does not fix any vertex. Suppose
Gv
has finite diameter for some
v\in VT
. Then there is a smallest subtree
T^{\prime}
containing
Gv
, and
T^{\prime}
has finite diameter. In particular,
G
preserves a subtree
T^{\prime}
of finite diameter. By repeatedly pruning the leaves of
T^{\prime}
, we arrive at a
G
-invariant subtree
T^{\prime\prime}
in which all vertices are leaves. Since
G
does not fix any vertex,
T^{\prime\prime}
must be a single undirected edge, and
G
includes a reversal of that edge. Thus
G
is of inversion type.

From now on we may suppose that
G
has unbounded action (that is, all orbits of vertices under
G
are of unbounded diameter). Suppose that
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
are distinct ends of
T
fixed by
G
. Then there is a unique line
L
with ends
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
, which is therefore
G
-invariant. Since
G
has unbounded action, it must translate
L
; it then follows that
G
cannot fix any more ends. Thus
G
has lineal action.

From now on we may suppose that
G
fixes at most one end. Suppose that
G
fixes an end
\xi
. If
G
acts without translation, then it is horocyclic, whereas if
G
acts with translation, then it is focal.

In the remaining case,
G
is unbounded and fixes no ends. Then
G
acts with translation by Lemma
2.4
.
∎

From a geometric perspective, the fixed vertex and inversion cases can be grouped together as ‘bounded’ (in other words, every orbit has finite diameter), but from the perspective of local actions it is useful to distinguish them. Note that compact subgroups of
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
have finite orbits, so they are bounded; the converse is true if
T
is locally finite, but not otherwise.

If
G
has a horocyclic action then every element in
G
must be elliptic, and no element can be an inversion (because inversions fix no end); thus every element must fix a vertex.
A corollary to our next lemma shows that such a group
G
cannot be compactly generated.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
T
be a tree and let
G
be a compactly generated locally compact group acting continuously on
T
, such that every element of
G
fixes a vertex of
T
. Then
G
fixes a vertex of
T
.

Proof.

Note that the hypotheses imply that
G
acts without inversion. In the case that
G
is finitely generated, the conclusion is
[
16
, I.6.5, Corollary 3]
.

It is a general fact (see
[
13
, Lemma 2]
, for example) that given any compactly generated t.d.l.c. group
G
and compact open subgroup
U
of
G
, there is a finitely generated subgroup
H
of
G
such that
G=UH
. Since
G
acts continuously on
T
, the action of
U
has finite orbits. Now
H
fixes a vertex
v
of
T
, which means that the orbit of
v
under
G=UH
is finite. Let
S
be the smallest subtree of
T
spanned by
Gv
. Then
S
is a finite tree, so it has a canonical centre, which is either a vertex or a pair of adjacent vertices. Thus
G
either fixes a vertex or preserves a pair of adjacent vertices. Since
G
acts without inversion, in fact
G
fixes a vertex.
∎

Corollary 2.7
.

Let
T
be a tree and let
G
be a compactly generated locally compact group acting continuously on
T
. Then the action is not horocyclic. Consequently, either the action of
G
is bounded, or
G
acts with translation.

Lineal and focal actions imply a special group structure. To explain why, it is useful to borrow from CAT(0) geometry an idea introduced by H. Busemann (see
[
3
]
). The interpretation of this idea for trees is easy to understand combinatorially. The next two lemmas are well-known, but we give proofs here for clarity.

Definition 2.8
.

Let
T
be a tree and let
\xi
be an end of
T
. A
(combinatorial) Busemann function with focus
\xi
is a function
b:VT\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
such that for all arcs
a
pointing towards
\xi
(that is, such that
\xi
is an end of
T_{a}
), we have
b(o(a))-b(t(a))=1
. A
horosphere
, respectively
horoball centred at
\xi
is a set of the form
\{v\in VT\mid b(v)=n\}
, respectively
\{v\in VT\mid b(v)\leq n\}
, for some Busemann function
b
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
T
be a tree. Choose some vertex
v_{0}\in VT
and an end
\xi
of
T
.

(i)

For each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
there is exactly one Busemann function
b_{n}
with focus
\xi
such that
b_{n}(v_{0})=n
. Moreover,
b_{n}(v)=b_{0}(v)+n
for all vertices
v
.

(ii)

Let
b
be a Busemann function with focus
\xi
. Then for all
g\in\mathrm{Aut}(T)
the function
g.b
is a Busemann function with focus
g.\xi
, where
g.b(v):=b(g^{-1}v)
. Consequently, given a group
G\leq\mathrm{Aut}(T)
such that
G
fixes
\xi
, the function

\beta_{\xi}:G\rightarrow\mathbb{Z};\;\beta_{\xi}(g)=g.b(v)-b(v)

is a homomorphism that does not depend on the choices of
b
or
v
.

(iii)

Let
G
be a group of automorphisms of
T
fixing
\xi
, let
\beta_{\xi}
be as in (ii) and let
g\in G
. If
\beta_{\xi}(g)>0
, then
g
is a translation with attracting end
\xi
; if
\beta_{\xi}(g)<0
, then
g
is a translation with repelling end
\xi
; and if
\beta_{\xi}(g)=0
, then
g
fixes pointwise a ray representing
\xi
.

Proof.

Fix a ray
(v_{0},v_{1},\dots)
of vertices representing
\xi
.

(i)
We set

b_{0}(v)=\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}d(v,v_{t})-t

where
d
is the usual graph metric on
VT
. It is easy to see that
b_{0}(v_{t})=-t
for all
t\geq 0
. For a general vertex
v
, we see that for all but finitely many
t\geq 0
and all
t^{\prime}>t
, the path from
v
to
v_{t^{\prime}}
passes through
v_{t}
; we then see that

d(v,v_{t^{\prime}})-t^{\prime}=d(v,v_{t})-t,

and hence for any such choice of
t
, we have
b_{0}(v)=d(v,v_{t})-t
. Thus
b_{0}
is a well-defined integer-valued function. Given an arc
a
terminating at
v
such that
\xi
is an end of
T_{a}
, then the path from
o(a)
to
v_{t^{\prime}}
passes through
v
and
v_{t}
for all sufficiently large
t
and
t^{\prime}>t
; hence, for
t
sufficiently large we have

b_{0}(o(a))=d(o(a),v_{t})-t=1+d(v,v_{t})-t=b_{0}(t(a))+1.

Thus
b_{0}
is a Busemann function with
b_{0}(v_{0})=0
. Clearly also
b_{n}(v):=b_{0}(v)+n
defines a Busemann function such that
b_{n}(v_{0})=n
, for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. To see that
b_{n}
is unique for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, note that any Busemann function
b
is uniquely specified by its value at a given vertex: once we know
b(v)
for some vertex
v
, then for
a\in o^{-1}(v)
, we have
b(t(a))=b(v)-1
if
a
points towards
\xi
and
b(t(a))=b(v)+1
otherwise, so we also know
b(w)
for every neighbour
w
of
v
, and hence by connectedness the value is specified at every vertex.

(ii)
Let
g\in\mathrm{Aut}(T)
. If
a\in AT
points towards
g.\xi
, then
g^{-1}a
points towards
\xi
, so

g.b(o(a))-g.b(t(a))=b(g^{-1}o(a))-b(g^{-1}t(a))=b(o(g^{-1}a))-b(t(g^{-1}a))=1.

Hence
g.b
is a Busemann function with focus
g.\xi
.

Now suppose that
G\leq\mathrm{Aut}(T)
fixes
\xi
and let
g\in G
. By (i), we have
b=b_{n}
and
g.b=b_{m}
for some
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}
; thus
g.b(v)-b(v)=m-n
, independent of the choice of
v
. Clearly adding a constant to
b
commutes with the action of
g
on the set of Busemann functions, so
g.b(v)-b(v)
is also independent of the choice of
b
. To see that
\beta:=\beta_{\xi}
is a homomorphism, we see that

\beta(gh)=gh.b(v)-b(v)=g.(h.b)(v)-(h.b)(v)+h.b(v)-b(v)=\beta(g)+\beta(h),

using the fact that
h.b
is also a Busemann function.

(iii)
Let
G\leq\mathrm{Aut}(T)
fix
\xi
and let
g\in G
. Then
(g^{-1}.v_{0},g^{-1}.v_{1},\dots)
is a ray representing
\xi
, so there is some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
such that for all but finitely many
t\geq 0
, we have
g^{-1}.v_{t}=v_{t-n}
. We then have

\beta(g)=b(v_{t-n})-b(v_{t})=n.

Moreover, we see that
\xi
is an attracting end of
g
if and only if
n>0
and a repelling end if
n<0
. If
n=0
, then
g
fixes pointwise the ray
(v_{t},v_{t+1},\dots)
for
t
sufficiently large.
∎

We now see why, in the taxonomy of Theorem
2.5
, the name
horocyclic
is used. Suppose
(G,T)
is horocyclic with fixed end
\xi
, and for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and some Busemann function
b
with focus
\xi
, let
H=\{v\in VT\mid b(v)=n\}
be a horosphere. By Lemma
2.9
(
iii
), we have
\beta_{\xi}(g)=0
for any
g\in G
. Therefore
gH=\{gv\in VT\mid b(v)=n\}=\{w\in VT\mid g.b(w)=n\}=H
. Thus for a horocyclic group
G
, each Busemann function, each horoball and each horocycle is invariant under
G
.

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
T
be a tree and let
G\leq\mathrm{Aut}(T)
, equipped with the subspace topology in
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
.

(i)

If
G
is lineal or focal, then
G
decomposes as a semidirect product
E\rtimes\langle s\rangle
, where
E
is an open subgroup consisting of all elliptic elements of
G
and
s
is a translation. If
G
is lineal then
E
fixes pointwise the axis of
\xi
, whereas if
G
is focal then
E
is horocyclic.

(ii)

Suppose
G
is topologically perfect (i.e., the commutator
[G,G]
subgroup is dense in
G
). Then
G
is of fixed vertex, horocyclic or general type, and if
G
is of general type then
G
does not preserve any line of
T
.

Proof.

(i)
Let
\xi
be an end of
T
fixed by
G
and take vertices
v_{t}
for
t\geq 0
and the homomorphism
\beta:=\beta_{\xi}
as in Lemma
2.9
. Then given
g\in G
, we see that
\beta(g)=0
if and only if
g
is elliptic. Thus
E:=\ker\beta
is a subgroup consisting of all the elliptic elements; in particular
E
contains all vertex stabilisers, so it is open. Taking a translation
s
in
G
such that
|\beta(s)|
is minimised, we then see that for each
g\in G
the value
|\beta(s)|
must divide
|\beta(g)|
. Consequently,
\beta(\langle s\rangle)=\beta(G)
and hence
G=E\rtimes\langle s\rangle
. Now
E
acts without translation and fixes an end; by considering the six types of action on a tree, we conclude that
E
is of fixed vertex or horocyclic type. To finish the proof of this part, we argue that
E
fixes a vertex if and only if
G
is lineal.

Suppose
E
fixes a vertex
v
. Then, being normal,
E
also fixes
s^{k}.v
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, and hence fixes pointwise the smallest subtree
T^{\prime}
containing
\{s^{k}.v\mid k\in\mathbb{Z}\}
. Since
T^{\prime}
is an
\langle s\rangle
-invariant subtree, it contains the axis of
s
. Thus
E
fixes pointwise the axis of
s
, and hence
G
preserves the axis of
s
, showing that
G
is lineal.

Conversely, suppose
G
is lineal and let
\xi
and
-\xi
be the two fixed ends of
G
, in other words the ends of the axis
L
of
s
. Then given
g\in E
, by Lemma
2.9
,
g
fixes pointwise a ray
R
representing
\xi
and
g
also fixes pointwise a ray
-R
representing
-\xi
. The union of these two rays contains all but finitely many vertices of
L
, while the remaining vertices of
L
lie on the shortest path from
-R
to
R
, so in fact
g
fixes
L
pointwise. Thus
E
fixes
L
pointwise.

(ii)
Since
G
is topologically perfect, it has no proper open normal subgroup
N
such that the quotient
G/N
is abelian. Hence, by (i),
G
cannot be lineal or focal. If
G
is of inversion type, or
G
is of general type and preserves a line
L
, then it has an open subgroup
H
of index
2
: in the former case we take
H
to be the parity-preserving subgroup of
G
, and in the latter case we take
H
to be the subgroup that preserves the orientation of
L
. In either case,
G/H
is a discrete abelian group of order
2
, which contradicts the hypothesis that
G
is topologically perfect. Thus
G
must be of one of the remaining types, that is, fixed vertex, horocyclic or general type, and in the general type case,
G
cannot preserve any line.
∎

The
smallest invariant subtree
of an action
(T,G)
is a proper nonempty subtree
T^{\prime}
of
T
such that
gT^{\prime}=T^{\prime}
and such that
T^{\prime}\subseteq T^{\prime\prime}
for every
G
-invariant subtree
T^{\prime\prime}
of
T
. Note that if
(T,G)
has a smallest invariant subtree then the smallest invariant subtree is necessarily unique.

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
(T,G)
be an action on a tree. Then
(T,G)
has a smallest invariant subtree
T^{\prime}
exactly when one of the following holds:

(i)

G
fixes a unique vertex, in which case
T^{\prime}
is that vertex;

(ii)

G
is of inversion type, in which case
T^{\prime}
is the invariant undirected edge;

(iii)

G
acts with translation, in which case
T^{\prime}
is the union of the axes of translation of
G
, and
T^{\prime}
is either a line or an infinitely ended tree. In particular, if
G
is of general type then
G
acts geometrically densely on
T^{\prime}
.

Proof.

In cases (i) and (ii), the smallest invariant subtree is given by
[
20
, Corollaire 3.5]
and its proof. If
G
acts with translation, then there is a smallest invariant subtree
T^{\prime}
by
[
20
, Corollaire 3.5]
, and moreover
T^{\prime}
is infinite. By
[
14
, Lemma 2.1(iii)]
,
T^{\prime}
is the union of the axes of translation of
G
on
T
. If
T^{\prime}
is not a line, then there are at least two distinct axes; since the powers of a translation
g
do not fix any end other than the ends of the axes of
g
, it follows that
G
has an infinite orbit on the ends of
T^{\prime}
, and in particular,
T^{\prime}
is infinitely ended. If
G
is of general type, then
G
fixes no end, so clearly the action of
G
on
T^{\prime}
is geometrically dense.

By Theorem
2.5
, the remaining cases are that
G
fixes more than one vertex, or that
G
is horocyclic. In the former case, distinct fixed vertices give disjoint invariant subtrees, so there is no smallest invariant subtree. If
G
is horocyclic with fixed end
\xi
, we see from Lemma
2.9
that there is a
G
-invariant Busemann function with focus
\xi
, so each of the horoballs centred at
\xi
spans a
G
-invariant subtree; the horoballs centred at
\xi
have empty intersection, and hence there is no smallest invariant subtree.
∎

The types of action on a tree

General type action implies a useful dichotomy in the normal subgroups of
G
.

Lemma 2.12
.

Let
T
be a tree, let
G\leq\mathrm{Aut}(T)
act with translation, let
T^{\prime}
be the smallest invariant subtree of
G
and let
N
be a normal subgroup of
G
. Suppose that
N
acts nontrivially on
T^{\prime}
. Then
N
is unbounded. If
G
is of general type and
T^{\prime}
has more than two ends, then
N
is of general type and the smallest invariant subtree of
N
is the same as that of
G
.

Proof.

Suppose that
N
fixes a vertex. Then there is a unique smallest subtree
T_{0}
containing all the fixed vertices of
N
, and we see that in fact,
N
fixes
T_{0}
pointwise. In particular, since
N\unlhd G
,
T_{0}
is
G
-invariant, so
T^{\prime}\subseteq T_{0}
. But then
N
acts trivially on
T^{\prime}
, a contradiction. Thus
N
has no fixed vertices. A similar argument shows that
N
does not preserve any undirected edge. So
N
is unbounded.

Now suppose that
G
is of general type and
T^{\prime}
has more than two ends, and consider the set
E
of ends fixed by
N
. Then
E
is
G
-invariant, and since
N
has unbounded action,
|E|\leq 2
. However,
G
cannot fix any end or preserve a set of two ends, since
T^{\prime}
has more than two ends. So
E=\emptyset
. We deduce that
N
has general type, with minimal subtree
T^{\prime\prime}
spanned by the axes of translation of
N
. Since
N\leq G
we see that
T^{\prime\prime}\subseteq T^{\prime}
; but since
N\unlhd G
, the tree
T^{\prime\prime}
is
G
-invariant, so
T^{\prime}\subseteq T^{\prime\prime}
. Thus
T^{\prime}=T^{\prime\prime}
.
∎

We also note a sufficient condition for
G
to have finitely many orbits on its smallest invariant subtree.

Lemma 2.13

(See also
[
4
, Lemma 2.4]
)
.

Let
(T,G)
be an action with translation on a tree, and let
T^{\prime}
be the smallest invariant subtree of
T
. Suppose that
G
is generated by finitely many cosets of
G_{v}
, for some
v\in VT^{\prime}
. Then
G
has finitely many orbits on
VT^{\prime}\sqcup AT^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We can take a symmetric generating set for
G
of the form
S=F\cup G_{v}
, where
F
is finite. Then the set
\{sv\mid s\in S\}
is finite and for each
s\in S
, the path
[v,sv]
from
v
to
sv
is contained in
T^{\prime}
. Let
T^{\prime\prime}
be the subtree spanned by the paths
[v,sv]
as
s
ranges over
S
. Then
T^{\prime\prime}
is finite and for each
s\in S
, the graph
T^{\prime\prime}\cup sT^{\prime\prime}
is connected: specifically, both
T^{\prime\prime}
and
sT^{\prime\prime}
are connected and contain
sv
. From here, we see that the graph
\bigcup_{g\in G}gT^{\prime\prime}
is also connected, and hence equal to
T^{\prime}
. This shows that
G
has finitely many orbits on
VT^{\prime}\sqcup AT^{\prime}
.
∎

2.3
The
(\mathrm{P})
-closure and property
(\mathrm{P})

Given a set
X
, we equip
\mathrm{Sym}(X)
with the permutation topology, that is, the coarsest group topology such that the stabiliser of every
x\in X
is open. Given a tree
T
, we give
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
the subspace topology, regarding
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
as a subgroup of
\mathrm{Sym}(VT)
. Observe that in fact
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
corresponds to a closed subgroup of
\mathrm{Sym}(VT)
; if
VT
is countable, this ensures that
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
is Polish (that is, separable and completely metrisable) and also totally disconnected, but
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
is not necessarily locally compact. Assuming
T
has no leaves, one could equivalently define the topology of
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
with respect to the permutation topology on arcs or undirected edges, or the permutation topology on one part of the natural bipartition of the vertices of
T
: this can be seen by noting that two undirected edges suffice to specify a vertex, two vertices to specify an arc, and two vertices in one part of the bipartition to specify a vertex in the other part.

Given
G\leq\mathrm{Aut}(T)
and
k\geq 1
, the
(\mathrm{P}_{k})
-closure
2
2

2

The notion of the
(\mathrm{P}_{k})
-closure of a group acting on a tree was introduced in
[
1
]
where it was simply called the
k
-closure; however the term
k
-closure has a well-established meaning in permutation group theory due to Wielandt, so here we use the term
(\mathrm{P}_{k})
-closure. The same definition is given independently in
[
18
]
in the context of generalising Leighton’s Theorem, where it is called the
k
-symmetry-restricted closure
. In contrast to
[
1
]
and
[
18
]
, we are interested in actions on trees that are not necessarily locally finite; we specify the condition on finite subsets of
B_{k}(v)
rather than
B_{k}(v)
itself in order to ensure that the
(\mathrm{P}_{k})
-closure is closed in the permutation topology.
of
G
, denoted by
G^{(\mathrm{P}_{k})}
, is the set of automorphisms
g\in\mathrm{Aut}(T)
such that for all
v\in VT
, and every finite set of vertices
X
all of which are at distance at most
k
from
v
, there exists
g_{X}\in G
such that
gw=g_{X}w
for every vertex
w\in X
. We say
G
is
(\mathrm{P}_{k})
-closed
if
G=G^{(\mathrm{P}_{k})}
.

We will use some basic properties of the
(\mathrm{P}_{k})
-closure. We include proofs here because the approach of
[
1
]
implicitly assumes that trees are locally finite.

For the rest of this article, we define
G_{(X)}:=\{g\in G\mid\forall x\in X:gx=x\}
, where
X
is a set of vertices of
T
.

Proposition 2.14

(See
[
1
]
Proposition 3.4)
.

Let
T
be a tree, let
G\leq\mathrm{Aut}(T)
and let
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

(i)

G^{(\mathrm{P}_{k})}
is a closed subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
.

(ii)

G^{(\mathrm{P}_{l})}=(G^{(\mathrm{P}_{k})})^{(\mathrm{P}_{l})}
whenever
l\leq k
. In particular,
(G^{(\mathrm{P}_{k})})^{(\mathrm{P}_{k})}=G^{(\mathrm{P}_{k})}
, so
G^{(\mathrm{P}_{k})}
is
(\mathrm{P}_{k})
-closed.

Proof.

(i)
Write
A:=\mathrm{Aut}(T)
. Let
g,h\in G^{(\mathrm{P}_{k})}
, let
v\in VT
and let
X
be a finite set of vertices all of which are at distance at most
k
from
v
. Then there exists
h_{X}\in G
such that
h_{X}w=hw
for all
w\in X
. In turn,
hX:=\{hw\mid w\in X\}
is a finite set of vertices, all of which are at distance at most
k
from
hv
, so there exists
g_{hX}\in G
such that
g_{hX}w=gw
for all
w\in hX
. Thus
g_{hX}h_{X}
is an element of
G
such that
g_{hX}h_{X}w=ghw
for all
w\in X
. We conclude that
gh\in G^{(\mathrm{P}_{k})}
. Similarly, it is clear that
G^{(\mathrm{P}_{k})}
is closed under inverses. Thus
G^{(\mathrm{P}_{k})}
is a subgroup of
A
.

Let
\mathcal{X}_{k}
be the set of all finite sets
X
of vertices in
T
, such that there is a vertex
v
at distance at most
k
from every vertex in
X
. Then
A_{(X)}
is an open subgroup of
A
for every
X\in\mathcal{X}_{k}
. Observe that given
g\in A\smallsetminus G^{(\mathrm{P}_{k})}
, then there exists
X_{g}\in\mathcal{X}_{k}
such that no element of
G
agrees with
g
on
X_{g}
, and hence no element of
G^{(\mathrm{P}_{k})}
agrees with
g
on
X_{g}
, that is,
G^{(\mathrm{P}_{k})}\cap gA_{(X_{g})}=\emptyset
. We can therefore express the complement of
G^{(\mathrm{P}_{k})}
as the following union of open sets:

A\smallsetminus G^{(\mathrm{P}_{k})}=\bigcup_{g\in A\smallsetminus G^{(\mathrm{P}_{k})}}gA_{(X_{g})}.

Hence
G^{(\mathrm{P}_{k})}
is closed in
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
.

(ii)
Since
G\leq G^{(\mathrm{P}_{k})}
then
G^{(\mathrm{P}_{l})}\leq(G^{(\mathrm{P}_{k})})^{(\mathrm{P}_{l})}
. Let
g\in(G^{(\mathrm{P}_{k})})^{(\mathrm{P}_{l})}
and let
X
be a finite set of vertices of
T
, all of which are at distance at most
l
from some vertex
v
. Then there exists
g_{X}\in G^{(\mathrm{P}_{k})}
such that
g_{X}w=gw
for all
w\in X
. But then all the vertices in
X
are at distance at most
k
from
v
, so there exists
g^{\prime}_{X}\in G
such that
g^{\prime}_{X}w=g_{X}w=gw
for all
w\in X
. Hence
G^{(\mathrm{P}_{l})}=(G^{(\mathrm{P}_{k})})^{(\mathrm{P}_{l})}
. The remaining conclusions are clear.
∎

It is useful to note that the property of being
(\mathrm{P}_{k})
-closed is inherited by fixators of vertices and is stable under taking intersections of subgroups.

Lemma 2.15
.

Let
T
be a tree, let
k
be a positive integer and let
\mathcal{G}
be a family of
(\mathrm{P}_{k})
-closed subgroups of
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
. Then
H=\bigcap_{G\in\mathcal{G}}G
is
(\mathrm{P}_{k})
-closed.

Proof.

Let
h\in H^{(\mathrm{P}_{k})}
and let
G\in\mathcal{G}
. Then for each
v\in VT
and each finite set
X
of vertices in
B_{k}(v)
, there is some
g_{X}\in H
such that
g_{X}w=hw
for all
w\in X
. In particular,
g_{X}\in G
. Since
G
is
(\mathrm{P}_{k})
-closed, it follows that
h\in G
; since
G\in\mathcal{G}
was arbitrary, in fact
h\in H
. Thus
H=H^{(\mathrm{P}_{k})}
, so
H
is
(\mathrm{P}_{k})
-closed.
∎

Lemma 2.16
.

Let
T
be a tree, let
G\leq\mathrm{Aut}(T)
, let
k\geq 1
and let
X
be a set of vertices of
T
. If
G
is
(\mathrm{P}_{k})
-closed, then so is
G_{(X)}
.

Proof.

Suppose
G
is
(\mathrm{P}_{k})
-closed, and let
H=(G_{(X)})^{(\mathrm{P}_{k})}
. Given
v\in X
and
g\in H
, we see from the definition of the
(\mathrm{P}_{k})
-closure that
g
must fix
v
. So in fact
H
is a subgroup of
(G^{(\mathrm{P}_{k})})_{(X)}
, which is just
G_{(X)}
. Hence
H=G_{(X)}
.∎

We also recall Tits’ property
(\mathrm{P})
, introduced in
[
20
]
.

Definition 2.17
.

Let
T
be a tree and let
\theta:G\rightarrow\mathrm{Aut}(T)
be a group homomorphism. Given a nonempty (finite or infinite) simple path
L
in
T
, let
\pi_{L}:VT\rightarrow VL
be the closest point projection of the vertices of
T
onto
L
; observe that for each
x\in L
, the set
\pi^{-1}_{L}(x)
is a nonempty subtree of
T
. Write
\theta(G)_{(L)}
for the pointwise stabiliser of
L
(so
\theta(G)_{(L)}
preserves setwise each of the fibres
\pi^{-1}_{L}(x)
of
\pi_{L}
). Then for each vertex
x\in L
, there is a natural homomorphism
\phi_{x}:\theta(G)_{(L)}\rightarrow\mathrm{Sym}(\pi^{-1}_{L}(x))
induced by the action of
\theta(G)_{(L)}
on
\pi^{-1}_{L}(x)
. We can combine the homomorphisms
\phi_{x}
in the obvious way to obtain a homomorphism

\phi_{L}:\theta(G)_{(L)}\rightarrow\prod_{x\in VL}\phi_{x}(\theta(G)_{(L)}).

In general,
\phi_{L}
is injective but not necessarily surjective. We say
G
(or more precisely, the action of
G
on
T
) has
property
(\mathrm{P})
(with respect to a collection
\mathcal{L}
of simple paths) if
\theta(G)
is closed in
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
and
\phi_{L}
is surjective for every possible choice of
L
(such that
L\in\mathcal{L}
).

A major motivation of
[
1
]
was to generalise Tits’ property
(\mathrm{P})
, and indeed property
(\mathrm{P})
has a natural interpretation in terms of the
(\mathrm{P}_{1})
-closure.

Theorem 2.18

(See
[
1
]
Theorem 5.4 and Corollary 6.4)
.

Let
T
be a tree and let
G
be a closed subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
. Then
G=G^{(\mathrm{P}_{1})}
if and only if
G
satisfies Tits’ property
(\mathrm{P})
. Furthermore, if
G
has property
(\mathrm{P})
with respect to the edges of
T
, then
G=G^{(\mathrm{P}_{1})}
, so
G
has property
(\mathrm{P})
with respect to all simple paths.

Proof.

Let
L
be a nonempty simple path in
T
, and let
g\in\mathrm{Aut}(T)_{(L)}
such that

{\phi_{L}(g)\in\prod_{v\in VL}\phi_{v}(G_{(L)})};

say
\phi_{L}(g)=(s_{v})_{v\in VL}
. We now claim that
g\in G^{(\mathrm{P}_{1})}
(indeed,
g\in(G_{(L)})^{(\mathrm{P}_{1})}
). Let
X
be a finite set of vertices, all adjacent to some vertex
w
of
T
.
We will show that there exists
g_{X}\in G_{(L)}
such that
g
agrees with
g_{X}
on
X
.
We may assume that
X\cap VL=\emptyset
, since the vertices of
L
all are all fixed by both
g
and
G_{(L)}
. Let
x=\pi_{L}(w)
. We observe that since
X
is disjoint from
VL
and the set
X\cup\{w\}
spans a subtree, any path from
X
to
L
must pass through
x
, in other words
X\subseteq\pi^{-1}_{L}(x)
. There is then
g_{X}\in G_{(L)}
such that
\phi_{v}(g_{X})=s_{x}
, so that
g
agrees with
g_{X}
on
\pi^{-1}_{L}(x)
and in particular on
X
. Given the freedom of choice of
X
, we conclude that
g\in G^{(\mathrm{P}_{1})}
as claimed. Thus if
G=G^{(\mathrm{P}_{1})}
, then
G
has property
(\mathrm{P})
.

Conversely, suppose that
G
is closed and satisfies property
(\mathrm{P})
with respect to the edges of
T
. Suppose that
G\neq G^{(\mathrm{P}_{1})}
and let
g\in G^{(\mathrm{P}_{1})}\smallsetminus G
. Since
G
is closed, the set
G^{(\mathrm{P}_{1})}\smallsetminus G
is a neighbourhood of
g
in
G^{(\mathrm{P}_{1})}
, so there is a finite set
X
of vertices such that
g(G^{(\mathrm{P}_{1})})_{(X)}\cap G=\emptyset
. Let
S
be the smallest subtree of
T
containing
X
; note that
\mathrm{Aut}(T)_{(X)}=\mathrm{Aut}(T)_{(S)}
, since every vertex of
S
lies on the shortest path between a pair of vertices in
X
. Let us suppose that
X
has been chosen so that
|S|
is minimised.

By the definition of
G^{(\mathrm{P}_{1})}
, we see that
S
is not a star, so for every
x\in S
, there is a vertex in
S
at distance
2
from
x
. Hence there exist adjacent vertices
x
and
y
of
S
such that neither
x
nor
y
is a leaf of
S
. Let
L
be the path formed by the single arc
(x,y)
. By the minimality of
|S|
, there is some
h\in G
such that
gx=hx
and
gy=hy
, so that
h^{-1}g
fixes
L
pointwise. Let

S_{1}=(S\cap\pi^{-1}_{L}(x))\cup\{y\}\text{ and }S_{2}=(S\cap\pi^{-1}_{L}(y))\cup\{x\}.

Note that for
i=1,2
, then
S_{i}
is the set of vertices of a subtree of
S
that contains
L
. The condition that neither
x
nor
y
is a leaf of
S
ensures that there is some neighbour of
x
in
S
that is not contained in
S_{2}
, and similarly there is some neighbour of
y
in
S
that is not contained in
S_{1}
. Hence
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
are both proper subtrees of
S
, so by the minimality of
|S|
, there exists
h_{1},h_{2}\in G
such that
h_{i}w_{i}=h^{-1}gw_{i}
for all
w_{i}\in S_{i}
(
i=1,2
). Indeed,
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
are elements of
G_{(L)}
, since
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
both agree with
h^{-1}g
on
L
. In particular, we see that the action of
h^{-1}g
induces an element of
\phi_{x}(G_{(L)})\times\phi_{y}(G_{(L)})
. But then by (the restricted) property
(\mathrm{P})
, we have
h^{-1}g\in G_{(L)}
and hence
g\in G
, a contradiction.
∎

Recall that an action on a tree is
(\mathrm{P})
-closed if it is closed and has Tits’ independence property
(\mathrm{P})
. From now on we can refer to the
(\mathrm{P})
-closure
of an action, meaning the smallest
(\mathrm{P})
-closed group that contains it. We will use repeatedly without comment the fact that
(\mathrm{P})
is equivalent to
(\mathrm{P}_{1})
.

Lemma 2.19
.

Let
T
be a tree, let
G\leq\mathrm{Aut}(T)
and let
T^{\prime}
be a
G
-invariant subtree of
T
. Suppose that
(T,G)
has property
(\mathrm{P})
and that the action of
G
on
T^{\prime}
is closed. Then
(T^{\prime},G)
also has property
(\mathrm{P})
.

Proof.

Let
L
be a simple path in
T^{\prime}
. Since
(T,G)
has property
(\mathrm{P})
, the natural homomorphism

\phi_{L}:G_{(L)}\rightarrow\prod_{x\in VL}\phi_{x}(G_{(L)})

is surjective, where
\phi_{L}
and
\phi_{x}
are defined with respect to
T
. Now consider what happens if we replace
\phi_{L}
and
\phi_{x}
with
\phi^{\prime}_{L}
and
\phi^{\prime}_{x}
respectively, which are now defined with respect to
T^{\prime}
. We also have closest point projections
\pi_{L}
for
L
as a simple path in
T
and
\pi^{\prime}_{L}
for
L
as a simple path in
T^{\prime}
, but in fact
\pi_{L}
and
\pi^{\prime}_{L}
agree on
VT^{\prime}
. If we choose
g_{x}\in\phi^{\prime}_{x}(G_{(L)})
for each
x\in VL
, then there is some
h_{x}\in G_{(L)}
such that
\phi^{\prime}_{x}(h_{x})=g_{x}
, and then by the surjectivity of
\phi_{L}
, there is
g\in G_{(L)}
such that
\phi_{L}(g)=(\phi_{x}(h_{x}))_{x\in VL}
. But then since

(\pi^{\prime})^{-1}_{L}(x)=\pi^{-1}_{L}(x)\cap VT^{\prime}\subseteq\pi^{-1}_{L}(x),

we immediately see that

\phi^{\prime}_{L}(g)=(\phi^{\prime}_{x}(h_{x}))_{x\in VL}=(g_{x})_{x\in VL}.

Thus
\phi^{\prime}_{L}
is surjective, so
G
has property
(\mathrm{P})
on
T^{\prime}
.
∎

Lemma 2.20
.

Let
T
be a tree, let
G\leq\mathrm{Aut}(T)
be closed and let
T^{\prime}
be an invariant subtree for
(T,G)
. If there is an arc
a\in AT^{\prime}
such that the arc stabiliser
G_{a}
is compact in
(T,G)
, then the action of
G
on
T^{\prime}
is closed.

Proof.

Suppose
T^{\prime}
is an invariant subtree of
(T,G)
, and
a\in AT^{\prime}
is such that
G_{a}
is compact in
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
. The action of
G
on
T^{\prime}
is continuous because
(T,G)
is Hausdorff and vertex stabilisers
G_{v}
for
v\in VT^{\prime}
are open in
(T,G)
. The continuous image of the compact group
G_{a}
is compact in
(T^{\prime},G)
, and therefore closed in the Hausdorff group
\mathrm{Aut}(T^{\prime})
. In other words, the stabiliser in
(T^{\prime},G)
of
a\in T^{\prime}
is a closed subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(T^{\prime})
, and it follows then that
(T^{\prime},G)
is a closed subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(T^{\prime})
.
∎

Property
(\mathrm{P})
was originally introduced as a sufficient condition to obtain a simple group. We recall two relevant results from the literature.

Definition 2.21
.

Given
G\leq\mathrm{Aut}(T)
, write
G^{+}
for the subgroup of
G
generated by arc stabilisers in
G
. Given
a\in AT
, write
\mathrm{rist}_{G}(T_{a})
for the rigid stabiliser of
T_{a}
, in other words, the pointwise stabiliser of
T_{\overline{a}}
; write
G^{++}
for the closure of the subgroup of
G
generated by the rigid stabilisers of half-trees in
G
.

Theorem 2.22

(
[
20
]
Théorème 4.5)
.

Let
T
be a tree and let
G
be a geometrically dense subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
with property
(\mathrm{P})
. Then every nontrivial subgroup of
G
normalised by
G^{+}
contains
G^{+}
. In particular,
G^{+}
is trivial or abstractly simple.

Theorem 2.23

(
[
14
]
Theorem 2.4)
.

Let
T
be a tree and let
G
be a closed geometrically dense subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
. Then every nontrivial closed subgroup of
G
normalised by
G^{++}
contains
G^{++}
. In particular,
G^{++}
is trivial or topologically simple.

Remark 2.24
.

Note that in general
G^{++}\leq G^{+}
; if
G
is
(\mathrm{P})
-closed, then equality holds. Since every proper subtree is contained in a half-tree, another way of expressing the condition that
G^{++}
is nontrivial is the following: there exists
g\in G
, such that the convex hull of
\{v\in VT\mid gv\neq v\}
is not the whole of
T
.

In the situations of Theorems
2.22
and
2.23
, we note that as soon as
G^{+}
or
G^{++}
is nontrivial, then it is geometrically dense by Lemma
2.12
. (Since
G
is geometrically dense, the tree has no leaves; the case that
T
has fewer than three ends is ruled out by the existence of nontrivial arc stabilisers.)

An additional complication that occurs when working with locally infinite trees is that the local action of a given group need not be closed. We say a subgroup
G\leq\mathrm{Aut}(T)
has
closed local actions
if for every
v\in VT
, the permutation group induced by
G_{v}
on
o^{-1}(v)
is closed. In practice, most of the groups we are interested in will have closed local actions; the next lemma justifies this assumption by providing some sufficient conditions.

Lemma 2.25
.

Let
T
be a tree and let
G\leq\mathrm{Aut}(T)
. If one or more of the following conditions holds, then
G
has closed local actions:

(i)

G
is
(\mathrm{P})
-closed;

(ii)

G_{a}
is compact for every
a\in AT
;

(iii)

There is some group
L\leq\mathrm{Aut}(T)
, such that
L^{+}\leq G\leq L
and
L
has closed local actions.

Proof.

Fix
v\in VT
and let
\theta:\mathrm{Aut}(T)_{v}\rightarrow\mathrm{Sym}(o^{-1}(v))
be the action of
\mathrm{Aut}(T)_{v}
on
o^{-1}(v)
. We aim to show in each case that
\theta(G_{v})
is closed in
\mathrm{Sym}(o^{-1}(v))
.

For (i), we suppose that
G
is
(\mathrm{P})
-closed. Suppose there is
h\in\mathrm{Sym}(o^{-1}(v))
and a net
(g_{i})_{i\in I}
of elements of
G_{v}
such that
\theta(g_{i})\rightarrow h
. Then for all finite subsets
F
of
o^{-1}(v)
, there is
i\in I
such that for all
j>i
and
a\in F
we have
g_{j}a=ha
. We build an automorphism
g
of
T
as follows: set
gv=v
and if
a\in o^{-1}(v)
, set
ga=ha
and
g\overline{a}=\overline{ha}
. For each
a\in o^{-1}(v)
, we choose
j_{a}\in I
large enough that
g_{j_{a}}a=ha
(the choices of
j_{a}
can be made independently of one another). We then set
gr=g_{j_{a}}r
, for all vertices and arcs
r
of
T_{a}
. It is then easy to check that
g
is an automorphism of
T
such that for all
w\in VT
and all finite subsets
F\subseteq o^{-1}(w)
, there is some
g_{F}\in G
such that
g_{F}
agrees with
g
on
F
. Hence

g\in G^{(\mathrm{P}_{1})}=G^{(\mathrm{P})}=G.

In particular, we have obtained an element of
G_{v}
that acts as
h
on
o^{-1}(v)
; given the choice of
h
, we conclude that
\theta(G_{v})
is closed, so
G
has closed local actions.

Case (ii) follows from Lemma
2.20
, by considering the action of
G_{v}
on the
G_{v}
-invariant subtree spanned by
v
and its neighbours.

In case (iii), we see that
\theta(G_{v})
contains all the point stabilisers of
\theta(L_{v})
, so
\theta(G_{v})
is open in
\theta(L_{v})
. Since
\theta(L_{v})
is closed in
\mathrm{Sym}(o^{-1}(v))
, we deduce that
\theta(G_{v})
is closed in
\mathrm{Sym}(o^{-1}(v))
.
∎

2.4
Bass–Serre theory

Here we recall some standard results in Bass–Serre theory for groups acting on trees. In this article we will not be using Bass–Serre theory to construct the groups but we will use it occasionally to analyse them. Note that conventional Bass–Serre theory considers only actions on trees without inversion, whereas we allow reversal of edges; we keep track of these edge reversals in the quotient graph by allowing a loop to be its own inverse. This added generality has no deep significance, since an action with inversion can always be converted to an action without inversion by subdividing edges, but it necessitates some adjustments to the statements.

Given a group
G
acting on a tree
T
, we define the
inversion-free subdivision

T^{i}
by subdividing in two parts those edges
a
of
T
such that
\overline{a}\in Ga
. Analogously, in the quotient graph
\Gamma=G\backslash T
, we define the
inversion-free subdivision
(or
orientable subdivision
) of
\Gamma^{i}
of
\Gamma
by taking each loop
a
such that
a=\overline{a}
(with
o(a)=t(a)=v
, say), adding a new vertex
v_{a}
, and replacing
a
with the geometric edge
\{a^{\prime},\overline{a^{\prime}}\}
where
o(a^{\prime})=v
and
t(a^{\prime})=v_{a}
, so that
\Gamma^{i}
is an orientable graph. The action of
G
on
T^{i}
is then without inversion, and the quotient map from
T
to
\Gamma
naturally gives rise to a quotient map from
T^{i}
to
\Gamma^{i}
.

Lemma 2.26

(
[
16
, I.3.1, Proposition 14]
)
.

Let
G
be a group acting without inversion on a tree
T
. Then every subtree of
G\backslash T
lifts to a subtree of
T
.

Theorem 2.27

(
[
16
, I.5.4, Corollary 1 & Exercise 2]
)
.

Suppose that
T
is a tree, and let
G
be a group acting on
T
without inversion. Let
R
be the group generated by all vertex stabilisers
G_{v}
,
v\in VT
. Then
R
is a normal subgroup of
G
, and
G/R
is isomorphic to the fundamental group of
G\backslash T
. Moreover,
G=R
if and only if
G\backslash T
is a tree.

Theorem 2.28

(
[
16
, I.5.4, Theorem 13]
)
.

(Bass–Serre structure theorem for groups acting on trees)
Let
G
be a group acting on a tree
T
. For the inversion-free subdivision
T^{i}
, let
\pi:T^{i}\rightarrow G\backslash T^{i}
be the quotient map of
(T^{i},G)
. Choose a subtree
T^{\prime}
of
T^{i}
that is a lift of a maximal subtree of
G\backslash T^{i}
. Choose a subset
E^{+}\subseteq AT^{i}
such that
o(a)\in VT^{\prime}
for all
a\in E^{+}
, such that
\pi
is injective on
E^{+}
and
\pi(E)
is an orientation of
G\backslash T^{i}
and set
E=E^{+}\cup\overline{E^{+}}
. For each
a\in AT^{i}
let
\tau_{a}
be the inclusion of
G_{a}
into
G_{t(a)}
. For each
a\in E^{+}
choose
s_{a}\in G
so that
s^{-1}_{a}t(a)\in VT^{\prime}
, with
s_{a}=1
if
a\in AT^{\prime}
, and set
s_{\overline{a}}=s^{-1}_{a}
. Write
F(E)
for the free group over
\{s_{a}\mid a\in E\}
. Then
G
has the form
