\frac{F(E)\ast\Asterisk_{v\in VT^{\prime}}G_{v}}{\langle\langle s_{a}\tau_{a}(g)s_{\overline{a}}\tau_{\overline{a}}(g)^{-1}\;(a\in E,g\in G_{a}),\;s_{a}s_{\overline{a}}\;(a\in E),\;s_{a}\;(a\in AT^{\prime})\rangle\rangle}.

Definition 2.29
.

Retain the hypotheses and notation of the previous theorem. Let
c
be a directed path in
G\backslash T^{i}
with vertex sequence
(v_{0},v_{1},\dots,v_{n})
and arc sequence
(a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{n})
. A
word of type
c
is then a word
w=g_{0}s_{e_{1}}g_{1}\dots g_{n-1}s_{e_{n}}g_{n}
over
E\sqcup\bigsqcup_{v\in VT^{\prime}}G_{v}
such that
g_{i}\in G_{v^{\prime}_{i}}
where
\pi(v^{\prime}_{i})=v_{i}
and
\pi(e_{i})=a_{i}
. Say that
w
is
reduced
if it is of type
c
for some directed path
c
in
G\backslash T^{i}
, and satisfies the following conditions:

If
n=0
then
g_{0}\neq 1
; if
n\geq 1
, then for each index
i
such that
a_{i+1}=\overline{a_{i}}
, then
g_{i}\not\in G_{e_{i}}
.

Theorem 2.30

(
[
16
, I.5.2, Theorem 11]
)
.

(Normal form theorem of Bass–Serre theory)
Under the hypotheses of Theorem
2.28
, every reduced word evaluates to a nontrivial element of
G
.

The following corollary is valid without assuming that
G
acts without inversion, since on the one hand, every group generated by vertex stabilisers acts without inversion (since it is parity-preserving) and on the other, if
\Gamma=G\backslash T
is a tree then
\Gamma
has no loops, so certainly
G
acts on
T
without inversion.

Corollary 2.31

(
[
16
, I.5.4, Exercise 2]
)
.

Let
G
be a group acting on a tree
T
. Then
G\backslash T
is a tree if and only if
G
is generated by vertex stabilisers. Moreover, if
G
is generated by vertex stabilisers, then
G=\langle G_{v}\mid v\in VT^{\prime}\rangle
where
T^{\prime}
is a lift of a maximal subtree in
G\backslash T
.

3
A parametrisation of
(\mathrm{P})
-closed groups

Definition 3.1
.

A
local action diagram
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(G(v)))
consists of the following information:

•

A connected graph
\Gamma
.

•

For each arc
a
of
\Gamma
, a nonempty set
X_{a}
(called the
colour set
of
a
). We take the colour sets of distinct arcs to be disjoint from one another, and the elements of
\bigsqcup_{a\in A\Gamma}X_{a}
are the
colours
of
\Delta
.

•

For each vertex
v
of
\Gamma
, a group
G(v)
(called the
local action
at
v
) with the following properties: write
X_{v}
to denote the disjoint union
\bigsqcup_{a\in o^{-1}(v)}X_{a}
, then the group
G(v)
is a closed subgroup of
\mathrm{Sym}(X_{v})
and the sets
X_{a}
are the orbits of
G(v)
on
X_{v}
.

Definition 3.2
.

Let
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(G(v)))
and
\Delta^{\prime}=(\Gamma^{\prime},(X^{\prime}_{a}),(G^{\prime}(v)))
be local action diagrams.

An
isomorphism

\boldsymbol{\theta}=(\theta,(\theta_{v}))
from
\Delta
to
\Delta^{\prime}
is an isomorphism
\theta:\Gamma\rightarrow\Gamma^{\prime}
of graphs, together with a bijection
\theta_{v}:X_{v}\rightarrow X^{\prime}_{\theta(v)}
for each
v\in V\Gamma
that restricts to a bijection from
X_{a}
to
X_{\theta(a)}
for each
a\in o^{-1}(v)
, and such that
\theta_{v}G(v)\theta^{-1}_{v}=G^{\prime}(v^{\prime})
.

Local action diagrams have the advantage of having a simple description from a combinatorial perspective. In terms of the permutation groups
G(v)
, there are no interactions between them or compatibility conditions to check, except that
G(v)
should have the specified orbit structure. However, we will see that they provide a parametrisation of all
(\mathrm{P})
-closed groups of tree automorphisms, taken up to isomorphisms of the tree. Our aim in this section is to prove the following:

Theorem 3.3
.

There is a natural one-to-one correspondence between isomorphism classes of local action diagrams, and isomorphism classes of pairs
(T,G)
where
T
is a tree and
G
is a
(\mathrm{P})
-closed subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
.

Definition 3.4
.

Given a local action diagram
\Delta
, a
\Delta
-tree

\mathbf{T}
is a tree
T
together with a surjective graph homomorphism
\pi:T\rightarrow\Gamma
and a
\Delta
-colouring
, that is, a map
\mathcal{L}:AT\rightarrow\bigsqcup_{a\in A\Gamma}X_{a}
, such that for each vertex
v\in VT
, and each arc
a
in
o^{-1}(\pi(v))
, the map
\mathcal{L}
restricts to a bijection
\mathcal{L}_{v,a}
from
\{b\in o^{-1}(v)\mid\pi(b)=a\}
to
X_{a}
. Write
\mathbf{T}=(T,\mathcal{L},\pi)
, and given
v\in VT
, write
\mathcal{L}_{v}
for the restriction of
\mathcal{L}
to a bijection from
o^{-1}(v)
to
X_{\pi(v)}
.

Note that the groups
G(v)
play no role in the definition of
\mathbf{T}
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
\Delta
be a local action diagram. Then there exists a
\Delta
-tree. Moreover, given any two
\Delta
-trees
(T,\pi,\mathcal{L})
and
(T^{\prime},\pi^{\prime},\mathcal{L}^{\prime})
, there is a graph isomorphism
\alpha:T\rightarrow T^{\prime}
such that
\pi^{\prime}\circ\alpha=\pi
.

Proof.

Choose a base vertex
v_{0}\in V\Gamma
. We construct a
\Delta
-tree
\mathbf{T}
as follows.

Given
v\in V\Gamma
and
c\in X_{v}
, the
type

p(c)
of
c
is the unique
a\in A\Gamma
such that
c\in X_{a}
. A
coloured path (of length
n
)
in
\Gamma
is a finite sequence
(c_{1},c_{2},\dots,c_{n})
of colours, where for each
1\leq i<n
, we have
o(p(c_{i+1}))=t(p(c_{i}))
. The
origin
of the coloured path is
o(p(c_{1}))
.

Vertices
v\in VT
will be labelled by coloured paths with origin
v_{0}
. For vertex labels
v=(c_{1},c_{2},\dots,c_{n})
and
w=(c_{1},c_{2},\dots,c_{n},c_{n+1},\dots,c_{m})
we say that
v
is a
prefix
of
w
.
For each vertex label
v=(c_{1},c_{2},\dots,c_{n})
, there will also be a reverse label
\overline{v}=(d_{1},d_{2},\dots,d_{n})
of the same length, where
d_{i}
is a colour such that
p(d_{i})=\overline{p(c_{i})}
, and such that if
v
is a prefix of
w
, then
\overline{v}
is the corresponding prefix of
\overline{w}
. We produce the vertices of
VT
inductively starting at a root vertex
()
.

Suppose we have defined a vertex
v=(c_{1},c_{2},\dots,c_{n})
with reverse label
\overline{v}=(d_{1},d_{2},\dots,d_{n})
. Then we define vertices
v_{+c_{n+1}}=(c_{1},\dots,c_{n},c_{n+1})
, for all
c_{n+1}
such that
o(p(c_{n+1}))=t(p(c_{n}))
and
c_{n+1}\neq d_{n}
. We then set
\overline{v_{+c_{n+1}}}=(d_{1},d_{2},\dots,d_{n+1})
, where
d_{n+1}
is some element of
X_{\overline{p(c_{n+1})}}
(chosen arbitrarily).

The set
AT_{+}
of forward arcs of
T
consists of ordered pairs
(v,w)
, where
v
is a prefix of
w
of length one less than
w
; then
AT_{-}=:\{(w,v)\mid(v,w)\in AT_{+}\}
and
AT:=AT_{-}\sqcup AT_{+}
. Origin and terminal vertices and edge reversal are defined in the obvious way, and it is clear that we obtain a tree. The colouring
\mathcal{L}
is defined as follows: given
(v,w)\in AT_{+}
, then
\mathcal{L}(v,w)
is the last entry of
w
and
\mathcal{L}(w,v)
is the last entry of
\overline{w}
.

The graph homomorphism
\pi:T\rightarrow\Gamma
is given by
\pi(())=v_{0}
for the base vertex;
\pi(v)=t(p(c_{n}))
for any vertex
v=(c_{1},\dots,c_{n})
in
VT
; and
\pi(a)=p(\mathcal{L}(a))
for
a\in AT
. Given the way in which the entries
c_{i}
and
d_{i}
were chosen and used to define
\mathcal{L}
, one sees that
\pi
is a surjective graph homomorphism.

Given a vertex
v=(c_{1},\dots,c_{n})
with reverse label
\overline{v}=(d_{1},d_{2},\dots,d_{n})
, then
v
has one parent vertex
(c_{1},\dots,c_{n-1})
and a set of child vertices of the form
(c_{1},\dots,c_{n},c^{\prime})
, where
c^{\prime}
ranges over the set
X_{t(p(c_{n}))}\smallsetminus\{d_{n}\}=X_{\pi(v)}\smallsetminus\{d_{n}\}
. The set
o^{-1}(v)
is thus in a natural bijection with
X_{\pi(v)}
in a manner that respects the partition into sets
X_{a}
for
a\in o^{-1}(\pi(v))
, and the colouring produces the same bijection. In particular, for each arc
a\in o^{-1}(\pi(v))
, we see that
\mathcal{L}
restricts to a bijection from
\{b\in o^{-1}(v)\mid\pi(b)=a\}
to
X_{a}
. Thus the object
\mathbf{T}
we have constructed is a
\Delta
-tree.

Now suppose that we have two
\Delta
-trees
(T,\pi,\mathcal{L})
and
(T^{\prime},\pi^{\prime},\mathcal{L}^{\prime})
. We construct a graph isomorphism
\alpha:T\rightarrow T^{\prime}
compatible with
(\pi,\pi^{\prime})
inductively as follows.

Choose
v_{1}\in VT
and
w_{1}\in VT^{\prime}
such that
\pi(v_{1})=\pi^{\prime}(w_{1})=v_{0}
, and set
\alpha(v_{1})=w_{1}
. Suppose we have defined
\alpha
for vertices and arcs in
B_{n}(v_{1})
(
n\geq 0
), let
v
be a vertex in
T
at distance
n
from
v_{1}
and let
w=\alpha(v)
. Then
\pi(v)=\pi^{\prime}(w)
by the induction hypothesis. Given
a\in o^{-1}(\pi(v))
, we have bijections
\mathcal{L}_{v,a}:\{b\in o^{-1}(v)\mid\pi(b)=a\}\rightarrow X_{a}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{w,a}:=\{b\in o^{-1}(w)\mid\pi^{\prime}(b)=a\}\rightarrow X_{a}
. In particular, the sets
\{b\in o^{-1}(v)\mid\pi(b)=a\}
and
\{b\in o^{-1}(w)\mid\pi^{\prime}(b)=a\}
have the same size, so we can extend
\alpha
to include
o^{-1}(v)
in its domain, in such a way that it restricts to a bijection from
\{b\in o^{-1}(v)\mid\pi(b)=a\}
to
\{b\in o^{-1}(w)\mid\pi^{\prime}(b)=a\}
. The choice of bijection is unimportant here, except in the case that
\pi^{-1}(a)
contains an arc
a^{\prime}
starting at
v
in the direction of
v_{1}
: in this case,
\alpha(a^{\prime})
has already been chosen, so we choose a bijection from
\{b\in o^{-1}(v)\mid\pi(b)=a,b\neq a^{\prime}\}
to
\{b\in o^{-1}(w)\mid\pi^{\prime}(b)=a,b\neq\alpha(a^{\prime})\}
. For
b\in t^{-1}(v)
and
v^{\prime}=o(b)
, we set
\alpha(b)=\overline{\alpha(\overline{b})}
and
\alpha(v^{\prime})=o(\alpha(b))
respectively. This extends the definition of
\alpha
to a ball of radius
n+1
about
v_{1}
; notice that
\alpha
still produces a graph isomorphism from
B_{n+1}(v_{1})
to
B_{n+1}(w_{1})
, completing the inductive step. We can thus extend
\alpha
to a graph isomorphism from
T
to
T^{\prime}
such that
\pi^{\prime}\circ\alpha=\pi
.
∎

Note: we do not claim that
\alpha
can be chosen to map
\mathcal{L}
to
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
.

Definition 3.6
.

Let
G
be a group of automorphisms of a tree
T
. We define an
associated local action diagram

\Delta
and equip
T
with the structure of a
\Delta
-tree as follows.

•

\Gamma
is the quotient graph
G\backslash T
, and
\pi
is the natural quotient map.

•

For each
v\in V\Gamma
, choose a vertex
v^{*}\in\pi^{-1}(v)
; write
V^{*}
for the set of vertices so obtained. Given
a\in A\Gamma
such that
v=o(a)
, let
X_{a}=\{b\in o^{-1}(v^{*})\mid\pi(b)=a\}
. The set
X_{v}:=o^{-1}(v^{*})
is then naturally partitioned as required. Define the group
G(v)
to be the closure of the permutation group induced on
X_{v}
by
G_{v^{*}}
.

•

For each
w\in VT
, choose
g_{w}\in G
such that
g_{w}w\in V^{*}
. Then
g_{w}
also induces a bijection from
o^{-1}(w)
to
X_{v}
. Given
b\in o^{-1}(w)
, set
\mathcal{L}(b)=g_{w}b
.

The definition is such that given
v,w\in VT
such that
\pi(v)=\pi(w)
, the restrictions
\mathcal{L}_{v}
and
\mathcal{L}_{w}
of
\mathcal{L}
to
o^{-1}(v)
and
o^{-1}(w)
respectively are bijections that form two sides of a commuting triangle:
if
r_{v,w}
is the map from
o^{-1}(v)
to
o^{-1}(w)
induced by
g^{-1}_{w}g_{v}
, then

\mathcal{L}_{v}=\mathcal{L}_{w}r_{v,w}.

There are many choices for the associated local action diagram, but they are all isomorphic, as we see in the following lemma.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
T
be a tree and let
G
be a group of automorphisms of
T
. Then any two local action diagrams
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(G(v)))
and
\Delta^{\prime}=(\Gamma,(X^{\prime}_{a}),(G^{\prime}(v)))
associated to
G
are isomorphic, via an isomorphism
\boldsymbol{\theta}
that is the identity map on the graph
\Gamma
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality we can assume
G
is closed in
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
. From the definition, we see that
\Delta
and
\Delta^{\prime}
have the same associated graph
\Gamma=G\backslash T
; let
\theta
be the trivial graph automorphism of
\Gamma
. Given
v\in V\Gamma
, say the chosen element of
\pi^{-1}(v)
is
v^{*}
in the construction of
\Delta
, and
v^{**}
in the construction of
\Delta^{\prime}
. Then
v^{**}=g_{v}v^{*}
for some
g_{v}\in G
, since
\pi(v^{*})=Gv^{*}
. We can thus define a bijection
\theta_{v}
from
X_{v}:=o^{-1}(v^{*})
to
X^{\prime}_{v}:=o^{-1}(v^{**})
by setting
\theta_{v}(a)=g_{v}a
. Given that
g_{v}G_{v^{*}}g^{-1}_{v}=G_{v^{**}}
, and
G(v)
and
G^{\prime}(v)
are determined by the actions of the vertex stabilisers
G_{v^{*}}
and
G_{v^{**}}
respectively, we see that
\theta_{v}G(v)\theta^{-1}_{v}=G^{\prime}(v)
. In particular,
\theta_{v}
sends orbits of
G(v)
to orbits of
G^{\prime}(v)
, so it restricts to a bijection from
X_{a}
to
X^{\prime}_{a}
for each
a\in o^{-1}(v)
. Thus
(\theta,\theta_{v})
is an isomorphism of local action diagrams.
∎

Thus from now on, we can talk about
the
local action diagram
\Delta(T,G)
associated to
(T,G)
without ambiguity.

Definition 3.8
.

An
automorphism
of the
\Delta
-tree
\mathbf{T}
is a graph automorphism
\theta
of
T
such that
\pi\circ\theta=\pi
. Write
\mathrm{Aut}_{\pi}(T)
for the group of all such automorphisms. Given
g\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\pi}(T)
, a vertex
v\in VT
, and
\mathcal{L}
the colouring associated to
\mathbf{T}
, we define the
\mathcal{L}
-local action
of
g
at
v
as follows:

\sigma_{\mathcal{L},v}(g):X_{\pi(v)}\rightarrow X_{\pi(v)}\quad\quad\sigma_{\mathcal{L},v}(g)(c):=\mathcal{L}g\mathcal{L}|^{-1}_{o^{-1}(v)}(c).

We see that
\sigma_{\mathcal{L},v}(g)
is a permutation of
X_{\pi(v)}
, and for a vertex
v\in VT
the map
g\mapsto\sigma_{\mathcal{L},v}(g)
is continuous.
Finally, we define the
universal group of
\mathbf{T}
with respect to local actions
(G(v))_{v\in V\Gamma}
to be the set
\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{T},(G(v)))
of all elements
g
of
\mathrm{Aut}_{\pi}(T)
such that for every
v\in VT
, the permutation
\sigma_{\mathcal{L},v}(g)
belongs to
G(\pi(v))
.

Theorem 3.9
.

Let
\Delta
be a local action diagram, let
\mathbf{T}
be a
\Delta
-tree, and let
H=\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{T},(G(v)))
. Then
H
is a
(\mathrm{P})
-closed subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
;
\Delta
is isomorphic to a local action diagram associated to
H
; and for every vertex
v\in VT
and
g\in G(\pi(v))
, there is
h\in H_{v}
such that
\sigma_{\mathcal{L},v}(h)=g
.

Proof.

Let
g,h\in\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{T},(G(v)))
and let
v\in VT
. It is clear that
g^{-1},gh\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\pi}(T)
, so there is a fixed vertex
w\in V\Gamma
such that
w=\pi(v)=\pi(hv)=\pi(gv)=\pi(g^{-1}v)
. It is easily seen that
\sigma_{\mathcal{L},v}(g^{-1})
and
\sigma_{\mathcal{L},v}(gh)
are given by the following formulae:

\sigma_{\mathcal{L},v}(gh)=\sigma_{\mathcal{L},hv}(g)\sigma_{\mathcal{L},v}(h)

\sigma_{\mathcal{L},v}(g^{-1})=\left(\sigma_{\mathcal{L},g^{-1}v}(g)\right)^{-1}

Since
\sigma_{\mathcal{L},hv}(g)
,
\sigma_{\mathcal{L},v}(h)
and

\sigma_{\mathcal{L},g^{-1}v}(g)

are all in the group
G(w)
, we see that
\sigma_{\mathcal{L},v}(gh)
and
\sigma_{\mathcal{L},v}(g^{-1})
are also elements of
G(w)
. This proves
\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{T},(G(v)))
is closed under products and inverses. Since
\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{T},(G(v)))
clearly also contains the trivial automorphism of
T
, we conclude that
H:=\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{T},(G(v)))
is a subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}_{\pi}(T)
.

Since
H\leq\mathrm{Aut}_{\pi}(T)
, certainly every orbit of
H
is contained in a fibre of
\pi
. We claim that in fact
H
acts transitively on
\pi^{-1}(e)
where
e
is a vertex or arc of
\Gamma
. It is enough to show that
H
is transitive when
e
is an arc of
\Gamma
, as the vertex case will then follow by considering origin vertices of arcs. So fix arcs
a,b\in\pi^{-1}(e)
; we aim to construct
g\in H
such that
ga=b
. We define
g
in stages on balls of radius
n
about
v_{0}:=o(a)
.

Let
v^{\prime}_{0}=o(b)
and let
w=\pi(v_{0})
. Choose an element
h_{0}\in G(w)
such that
h_{0}\mathcal{L}(a)=\mathcal{L}(b)
; this is possible since by definition,
\mathcal{L}(a)
and
\mathcal{L}(b)
must lie in the same
G(w)
-orbit. Then there is a unique graph isomorphism
g_{1}
from
B_{1}(v_{0})
to
B_{1}(v^{\prime}_{0})
such that
\mathcal{L}g_{1}\mathcal{L}|_{o^{-1}(v_{0})}(c)=h_{0}(c)
for all
c\in X_{w}
.

Let us also pause to note that by varying
b
, we can obtain every element of
G(w)
as a suitable
h_{0}
whilst also fixing
v_{0}
: specifically, given
h\in G(w)
, then
h\mathcal{L}(a)=\mathcal{L}(b)
for some unique
b\in o^{-1}(v_{0})
, and hence in this case we can take
h_{0}=h
. Thus, provided we can extend
g_{1}
to an element of
H
, we will have shown that
H_{v_{0}}
achieves all possible values of
\sigma_{\mathcal{L},v_{0}}
at the vertex
v_{0}
. By varying
a
, the vertex
v_{0}
can also be made an arbitrary vertex of
T
.

Suppose we have defined a graph isomorphism
g_{n}
from
B_{n}(v_{0})
to
B_{n}(v^{\prime}_{0})
, such that

\mathcal{L}g_{n}\mathcal{L}|_{o^{-1}(v)}\in G(\pi(v))\text{ for all }v\in VB_{n-1}(v_{0}).

Let
S_{n}(v_{0})
be the sphere of radius
n
about
v_{0}
and let
v\in S_{n}(v_{0})
. We have already defined
g_{n}v
and also
g_{n}r
for the unique arc
r\in o^{-1}(v)
in the direction of
v_{0}
. Similar to before, we see that
\mathcal{L}(r)
and
\mathcal{L}(g_{n}r)
lie in the same
G(\pi(v))
-orbit, so there is
h_{n}\in G(\pi(v))
such that
h_{n}\mathcal{L}(r)=\mathcal{L}(g_{n}r)
. There is then a unique graph isomorphism
h^{\prime}(v)
from
B_{1}(v)
to
B_{1}(g_{n}v)
such that
\mathcal{L}h^{\prime}\mathcal{L}|_{o^{-1}(v)}(c)=h_{n}(c)
for all
c\in X_{\pi(v)}
. Note the domains of the maps
\{h^{\prime}(v)\mid v\in S_{n}(v_{0})\}
are pairwise disjoint; for each
v\in S_{n}(v_{0})
, the domains of
g_{n}
and
h^{\prime}(v)
overlap only on a single edge and its endpoints, and for this overlap,
g_{n}
and
h^{\prime}(v)
agree. We can thus combine
g_{n}
with the set of maps
\{h^{\prime}(v)\mid v\in S_{n}(v_{0})\}
to produce a graph isomorphism
g_{n+1}
from
B_{n+1}(v_{0})
to
B_{n+1}(v^{\prime}_{0})
. By construction, we see that
\mathcal{L}g_{n+1}\mathcal{L}|_{o^{-1}(v)}\in G(\pi(v))
for all
v\in VB_{n}(v_{0})
, completing the inductive step.

By combining the sequence
(g_{n})
of graph isomorphisms, we thus obtain
g\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\pi}(T)
such that
ga=b
and such that
\sigma_{\mathcal{L},v}(g)\in G(\pi(v))
for all
v\in VT
, so
g\in H
.

Now let
\Delta^{\prime}=(\Gamma^{\prime},(X^{\prime}_{a}),(H(v)))
be a local action diagram associated to
H
. We aim to construct an isomorphism
\boldsymbol{\theta}=(\theta,(\theta_{v}))
of local action diagrams from
\Delta^{\prime}
to
\Delta
. We have shown that
H
acts transitively on each fibre of
\pi
, so the quotient graph
\Gamma^{\prime}=H\backslash T
can be naturally identified with
\Gamma
and
\theta
can be taken to be the trivial graph isomorphism on
\Gamma
. Given
v\in V\Gamma
, let
v^{*}
be the chosen vertex of
VT
in the construction of
\Delta^{\prime}
. Then for each
a\in A\Gamma
such that
v=o(a)
, by definition
X^{\prime}_{a}=\{b\in o^{-1}(v^{*})\mid\pi(b)=a\}
. The definition of
\mathbf{T}
then provides a bijection
\mathcal{L}_{v^{*},a}
from
X^{\prime}_{a}
to
X_{a}
. We can thus construct a bijection
\theta_{v}
from
X^{\prime}_{v}
to
X_{v}
by setting
\theta_{v}(c)=\mathcal{L}_{v^{*},a}(c)
. By definition,
H(v)
is just the closure of the group of permutations induced by
H_{v^{*}}
on
o^{-1}(v^{*})
. As previously observed, we obtain in this way the set of all permutations
h
of
o^{-1}(v^{*})
such that the permutation induced by
\mathcal{L}h\mathcal{L}|_{o^{-1}(v^{*})}
on
X_{v}
is an element of
G(v)
. Given
c\in X_{v}
, we see from the definition of
\theta_{v}
that

\mathcal{L}h\mathcal{L}|^{-1}_{o^{-1}(v^{*})}(c)=\theta_{v}h\theta^{-1}_{v}(c).

Thus
\theta_{v}H(v)\theta^{-1}_{v}=G(v)
, completing the proof that
(\theta,(\theta_{v}))
is an isomorphism of local action diagrams from
\Delta^{\prime}
to
\Delta
.

Finally, let
g
be an element of the
(\mathrm{P})
-closure of
H
. Then for every finite subset
Y
of
o^{-1}(v)
, there is some
h\in H
such that
gv=hv
and
gy=hy
for all
y\in Y
. In other words,
h
is such that
\sigma_{\mathcal{L},v}(h)(c)=\sigma_{\mathcal{L},v}(g)(c)
for all
c\in\mathcal{L}(Y)
; note that by definition,
\sigma_{\mathcal{L},v}(h)\in G(\pi(v))
. Since
Y
can be any finite subset of
o^{-1}(v)
and
G(\pi(v))
is closed, it follows that
\sigma_{\mathcal{L},v}(g)\in G(\pi(v))
for all
v\in VT
, and hence
g\in H
. Thus
H
is
(\mathrm{P})
-closed.
∎

Theorem 3.10
.

Let
T
be a tree, let
G\leq\mathrm{Aut}(T)
, let
\Delta=\Delta(T,G)
and let
\mathbf{T}
be an associated
\Delta
-tree structure on
T
. Then
\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{T},(G(v)))
is the
(\mathrm{P})
-closure of
G
.

Proof.

By Theorem
3.9
, the set
H=\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{T},(G(v)))
is a
(\mathrm{P})
-closed subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
. Let
V^{*}
be the set of vertices of
T
used to define the
\Delta
-tree structure, and let
v\in VT
. Then there is some
w\in V^{*}
such that
\pi(v)=\pi(w)
. In particular,
v
and
w
lie in the same
G
-orbit, and given the way in which
\mathbf{T}
is constructed, there is some
g_{v}\in G
such that
g_{v}v=w
and the colouring for
b\in o^{-1}(v)
is given by
\mathcal{L}(b)=g_{v}b
.

Let
g\in G
. Then
\pi(v)=\pi(gv)=\pi(w)
, so
g_{gv}(gv)=w
where
g_{gv}
is as in the definition of the colouring. Let
b\in o^{-1}(v)
and let
c=\mathcal{L}(b)
. Then
c=g_{v}b
and
\mathcal{L}(gb)=g_{gv}gb=(g_{gv}gg^{-1}_{v})c
, so

\sigma_{\mathcal{L},v}(g)(c)=(g_{gv}gg^{-1}_{v})c.

We see that
(g_{gv}gg^{-1}_{v})w=w
, so
g_{gv}gg^{-1}_{v}\in G_{w}
. Thus by definition, the permutation induced by
g_{gv}gg^{-1}_{v}
on
w
is an element of
G(\pi(w))=G(\pi(v))
, that is,
\sigma_{\mathcal{L},v}(g)\in G(\pi(v))
. Since this holds for all
v\in VT
, we see that
g\in H
. Thus
G\leq H
; since
H
is
(\mathrm{P})
-closed, in fact
G^{(\mathrm{P})}\leq H
.

Conversely, let
h\in H
. Then by the definition of
H
,

\sigma_{\mathcal{L},v}(h)\in G(\pi(v)).

Given the definition of
G(\pi(v))
, for every finite subset
X
of
o^{-1}(w)
there is
g^{\prime}_{X}\in G_{w}
such that for all
c\in X
,

g^{\prime}_{X}c=\sigma_{\mathcal{L},v}(h)(c).

Since
h\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\pi}(T)
we have
\pi(hv)=\pi(v)
, so we can write
g_{hv}(hv)=w
.
Given a finite subset
X
of
o^{-1}(v)
and
b\in X
, we have
\mathcal{L}(hb)=\sigma_{\mathcal{L},v}(h)(g_{v}b)
and also
\mathcal{L}(hb)=g_{hv}hb
. Thus

hb=g^{-1}_{hv}\sigma_{\mathcal{L},v}(h)(g_{v}b)=g^{-1}_{hv}g^{\prime}_{g_{v}X}g_{v}b.

Thus on the set
X
, we see that
h
agrees with the element
g^{-1}_{hv}g^{\prime}_{g_{v}X}g_{v}
of
G
. Since this can be achieved at every vertex
v\in VT
and for every finite subset
X
of
o^{-1}(v)
, we conclude that
h\in G^{(\mathrm{P})}
. This proves that
G^{(\mathrm{P})}=H
as required.
∎

Since the local action diagram can be recovered from the group
\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{T},(G(v)))
, we have the following corollary.

Corollary 3.11
.

Let
T
be a tree, let
G\leq\mathrm{Aut}(T)
and let
\Delta
be an associated local action diagram. Then
\Delta
is also an associated local action diagram for the action of
G^{(\mathrm{P})}
on
T
.

We have now shown that the
(\mathrm{P})
-closed subgroups of
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
are exactly the groups realisable as a group
\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{T},(G(v)))
definable from some local action diagram. It remains to show that each local action diagram
\Delta
gives rise to only one group
\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{T},(G(v)))
up to tree isomorphisms, in other words, the choices made in defining the
\Delta
-tree
\mathbf{T}
are not significant.

Theorem 3.12
.

Let
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(G(v)))
be a local action diagram and let
\mathbf{T}=(T,\pi,\mathcal{L})
and
\mathbf{T^{\prime}}=(T^{\prime},\pi^{\prime},\mathcal{L}^{\prime})
be
\Delta
-trees. Then there is a graph isomorphism
\phi:T\rightarrow T^{\prime}
such that
\phi\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{T},(G(v)))\phi^{-1}=\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{T}^{\prime},(G(v)))
.

Proof.

Let
G=\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{T},(G(v)))
and
H=\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{T}^{\prime},(G(v)))
. By Lemma
3.5
, there is a graph isomorphism
\alpha:T\rightarrow T^{\prime}
such that
\pi^{\prime}\circ\alpha=\pi
. Thus by applying
\alpha
to
\mathbf{T}
and replacing
G
with
\alpha G\alpha^{-1}
, we may assume that
T=T^{\prime}
and
\pi=\pi^{\prime}
, in other words,
G
and
H
act on the same tree with the same orbits, in such a way that the quotient graph can be naturally identified with
\Gamma
.

By Theorem
3.9
, for every pair of vertices
v,w\in VT
such that
\pi(v)=\pi(w)
, we have

\sigma_{\mathcal{L},v}(G_{v})=G(\pi(v))=\sigma_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime},w}(H_{w}).

Let
G^{*}_{v}
and
H^{*}_{w}
be the permutation groups induced by
G_{v}
on
o^{-1}(v)
and
H_{w}
on
o^{-1}(w)
respectively. We see that
G^{*}_{v}
and
H^{*}_{w}
are both isomorphic to
G(\pi(v))
as permutation groups; moreover, since
\pi=\pi^{\prime}
, given any element
g\in G
such that
gv=w
, then the groups
gG^{*}_{v}g^{-1}
and
H^{*}_{w}
also have the same orbits on
o^{-1}(w)
, with the same correspondence between orbits on
o^{-1}(w)
and elements of
o^{-1}(\pi(v))
. There is thus a bijection
\rho_{v,w}
from
o^{-1}(v)
to
o^{-1}(w)
such that
\pi(\rho_{v,w}(a))=\pi(a)
for all
a\in o^{-1}(v)
and such that
\rho_{v,w}G^{*}_{v}\rho^{-1}_{v,w}=H^{*}_{w}
. Since
G^{*}_{v}
is transitive on each
\pi
-fibre in
o^{-1}(v)
, for a single given
a\in o^{-1}(v)
, we are free to choose
\rho_{v,w}(a)
from the set
\{b\in o^{-1}(w)\mid\pi(b)=\pi(a)\}
.

We now aim to construct
\phi\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\pi}(T)
such that

\phi\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{T},(G(v)))\phi^{-1}=\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{T}^{\prime},(G(v))).

We construct
\phi
successively on balls of radius
n
centred on some vertex
v_{0}\in VT
, starting with
\phi(v_{0})=v_{0}
, such that at each stage
\phi
is a graph automorphism on
B_{n}(v_{0})
that commutes with
\pi
. To define
\phi
on
B_{1}(v_{0})
, we let it act as
\rho_{v_{0},v_{0}}
on
o^{-1}(v_{0})
, and then extend to the remaining arcs and vertices in
B_{1}(v_{0})
via the equations
\phi(\overline{a})=\overline{\phi(a)}
and
\phi(t(a))=t(\phi(a))
. Now suppose we have defined
\phi
on
B_{n}(v_{0})
for some
n\geq 1
, and let
v\in S_{n}(v_{0})
; write
\phi_{n}
for the automorphism of
B_{n}(v_{0})
. Let
a
be the unique arc in
o^{-1}(v)
in the direction of
v_{0}
. Then we have already specified
\phi(a)
, and it has been chosen in such a way that
\pi(\overline{\phi(a)})=\pi(\overline{a})
; hence it is also the case that
\pi(\phi(a))=\pi(a)
. We can thus choose
\rho_{v,\phi(v)}
so that
\rho_{v,\phi(v)}(a)=\phi(a)
. There is then a unique isomorphism
\phi_{v}
from
B_{1}(v)
to
B_{1}(\phi(v))
that is compatible with both
\phi_{n}
and with
\rho_{v,v_{0}}
. We then define
\phi(e)
for
e
a vertex or arc of
B_{n+1}(v_{0})
to be
\phi_{n}(e)
or
\phi_{v}(e)
as applicable, and observe that we have produced a graph automorphism of
B_{n+1}(v_{0})
that commutes with
\phi
. By induction, we produce
\phi\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\pi}(T)
.

Now let
v\in VT
, write
w=\phi(v)
and consider
\phi G_{v}\phi^{-1}
. The construction of
\phi
was such that the permutation group induced by
\phi G_{v}\phi^{-1}
on
o^{-1}(w)
is
\rho_{v,w}G_{v}\rho^{-1}_{v,w}
, or in other words, it is the same permutation group as the one induced by
H_{w}
on
o^{-1}(w)
. Thus
\sigma_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime},w}(\phi G_{v}\phi^{-1})=G(\pi(w))
. By varying
v
so that
w
ranges over
VT
, we see (from the definition of
H=\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{T}^{\prime},(G(v)))
) that
\phi G\phi^{-1}\leq H
. On the other hand, we see by a similar argument that
\phi^{-1}H\phi\leq G
, so
\phi G\phi^{-1}\geq H
. So in fact
\phi G\phi^{-1}=H
, as required.
∎

We can thus define
the
universal group of a local action diagram:
\mathbf{U}(\Delta)
is defined as
\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{T},(G(v)))
where
\mathbf{T}
is some
\Delta
-tree. Then
\mathbf{U}(\Delta)
is defined up to isomorphisms of the tree on which it acts; we write
\mathbf{U}_{\mathbf{T}}(\Delta)
if we want to impose a specific action on a specific tree.

To conclude the section, we now prove the correspondence theorem.

Proof of Theorem
3.3
.

Given a local action diagram
\Delta
, we have an associated pair
(T,\mathbf{U}(\Delta))
, and by Theorem
3.12
the pair
(T,\mathbf{U}(\Delta))
is specified uniquely up to isomorphisms; on the other hand, it is clear from the construction that if
\Delta
and
\Delta^{\prime}
are isomorphic as local action diagrams, then they will produce isomorphic pairs
(T,\mathbf{U}(\Delta))
and
(T^{\prime},\mathbf{U}(\Delta^{\prime}))
.
Thus we have a well-defined mapping
\beta
from isomorphism classes of local action diagram to isomorphism classes of pairs
(T,G)
where
T
is a tree and
G\leq\mathrm{Aut}(T)
is
(\mathrm{P})
-closed on
T
.
Theorem
3.10
shows that
\beta
is surjective and Theorem
3.9
shows that
\beta
is injective. Thus we have a natural one-to-one correspondence as claimed.
∎

4
(\mathrm{P})
-closed subgroups of
(\mathrm{P})
-closed groups

Let
G
be a
(\mathrm{P})
-closed group of automorphisms of the tree
T
. In this section, we identify some sufficient conditions for subgroups of
G
to be
(\mathrm{P})
-closed, and describe some structural properties of these subgroups that will be useful later.

4.1
Local subaction diagrams

In this subsection we give a general description of
(\mathrm{P})
-closed subgroups of a
(\mathrm{P})
-closed group based on the respective local action diagrams.

Definition 4.1
.

Suppose
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(G(v)))
is a local action diagram. A
local subaction diagram
of
\Delta
is a local action diagram
\Lambda=(\hat{\Gamma},(\hat{X}_{\hat{a}}),(H(\hat{v})))
admitting a locally surjective graph homomorphism
p:\hat{\Gamma}\rightarrow\Gamma
, and for each
\hat{v}\in\hat{\Gamma}
a bijection
\theta_{\hat{v}}:\hat{X}_{\hat{v}}\rightarrow X_{p(\hat{v})}
, satisfying the following conditions:

(i)

For each vertex
\hat{v}
of
\hat{\Gamma}
, then
\theta_{\hat{v}}H(\hat{v})\theta^{-1}_{\hat{v}}
is a closed subgroup of
G(p(\hat{v}))
.

(ii)

For each arc
a
of
\Gamma
and each vertex
\hat{v}
of
\hat{\Gamma}
such that
p(\hat{v})=o(a)
, then
\theta_{\hat{v}}
restricts to a bijection

\theta_{\hat{v},a}:\bigsqcup_{\hat{a}\in o^{-1}(\hat{v}),p(\hat{a})=a}\hat{X}_{\hat{a}}\rightarrow X_{a}.

If
\Lambda
is a local subaction diagram of
\Delta
with associated graph homomorphism
p:\hat{\Gamma}\rightarrow\Gamma
, then we write
\Lambda\leq_{p}\Delta
. We combine the bijections
\theta_{\hat{v}}
into a map

\theta_{p}:\bigsqcup_{\hat{v}\in V\hat{\Gamma}}\hat{X}_{\hat{v}}\rightarrow\bigsqcup_{v\in V\Gamma}X_{v};\;\text{ for }x\in\hat{X}_{\hat{v}},\;\theta_{p}(x):=\theta_{\hat{v}}(x).

In general
\theta_{p}
is surjective but not necessarily injective.

Proposition 4.2
.

Suppose that
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(G(v)))
is a local action diagram and
\Lambda=(\hat{\Gamma},(X_{\hat{a}}),(H(\hat{v})))
is a local subaction diagram of
\Delta
, with
\Lambda\leq_{p}\Delta
. If
\mathbf{\hat{T}}=(T,\mathcal{L},\hat{\pi})
is a
\Lambda
-tree, then
\mathbf{T}=(T,\theta_{p}\circ\mathcal{L},p\circ\hat{\pi})
is a
\Delta
-tree and
\mathrm{Aut}_{\hat{\pi}}(T)\leq\mathrm{Aut}_{p\circ\hat{\pi}}(T)
. Moreover,
\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{\hat{T}},(H(\hat{v})))\leq\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{T},(G(v)))
.

Proof.

The
\Lambda
-tree
\mathbf{\hat{T}}
is a tree
T
together with a surjective graph homomorphism
\hat{\pi}:T\rightarrow\hat{\Gamma}
and a
\Lambda
-colouring
\mathcal{L}:AT\rightarrow\bigsqcup_{\hat{a}\in A\hat{\Gamma}}X_{\hat{a}}
. For each vertex
v\in VT
and each arc
\hat{a}\in o^{-1}(\hat{\pi}(v))
, the map
\mathcal{L}
restricts to a bijection
\mathcal{L}_{v,\hat{a}}
from
\{b\in o^{-1}(v)\mid\hat{\pi}(b)=\hat{a}\}
to
\hat{X}_{\hat{a}}
(where here
o^{-1}
has codomain
AT
).

Define
\pi:=p\circ\hat{\pi}
, and note that
\pi:T\rightarrow\Gamma
is a surjective graph homomorphism. Now we see that
\theta_{p}\circ\mathcal{L}
is a map from
AT
to
\bigsqcup_{a\in A\Gamma}X_{a}
. Let us now consider the restriction of
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}:=\theta_{p}\circ\mathcal{L}
to
o^{-1}(v)
for some
v\in VT
. Then
\hat{\pi}(v)
is some vertex
\hat{v}\in V\hat{\Gamma}
and
\mathcal{L}
restricts to a bijection from
o^{-1}(v)
to
\hat{X}_{\hat{v}}
; in turn,
\theta_{p}
restricts to
\theta_{\hat{v}}
on
\hat{X}_{\hat{v}}
. Thus
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
restricts to a bijection from
o^{-1}(v)
to
X_{p(\hat{v})}=X_{\pi(v)}
. Now fix
a\in o^{-1}(\pi(v))
; given
b\in o^{-1}(v)
, consider when
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(b)\in X_{a}
. By the second condition in Definition
4.1
, this occurs if and only if
\mathcal{L}(b)\in\hat{X}_{\hat{b}}
for some
\hat{b}\in o^{-1}(\hat{v})
such that
p(\hat{b})=a
. By the definition of a
\Lambda
-colouring, this is equivalent to the condition that
p\circ\hat{\pi}(b)=a
, in other words,
\pi(b)=a
. Thus
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
restricts to a bijection

\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{v,a}:\{b\in o^{-1}(v)\mid\pi(b)=a\}\rightarrow X_{a}.

Thus
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
is a
\Delta
-colouring and hence
\mathbf{T}
is a
\Delta
-tree. For
\theta\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\hat{\pi}}(T)
we have
\theta\in\mathrm{Aut}(T)
and
\hat{\pi}\circ\theta=\hat{\pi}
. Hence
(p\circ\hat{\pi})\circ\theta=p\circ\hat{\pi}
, so
\theta\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\pi}(T)
.

Fix
g\in\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{\hat{T}},(H(v)))
; note that
g\in\mathrm{Aut}_{\hat{\pi}}(T)\leq\mathrm{Aut}_{\pi}(T)
. Given
v\in VT
, then
g
has local actions with respect to
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
at
v
, namely

\sigma_{\mathcal{L},v}(g)\in\mathrm{Sym}(\hat{X}_{\hat{\pi}(v)})
and
\sigma_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime},v}(g)\in\mathrm{Sym}(X_{\pi(v)})
; moreover
\sigma_{\mathcal{L},v}(g)\in H(\hat{\pi}(v))
. From the construction of
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
from
\mathcal{L}
, we see that in fact

\sigma_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime},v}(g)\theta_{\hat{\pi}(v)}=\theta_{\hat{\pi}(v)}\sigma_{\mathcal{L},v}(g).

The first condition of Definition
4.1
now ensures that
\sigma_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime},v}(g)\in G(\pi(v))
. Hence
g\in\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{T},(G(v)))
, showing that
\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{\hat{T}},(H(v)))\leq\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{T},(G(v)))
.
∎

In light of Theorem
3.12
and the definition of
\mathbf{U}(\Delta)
, we have the following immediate corollary.

Corollary 4.3
.

If
\Delta
is a local action diagram with local subaction diagram
\Lambda
, then
\mathbf{U}(\Lambda)
occurs as a subgroup of
\mathbf{U}(\Delta)
. ∎

Conversely, every
(\mathrm{P})
-closed subgroup of a
(\mathrm{P})
-closed group has an associated local subaction diagram.

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
T
be a tree, let
G
be a
(\mathrm{P})
-closed subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
, and let
H\leq G
such that
H=H^{(\mathrm{P})}
. Let
\Delta=\Delta(T,G)
and
\Lambda=\Delta(T,H)
. Then
\Lambda
is a local subaction diagram of
\Delta
.

Proof.

Note that
G
and
H
both have closed local actions by Lemma
2.25
. Write
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(G(v)))
and
\Lambda=(\hat{\Gamma},(\hat{X}_{\hat{a}}),(H(\hat{v})))
, with associated quotient maps
\pi:T\rightarrow\Gamma
and
\hat{\pi}:T\rightarrow\hat{\Gamma}
respectively. Suppose we have defined
\Delta
using the representative vertices
V^{*}\subseteq VT
(one vertex for each
G
-orbit on
VT
), and we have defined
\Lambda
using the representative vertices
V^{**}\subseteq VT
(one vertex for each
H
-orbit on
VT
). For each
v\in VT
we have chosen
g_{v}\in G
and
h_{v}\in H
such that
g_{v}v\in V^{*}
and
h_{v}v\in V^{**}
. By Lemma
3.7
, the choices we make in the constructions do not change
\Delta
and
\Lambda
up to isomorphism of local action diagrams. Since every
G
-orbit is partitioned into
H
-orbits, we may assume
V^{*}\subseteq V^{**}
without loss of generality.

If
e
is a vertex or arc of
T
, then by definition
\pi(e)=Ge
and
\hat{\pi}(e)=He
. Since
H\leq G
, we can simply set
p(He)=GHe=Ge
without ambiguity. This defines a
locally surjective graph homomorphism from
\hat{\Gamma}
to
\Gamma
, with the property that
p\circ\hat{\pi}=\pi
.

Given
\hat{v}\in V\hat{\Gamma}
, there is a unique
w\in V^{**}
such that
\hat{\pi}(w)=\hat{v}
, and then a unique
w^{\prime}\in V^{*}
in the same
G
-orbit as
w
; in fact
w^{\prime}=g_{w}w
, and we note that
\pi(w^{\prime})=\pi(w)
. The construction of
\Delta
and
\Lambda
is such that
X_{\pi(w^{\prime})}=o^{-1}(w^{\prime})
and
\hat{X}_{\hat{v}}=o^{-1}(w)
. We can thus define the bijection
\theta_{\hat{v}}:\hat{X}_{\hat{v}}\rightarrow X_{p(v)}
by setting
\theta_{\hat{v}}(b)=g_{w}b
.

Let
K
be the subgroup of
\mathrm{Sym}(o^{-1}(w))
induced by the action of
H_{w}
on
o^{-1}(w)=\hat{X}_{\hat{v}}
. Since
\Lambda
is an associated local action diagram, by definition
H(\hat{v})
is the closure of
K
. However,
K
is closed because
H
has closed local actions, so in fact
H(\hat{v})=K
. Thus
\theta_{\hat{v}}H(\hat{v})\theta^{-1}_{\hat{v}}
is the action of
g_{w}H_{w}g^{-1}_{w}
on
o^{-1}(w^{\prime})
; the latter is clearly a closed subgroup of
G_{w^{\prime}}
, so we see that

\theta_{\hat{v}}H(\hat{v})\theta^{-1}_{\hat{v}}

is a closed subgroup of
G(p(\hat{v}))
. Thus condition (i) of Definition
4.1
is satisfied.

For condition (ii), let
\hat{v}
,
w
and
w^{\prime}
be as before and let
a\in A\Gamma
be such that
p(\hat{v})=o(a)
. Then
a=Gb
for some
b\in o^{-1}(w)
. Given
c\in\hat{X}_{\hat{v}}
, then
c\in\hat{X}_{\hat{a}}
for some
\hat{a}\in o^{-1}(\hat{v})\subseteq A\hat{\Gamma}
; we can write
\hat{X}_{\hat{a}}=H_{w}b^{\prime}
for some
b^{\prime}\in o^{-1}(w)
. If
p(\hat{a})=a
then
Gb^{\prime}=Gb
, so
g_{w}c\in Gb\cap o^{-1}(w^{\prime})=X_{a}
. If
p(\hat{a})\neq a
then
Gb^{\prime}
is disjoint from
Gb
and hence
g_{w}c
is not in
X_{a}
. Thus
\theta_{\hat{v}}
restricts to a bijection
\theta_{\hat{v},a}
as required for condition (ii) of Definition
4.1
. We conclude that
\Lambda
is a local subaction diagram of
\Delta
as required.
∎

Remark 4.5
.

As a description of
(\mathrm{P})
-closed subgroups of
(\mathrm{P})
-closed groups, the results we have just obtained fall short of a classification in two respects.

(i)

There is no obvious recipe to construct all the local subaction diagrams
\Lambda=(\hat{\Gamma},(X_{\hat{a}}),(H(\hat{v})))
of a given local action diagram
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(G(v)))
. The case
\Gamma=\hat{\Gamma}
is relatively straightforward:
\Lambda
is then determined by choosing the tuple
(H(v))_{v\in V\Gamma}
, where
H(v)
is a closed subgroup of
G(v)
acting transitively on each
G(v)
-orbit, and the entries of
(H(v))_{v\in V\Gamma}
can be chosen independently of one another. However as soon as we move away from this case, there are dependencies between the choices of local actions
H(\hat{v})
. For example, even if we assume
V\Gamma=V\hat{\Gamma}
, then for each arc
a\in A\Gamma
, the number of orbits of
H(o(a))
on
X_{a}
must be the same as the number of orbits of
H(t(a))
on
X_{\overline{a}}
.

(ii)

Generally speaking, when classifying subgroups of a group of a special kind, one seeks a classification up to conjugacy. In other words, given a
(\mathrm{P})
-closed action
(T,G)
on a tree
T
, we would want a classification of
(\mathrm{P})
-closed subgroups of
G
up to conjugacy in
G
. Local subaction diagrams of
\Delta(T,G)
only yield a classification of
(\mathrm{P})
-closed subgroups of
G
up to conjugacy in
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
, which is a coarser classification in general.

In the rest of this section, we will focus on some special classes of
(\mathrm{P})
-closed subgroups
H
of a
(\mathrm{P})
-closed group
G
, especially those constructed from open subgroups of
G
, which will lead to proofs of Theorems
1.11
and
1.13
from the introduction.

4.2
Sufficient conditions for a subgroup to be
(\mathrm{P})
-closed

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
T
be a tree, let
G
be a
(\mathrm{P})
-closed subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
, and let
H\leq G
. Suppose that
H
contains an arc stabiliser of
G
. Then
H
is
(\mathrm{P})
-closed.

Proof.

Suppose that
H
contains the arc stabiliser
G_{(x,y)}
of
G
.
Note that
G
must be a closed subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
by Proposition
2.14
. Since
H
is an open subgroup of
G
, it is also a closed subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
. Thus, by Theorem
2.18
, it suffices for us to show that
H
has property
(\mathrm{P})
with respect to the edges of
T
.

Let
(v,w)\in AT
; we claim that
H
has
(\mathrm{P})
with respect to
(v,w)
. If
(v,w)
is equal to
(x,y)
or its reverse, then
H_{(v,w)}=G_{(x,y)}
and the claim is clear, so we may assume
(v,w)\not\in\{(x,y),(y,x)\}
. Then there is a simple path that passes through
x
and
y
in some order, and then later through
v
and
w
in some order; without loss of generality, let us say that the path passes through these points in the order
x,y,v,w
.

Since
G
has property
(\mathrm{P})
, we have
G_{(v,w)}=\mathrm{rist}_{G}(T_{(v,w)})\times\mathrm{rist}_{G}(T_{(w,v)})
. Note that
(x,y)
is outside
T_{(v,w)}
, and hence

\mathrm{rist}_{G}(T_{(v,w)})\leq G_{(x,y)}\leq H.

For
h\in H_{(v,w)}
we can write
h=k_{1}k_{2}
with
k_{1}\in\mathrm{rist}_{G}(T_{(v,w)})
and
k_{2}\in\mathrm{rist}_{G}(T_{(w,v)})
. Then
k_{1}\in H
, so also
k_{2}\in H
. The arc stabiliser
H_{(v,w)}
therefore decomposes as

H_{(v,w)}=\mathrm{rist}_{H}(T_{(v,w)})\times\mathrm{rist}_{H}(T_{(w,v)}).

Thus
H
satisfies property
(\mathrm{P})
with respect to all edges in
T
.
∎

Every vertex stabiliser contains the stabilisers of the arcs incident with that vertex, so the following is a special case of Lemma
4.6
.

Corollary 4.7
.

Let
G
be a
(\mathrm{P})
-closed subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
, and let
H\leq G
. Suppose that
H
contains a vertex stabiliser of
G
. Then
H
is
(\mathrm{P})
-closed.

4.3
Vertex stabilisers and
(\mathrm{P})
-closure

Given a
(\mathrm{P})
-closed action of a group
G
on a tree
T
, then every vertex stabiliser of
G
itself has
(\mathrm{P})
-closed action, by Corollary
4.7
. In turn,
(\mathrm{P})
-closed subgroups of
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
that fix a vertex have a special structure. This generalises the observation
[
19
, Proposition 15]
that the box product of two permutation groups
M
and
N
contains isomorphic copies of
M
and
N
as subgroups.

Proposition 4.8
.

Let
G
be a group acting on a tree
T
with property
(\mathrm{P})
, let
\epsilon\in VT
and let
B_{r}
be the closed ball of radius
r
around
\epsilon
. Let
G_{r}
be the pointwise stabiliser of
B_{r}
in
G
. Then there is an increasing sequence

(H_{r})_{r\geq 1}

of subgroups of
G_{0}
, each closed in
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
, such that
G_{0}=G_{r}\rtimes H_{r}
as topological groups for all
r\geq 1
. In particular, there is a permutational isomorphism between the action of
H_{1}
on
o^{-1}(\epsilon)
and the corresponding vertex group
G(\pi(\epsilon))
of the local action diagram of
G
.

Proof.

Let
\Gamma=G\backslash T
, let
\pi=\pi_{(T,G)}
and let
v_{0}=\pi(\epsilon)
. Form the local action diagram
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(G(v)))
for
(T,G)
. If we replace
G
with
G_{0}=G_{\epsilon}
, then it will not affect the permutational isomorphism type of
G(v_{0})
, nor the structure of
G_{0}
, so let us assume that
G=G_{0}
.
Since
G
fixes
\epsilon
, any local action of
G
at a vertex
w\in VT\smallsetminus\{\epsilon\}
must fix pointwise the unique edge incident with
w
contained in
[v_{0},w]
. Thus in any colouring
\mathcal{L}
such that
\mathbf{T}=(T,\pi,\mathcal{L})
is a
\Delta
-tree, if
a\in AT
such that
d(t(a),\epsilon)<d(o(a),\epsilon)
, then
|X_{\pi(a)}|=1
, so
\mathcal{L}(a)
must be the unique element of
X_{\pi(a)}
. Fix such a colouring
\mathcal{L}
and for
r\geq 1
, set

H_{r}=\{g\in G_{0}\mid\forall w\in VT:d(w,\epsilon)\geq r\Rightarrow\sigma_{\mathcal{L},w}(g)=1\}.

Given that
G_{0}
preserves distance from
\epsilon
, it is easy to see that
H_{r}
is a subgroup of
G_{0}
;
H_{r}\cap G_{r}=\{1\}
; and
H_{r}\leq H_{r^{\prime}}
whenever
r\leq r^{\prime}
. It is also clear that
H_{r}
is determined as a subgroup of
G_{0}
by its orbits on arcs, so
H_{r}
is closed in
G_{0}
; since
G_{0}
is closed in
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
, it follows that
H_{r}
is closed in
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
. To see that
G_{0}=G_{r}H_{r}
, consider some
r\geq 1
, an element
h\in G_{0}
, and a vertex
w\in VT
with
d(\epsilon,w)=r
.
Then
h
maps the arc in
o^{-1}(w)
directed towards
\epsilon
to the arc in
o^{-1}(hw)
directed towards
\epsilon
. This corresponds to a fixed point for the permutation
\sigma_{\mathcal{L},w}(h)\in G(\pi(w))
, so the action of
h
on
B_{r}
is compatible with having trivial local action at
r
. Hence there is
g\in\mathrm{Aut}(T)
that has the same action as
h
on
B_{r}
, but has trivial local action for every vertex
w\in VT
such that
d(w,\epsilon)\geq r
.
We then see that in fact
g\in H_{r}
and
h\in G_{r}g
, showing that
h\in G_{r}H_{r}
. In particular, it is now clear that
\sigma_{\mathcal{L},\epsilon}
restricts to a permutational isomorphism from
H_{1}
acting on
o^{-1}(\epsilon)
to
G(v_{0})
acting on
X_{v_{0}}
.

Finally, we show that as a topological group,
G_{0}
splits as the semidirect product
G_{r}\rtimes H_{r}
. Certainly
G_{0}
splits this way abstractly, since
G_{r}
is a closed normal subgroup that has trivial intersection with
H_{r}
. To show that the permutation topology of
G_{0}
is the product of the permutation topologies on
G_{r}
and
H_{r}
, it suffices to show for every vertex
w\in VT
that the vertex stabiliser
K=G_{w}
satisfies
K=(G_{r}\cap K)(H_{r}\cap K)
. Let
(a_{1},\dots,a_{t})
be the directed path of arcs from
\epsilon
to
w
. Given
g\in G
, by assumption we have
g\in G_{0}
, and thus we observe that
g\in K
if and only if
g
fixes
a_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq t
, which occurs if and only if
\sigma_{\mathcal{L},o(a_{i})}(g)
fixes
\mathcal{L}(a_{i})
for
1\leq i\leq t
. On the other hand, if
g=g_{r}h_{r}
for
g_{r}\in G_{r}
and
h_{r}\in H_{r}
, then

\sigma_{\mathcal{L},o(a_{i})}(g)=\begin{cases}\sigma_{\mathcal{L},o(a_{i})}(h_{r})&\mbox{if}\;i\leq r\\
\sigma_{\mathcal{L},h_{r}o(a_{i})}(g_{r})&\mbox{if}\;i>r.\end{cases}

From this, we see that
g
fixes
w
if and only if both
g_{r}
and
h_{r}
fix
w
. Thus
K=(G_{r}\cap K)(H_{r}\cap K)
as desired.
∎

We note that for an arbitrary group action
(T,G)
on a tree, the operation of taking the
(\mathrm{P})
-closure behaves well with respect to the subgroup generated by the arc stabilisers.

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
G
be a group acting on a tree
T
. Then

(G^{(\mathrm{P})})^{+}=(G^{+})^{(\mathrm{P})}.

Proof.

We see that
(G^{+})^{(\mathrm{P})}
has the same orbits on arcs as
G^{+}
, so
(G^{+})^{(\mathrm{P})}=G^{+}(G^{+})^{(\mathrm{P})}_{a}
for any
a\in AT
. In turn, it is clear that
G^{+}\leq(G^{(\mathrm{P})})^{+}
and
(G^{+})^{(\mathrm{P})}_{a}\leq(G^{(\mathrm{P})})_{a}\leq(G^{(\mathrm{P})})^{+}
, Thus
(G^{(\mathrm{P})})^{+}\geq(G^{+})^{(\mathrm{P})}
.

It remains to show that
(G^{(\mathrm{P})})^{+}\leq(G^{+})^{(\mathrm{P})}
. In fact it suffices to show that
(G^{(\mathrm{P})})_{a}\leq(G^{+})^{(\mathrm{P})}
for
a\in AT
. In other words we wish to show, for all
a\in AT
,
v\in VT
,
F
finite subsets of
o^{-1}(v)
and
g\in(G^{(\mathrm{P})})_{a}
, there exists
h\in G^{+}
such that
hg
fixes
F
pointwise. For this discussion we fix
a
and
g
; we will proceed by induction on
d=d(v,o(a))
.

In the base case,
d=0
, in other words
v=o(a)
. Since
g\in G^{(\mathrm{P})}
, for any finite
F\subseteq o^{-1}(v)
there is
h\in G
such that
hg
fixes
F
pointwise; since
g
fixes
a
, in fact
h\in G_{a}\leq G^{+}
. Thus,
g\in(G^{+})^{(\mathrm{P})}
.

From now on, we may assume
d>0
. Let
b
be the arc in
o^{-1}(v)
pointing towards
o(a)
. Then by induction there is
h^{\prime}\in G^{+}
such that
h^{\prime}g
fixes
\overline{b}\in o^{-1}(t(b))
, and hence fixes
b
. In turn for any finite
F\subseteq o^{-1}(v)
there is
h\in G
such that
h(h^{\prime}g)
fixes
F\cup\{b\}
pointwise. Since
h^{\prime}g
fixes
b
, in fact
h\in G_{b}\leq G^{+}
, and hence
hh^{\prime}\in G^{+}
. Thus,
g\in(G^{+})^{(\mathrm{P})}
. This completes the inductive step and hence completes the proof.
∎

4.4
The local action of
G^{+}

Given a tree
T
and a
(\mathrm{P})
-closed group of automorphisms
G
, we can describe both the action of
G^{+}
on
T
and the action of
G
on
G^{+}\backslash T
using local action diagrams derived from the local action diagram of
G
.

Definition 4.10
.

Given a local action diagram
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(G(v)))
and
v\in V\Gamma
, write
G(v)^{+}
for the subgroup of
G(v)
generated by the point stabilisers of its action on
X_{v}
.

We use the relationship between the groups
G(v)
and
G(v)^{+}
to define two more local action diagrams, which will reappear later.

The
reduced local action diagram
of
\Delta
is
\Delta^{*}=(\Gamma^{*},(X^{*}_{a}),(G^{*}(v)))
, where
\Gamma^{*}=\Gamma
;
G^{*}(v)=G(v)/G(v)^{+}
; and
X^{*}_{a}
is the
G^{*}(v)
-set formed by the set of
G(v)^{+}
-orbits on
X_{a}
.
Note that since
G(v)^{+}
is normal and contains every point stabiliser of the action on
X_{a}
, actually
X^{*}_{a}
is a regular
G^{*}(v)
-set. In particular, each group
G^{*}(v)
is closed in the permutation topology.

The
plus local subaction diagram
of
\Delta
is
\Delta^{+}=(\Gamma^{+},(X^{+}_{a}),(G^{+}(v)))
, constructed as follows. We take
\Gamma^{+}
to be a
\Delta^{*}
-tree, which is then equipped with a
locally surjective graph quotient map
p:\Gamma^{+}\rightarrow\Gamma
and a colouring
\mathcal{L}^{*}
. Given
v\in V\Gamma^{+}
, we can restrict
\mathcal{L}^{*}
to a bijection

\mathcal{L}^{*}_{v}:o^{-1}(v)\rightarrow\bigsqcup_{b\in o^{-1}(p(v))}X^{*}_{b}.

An arc
a\in A\Gamma^{+}
has a colour (when viewed as an arc in the
\Delta^{*}
-tree
\Gamma^{+}
), and this colour is an element of
X^{*}_{p(a)}
, which is in particular an orbit
Y_{a}
of
G(p(v))^{+}
in its original action on
X_{p(v)}
. We can thus take the colour set
X^{+}_{a}
of
a
in
\Delta^{+}
to be a set in bijection with
Y_{a}
, extending to a bijection
\theta_{v}
from
X^{+}_{v}:=\bigsqcup_{a\in o^{-1}(v)}X^{+}_{a}
to
X_{p(v)}
, and then let
G^{+}(v)
be the group of permutations of
X^{+}_{v}
such that
\theta_{v}G^{+}(v)\theta^{-1}_{v}=G(p(v))^{+}
. As in Definition
4.1
, we combine the bijections
\theta_{v}
into a surjection

\theta_{p}:\bigsqcup_{v\in V\Gamma^{+}}X^{+}_{v}\rightarrow\bigsqcup_{w\in V\Gamma}X_{w}.

It is now straightforward to verify the following.

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
\Delta
be a local action diagram. Then
\Delta^{*}
is also a local action diagram, while
\Delta^{+}
is a local subaction diagram of
\Delta
as witnessed by the homomorphism
p
and bijections
\theta_{v}
defined in Definition
4.10
.

We now give the interpretations of the reduced local action diagram and the plus local subaction diagram for the action of
\mathbf{U}(\Delta)
on its
\Delta
-tree.

Theorem 4.12
.

Let
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(G(v)))
be a local action diagram. Then there is a tree
T
, carrying the structures of both a
\Delta
-tree
\mathbf{T}
and a
\Delta^{+}
-tree
\mathbf{T}^{+}
, such that
\mathbf{U}_{\mathbf{T}^{+}}(\Delta^{+})=G^{+}
for
G=\mathbf{U}_{\mathbf{T}}(\Delta)
, while
\Delta^{*}
is the local action diagram of the action of
G/G^{+}
on the tree
G^{+}\backslash T
. Moreover, the action of
G/G^{+}
on
G^{+}\backslash T
has
(\mathrm{P})
; indeed, it acts freely on the arcs of
G^{+}\backslash T
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{T}^{+}=(T,\mathcal{L}^{+},\pi^{+})
be a
\Delta^{+}
-tree. Since
\Delta^{+}
is a local subaction diagram of
\Delta
, we can apply Proposition
4.2
to equip
T
with the structure of a
\Delta
-tree
\mathbf{T}=(T,\mathcal{L},\pi)
, where
\mathcal{L}=\theta_{p}\circ\mathcal{L}^{+}
and
\pi=p\circ\pi^{+}
, so that
H:=\mathbf{U}_{\mathbf{T}^{+}}(\Delta^{+})
is a subgroup of
G:=\mathbf{U}_{\mathbf{T}}(\Delta)
.

We now need to show that
H=G^{+}
. It is enough to prove the following three statements:

(a)

H
is generated by vertex stabilisers.

(b)

The vertex stabilisers of
H
are generated by arc stabilisers. Specifically, given
v\in VT
,

H_{v}=\langle H_{a}\mid a\in o^{-1}(v)\rangle.

(c)

For all
a\in AT
we have
G_{a}\leq H
.

Statement (a) follows from Corollary
2.31
, since
\Gamma^{+}
is a tree by construction. Taking
v\in VT
, then the action of
H_{v}
on
o^{-1}(v)
is isomorphic as a permutation group to
G^{+}(\pi^{+}(v))\cong G(\pi(v))^{+}
, which is generated by point stabilisers. The point stabilisers of the action of
H_{v}
on
o^{-1}(v)
are the arc stabilisers for arcs
a\in o^{-1}(v)
. Statement (b) now follows, since
H_{v}
and
\langle H_{a}\mid a\in o^{-1}(v)\rangle
have the same action on
o^{-1}(v)
and both groups contain
H_{a}
for
a\in o^{-1}(v)
.

Now fix
a\in AT
and consider
G_{a}
. Write
v_{0}=o(a)
; let
B_{r}
be the ball of the radius
r
around
v_{0}
and let
G_{r}
, respectively
H_{r}
, be the pointwise stabiliser of
B_{r}
in
G
, respectively
H
. Since the action of
G_{a}
on
o^{-1}(v_{0})
fixes a point, we see that it acts as a subgroup of
G(\pi(v))^{+}
; we deduce that
\sigma_{\mathcal{L},v_{0}}(H_{a})=\sigma_{\mathcal{L},v_{0}}(G_{a})
, so that
G_{a}=H_{a}G_{1}
. Suppose that
G_{a}=H_{a}G_{r}
for some
r\geq 1
, let
g\in G_{r}
and let
v
be a vertex at distance
r
from
v_{0}
. There is a unique arc
a^{\prime}\in o^{-1}(v)\cap B_{r}
. Since
\sigma_{\mathcal{L},v}(H_{a^{\prime}})=\sigma_{\mathcal{L},v}(G_{a^{\prime}})
, there is an element
h_{v}\in H_{a^{\prime}}
such that
h_{v}
and
g
induce the same action on
o^{-1}(v)
.
By property
(\mathrm{P})
we can take
h_{v}
to lie in the pointwise stabiliser of
T_{a^{\prime}}
. By taking a product of such elements as
v
ranges over the sphere of radius
r
around
v_{0}
, we obtain
h\in H_{r}\leq H_{a}
such that
g\in hG_{r+1}
. Thus,
G_{a}=H_{a}G_{r+1}
. By induction we have
G_{a}=H_{a}G_{r}
for all
r\geq 1
. Since
H_{a}
is closed, we conclude that
G_{a}=H_{a}
, proving (c).

We now consider the tree
G^{+}\backslash T
; since
G^{+}=\mathbf{U}_{\mathbf{T}^{+}}(\Delta^{+})
, we can naturally identify
G^{+}\backslash T
with the graph
\Gamma^{+}
of
\Delta^{+}
; since
G^{+}
is normal in
G
this identification yields an action
\alpha
of
G
on
\Gamma^{+}
. By construction
\Gamma^{+}
has the structure of a
\Delta^{*}
-tree
\mathbf{T}^{*}
equipped with a quotient map
p:\Gamma^{+}\rightarrow\Gamma
and a colouring
\mathcal{L}^{*}
: we claim that
\alpha(G)\leq\mathbf{U}_{\mathbf{T}^{*}}(\Delta^{*})
.
Since
\pi=p\circ\pi^{+}
, we see that for each vertex or arc
e
of
\Gamma^{+}
, then
Ge=p(e)
, so that
G
acts as a subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}_{p}(\Gamma^{+})
with the correct orbits on vertices and arcs. For the local actions, via our identification of
G^{+}\backslash T
with
\Gamma^{+}
and
\mathbf{T}^{*}
, we see that for
v\in VT
, the colouring at the vertex
G^{+}v
restricts to a bijection

\mathcal{L}^{*}_{G^{+}v}:o^{-1}(G^{+}v)\rightarrow\bigsqcup_{b\in o^{-1}(Gv))}X^{*}_{b}.

Given
a\in o^{-1}(v)
, then
\mathcal{L}^{*}(G^{+}a)=X^{+}_{\pi^{+}(a)}
, which is the
G(\pi(v))^{+}
-orbit on
X_{\pi(a)}
that contains
\mathcal{L}(a)
. Consequently, given
g\in G
, then
\sigma_{\mathcal{L}^{*},G^{+}v}(\alpha(g))
is obtained by taking the permutation
\sigma_{\mathcal{L},v}(g)
, which acts on
X_{\pi(v)}
, and reading off its action on the set of
G(\pi(v))^{+}
-orbits on
X_{\pi(v)}
. From this description we see that
\sigma_{\mathcal{L}^{*},G^{+}v}(\alpha(G))=G^{*}(\pi(v))
, and hence
\alpha(G)\leq\mathbf{U}_{\mathbf{T}^{*}}(\Delta^{*})
with
\Delta^{*}
the local action diagram of
\alpha(G)
on
\Gamma^{+}
.

Finally, given an arc
a
of
\Gamma^{+}
, we can write
a=G^{+}b
for
b\in AT
, and then

\alpha(g)a=a\Leftrightarrow gb\in G^{+}b\Leftrightarrow g\in G^{+}G_{b}=G^{+};

thus
\alpha
has kernel
G^{+}
and we have a free action of
G/G^{+}
on
A\Gamma^{+}
. Moreover,
\alpha(G)
can now be identified with the action of
G/G^{+}
on the tree
G^{+}\backslash T
, so this latter action has local action diagram
\Delta^{*}
, and a free action on arcs is easily seen to be closed with property
(\mathrm{P})
.
∎

We note two results arising from Theorem
4.12
and its proof that apply to groups acting on trees with closed local actions, without assuming property
(\mathrm{P})
. (We remind the reader that for a group
G
acting on a tree,
G^{+}
remains the subgroup of
G
generated by arc stabilisers.)

Corollary 4.13
.

Let
T
be a tree and let
G
be a subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
with closed local actions. Then for all
v\in VT
,

(G^{+})_{v}=\langle G_{a}\mid a\in o^{-1}(v)\rangle.

Proof.

Clearly
(G^{+})_{v}
contains
\langle G_{a}\mid a\in o^{-1}(v)\rangle
.
If the action of
(G^{+})_{v}
on
o^{-1}(v)
is generated by point stabilisers (that is,
\langle G_{a}\mid a\in o^{-1}(v)\rangle
and
(G^{+})_{v}
induce the same permutation group on
o^{-1}(v)
), then for any
g\in(G^{+})_{v}
and any
b\in o^{-1}(v)
there is an
h\in\langle G_{a}\mid a\in o^{-1}(v)\rangle
such that
h^{-1}gb=b
, so
h^{-1}g\in G_{b}\leq\langle G_{a}\mid a\in o^{-1}(v)\rangle
, and from this it follows that
(G^{+})_{v}
and
\langle G_{a}\mid a\in o^{-1}(v)\rangle
are equal. Thus it suffices to show that the action of
(G^{+})_{v}
on
o^{-1}(v)
is generated by point stabilisers.

Since
G
has closed local actions, the same is true of
G^{+}
by Lemma
2.25
(iii). Consequently
(G^{+})^{(\mathrm{P})}
has the same local actions as
G^{+}
, so it is enough to show that the action of
((G^{+})^{(\mathrm{P})})_{v}
on
o^{-1}(v)
is generated by point stabilisers, for all vertices
v
. Since
(G^{(\mathrm{P})})^{+}=(G^{+})^{(\mathrm{P})}
by Proposition
4.9
, without loss of generality we can now replace
G
with
G^{(\mathrm{P})}
, and assume that
G
is
(\mathrm{P})
-closed. In other words, by Theorem
3.10
, we can identify
G
with
\mathbf{U}(\Delta)
for some local action diagram
\Delta
. The conclusion is now statement (b) from the proof of Theorem
4.12
, given that we showed
H=G^{+}
in that context.
∎

Corollary 4.14
.

Let
(T,G)
be an action on a tree, with local action diagram
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(G(v)))
. Then
(G^{+}\backslash T,G/G^{+})
is a
(\mathrm{P})
-closed action (indeed, a free action on arcs) which depends only on
(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(G(v)))
; in particular it is unchanged if we replace
G
with
G^{(\mathrm{P})}
. The action
(G^{+}\backslash T,G/G^{+})
admits the following two equivalent descriptions:

(i)

It is the
(\mathrm{P})
-closed action admitting the reduced local action diagram
\Delta^{*}
of
\Delta
as given in Definition
4.10
.

(ii)

It is the fundamental group of a graph of groups over the graph
\Gamma^{i}
, arising as the quotient by the action of
G
on the inversion-free subdivision
T^{i}
of
T
, with the following data. Given
v\in V\Gamma^{i}
, if
v
is the image of a vertex of
T
, then the vertex group is
G^{*}(v)
as in (i) for the corresponding vertex of
\Gamma
; if instead
v
is the centre of an edge of
T
that is reversed by
G
, then the vertex group is
C_{2}
in its natural action on two points. The edge groups and associated embeddings for the graph of groups are all trivial.

Proof.

Let
L=G^{(\mathrm{P})}
. By Proposition
4.9
we have
L^{+}=(G^{+})^{(\mathrm{P})}
. In particular,
L^{+}
and
G^{+}
have the same orbits on arcs, so
L^{+}\backslash T=G^{+}\backslash T
. Since
G
and
L
have the same orbits on arcs, we have
L=GL_{a}
and in particular
L=GL^{+}
, so for every element of
L
, there is an element of
G
with the same action on
L^{+}\backslash T
. Recall from the definition of an associated local action diagram (Definition
3.6
) that the groups
G(v)
are closed. Thus we can also take
(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(\overline{G(v)}))=\Delta
as the local action diagram of
L
and we see, for all
v\in V\Gamma
, that

\overline{G(v)}/\overline{G(v)}^{+}=G(v)/G(v)^{+}

as permutation groups acting on the set of orbits of
G(v)^{+}
on
X_{v}
, that is on
X_{v}/G(v)^{+}=X_{v}/\overline{G(v)}^{+}
. So for the rest of the proof, it makes no difference if we replace
G
with
L
, so we may assume that
G
is
(\mathrm{P})
-closed.

The description (i) now follows from Theorem
4.12
. In particular, in the action
(G^{+}\backslash T,G/G^{+})
, all arc stabilisers are trivial. In this context, we can easily deduce the graph of groups description given in (ii).
∎

4.5
Open subgroups containing a translation

We now show that given a nondiscrete
(\mathrm{P})
-closed group
G
of automorphisms of a tree, if
g\in G
is a translation, then there is a unique smallest open subgroup
\mathrm{Res}_{G}(g)
of
G
normalised by that element. Given two translations
g
and
h
, whether or not
\mathrm{Res}_{G}(g)=\mathrm{Res}_{G}(h)
depends on a certain class of subtrees determined by the local actions of
G
.

Definition 4.15
.

Given a permutation group
H
acting on a set
X
, define a
quasiblock
of the action to be a nonempty subset
Y
of
X
with the property that the setwise stabiliser
H_{Y}
of
Y
contains the point stabiliser
H_{y}
for every
y\in Y
.

Given a group
G
acting on a tree
T
, say that
T^{\prime}
is a
quasiblock subtree
for the action of
G
on
T
if
T^{\prime}
is a subtree such that
AT^{\prime}
is a quasiblock for the action of
G
on
AT
.

Lemma 4.16
.

If
H\leq\mathrm{Sym}(X)
is primitive but not regular, and
Y\subseteq X
is a quasiblock, then either
Y=X
or
|Y|=1
.

Proof.

Suppose
H\leq\mathrm{Sym}(X)
is primitive, with quasiblock
Y
satisfying
|Y|>1
. For all
y\in Y
we have
H_{y}\leq H_{Y}\leq H
, with
H_{y}
maximal. Therefore either
H_{y}=H_{Y}
for all
y\in Y
or
H_{Y}=H
. The latter implies
Y
is an orbit of
H
, and since
H
is transitive on
X
we have
Y=X
. Suppose then that
H_{y}=H_{Y}
for all
y\in Y
. There are thus distinct
y,y^{\prime}\in Y
such that
H_{y}=H_{Y}=H_{y^{\prime}}
. Pairs of elements of
X
with equal point stabilisers thus form a nontrivial
H
-invariant equivalence relation on
X
. Since
H
is primitive, any stabiliser
H_{y}
must fix
X
pointwise and is therefore trivial; that is,
H
is regular on
X
.
∎

Notice that quasiblock subtrees are naturally constrained by the local actions of
G
: namely, if
T^{\prime}
is a quasiblock subtree, then
o^{-1}(v)\cap AT^{\prime}
is a quasiblock of
G_{v}
for all
v\in VT^{\prime}
.

Proposition 4.17
.

Let
T
be a tree, let
G
be a nondiscrete
(\mathrm{P})
-closed subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
, let
g\in G
be a translation and let
\mathrm{Res}_{G}(g)
be the intersection of all open
\langle g\rangle
-invariant subgroups of
G
.

(i)

There is a unique smallest
G
-quasiblock subtree
T^{\prime}
of
T
containing the axis of
g
.

(ii)

We have
\mathrm{Res}_{G}(g)=\langle G_{e}\mid e\in AT^{\prime}\rangle
. In particular,
\mathrm{Res}_{G}(g)
is open and determined as a subgroup of
G
by
T^{\prime}
.

(iii)

In the action of
O=\mathrm{Res}_{G}(g)\langle g\rangle
on
T
, the smallest invariant subtree is
T^{\prime}
, and
T^{\prime}
is spanned by the axes of translation of
O
.

For the proof, we use a lemma extracted from the proof of
[
14
, Proposition 2.7]
; we include the proof for clarity.

Lemma 4.18
.

Let
T
be a tree, let
G
be a closed subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
, let
H
be an open subgroup of
G
acting with translation, and let
T^{\prime}
be the smallest invariant subtree for the action of
H
. Then
\mathrm{rist}_{G}(T_{e})\times\mathrm{rist}_{G}(T_{\overline{e}})\leq H
for all
e\in AT^{\prime}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.11
,
e
lies on the axis of some
h\in H
. Then
T_{e}
is comparable with
T_{he}
; without loss of generality
T_{he}\subseteq T_{e}
. We then see that
\bigcap_{n\geq 0}T_{h^{n}e}=\emptyset
. Since
H
is open, there are
v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\in VT
such that
\bigcap^{k}_{i=1}G_{v_{i}}\leq H
. Now take
n
large enough that
VT_{h^{n}e}
is disjoint from
\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}
. Then

h^{n}\mathrm{rist}_{G}(T_{e})h^{-n}=\mathrm{rist}_{G}(T_{h^{n}e})\leq\bigcap^{k}_{i=1}G_{v_{i}}\leq H,

so
\mathrm{rist}_{G}(T_{e})\leq H
. Similarly,
\mathrm{rist}_{G}(T_{\overline{e}})\leq H
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.17
.

Given a subtree
T^{*}
of
T
, write
H(T^{*})=\langle G_{a}\mid a\in AT^{*}\rangle
. We can construct
T^{\prime}
recursively as follows: start with the axis
T_{0}
of
g
, and thereafter, for
\alpha
an ordinal we set
T_{\alpha+1}
to be the union of all
H(T_{\alpha})
-images of
T_{\alpha}
, and for
\lambda
a limit ordinal we set
T_{\lambda}=\bigcup_{\alpha<\lambda}T_{\alpha}
. By transfinite recursion this process eventually terminates,
yielding a tree
T_{\beta}=T_{\beta+1}
that is a
G
-quasiblock subtree of
T
. It is clear from the construction that
T^{\prime}:=T_{\beta}
is then the unique smallest
G
-quasiblock subtree containing the axis of
g
. This proves (i).

Let
O=\mathrm{Res}_{G}(g)\langle g\rangle
, let
P
be an open
\langle g\rangle
-invariant subgroup of
G
and let
e
be an arc on the axis of
g
. By Lemma
4.18
, we have
G_{e}\leq P\langle g^{n}\rangle
for all
n>0
, so

G_{e}\leq\bigcap_{n>0}P\langle g^{n}\rangle=P.

Thus
G_{e}\leq\mathrm{Res}_{G}(g)
; in particular,
\mathrm{Res}_{G}(g)
is open, so
O
is open. In addition, we see that
\mathrm{Res}_{G}(g)
is actually the smallest open normal subgroup of
O
, that is,
\mathrm{Res}_{G}(g)=\mathrm{Res}(O)
.

Since
O
acts with translation, by Lemma
2.11
, the union of the axes of translation of
O
is a subtree
T^{\prime\prime}
that is also the smallest invariant subtree for
O
. Let
R=\langle G_{e}\mid e\in AT^{\prime\prime}\rangle
. From the previous paragraph, we see that
R\leq\mathrm{Res}(O)
. On the other hand,
R
is clearly open and normalised by
O
, and
\mathrm{Res}(O)
is the smallest open normal subgroup of
O
, in fact
R=\mathrm{Res}(O)
as claimed.

To finish the proof, we argue that
T^{\prime\prime}=T^{\prime}
. The stabiliser of
T^{\prime}
in
G
is an open subgroup (since it contains arc stabilisers) that contains
g
(by the uniqueness of its construction from the axis of
g
). In particular, it follows that
O
stabilises
T^{\prime}
, ensuring that
T^{\prime\prime}\subseteq T^{\prime}
. On the other hand, since
R\leq O
, we see that
T^{\prime\prime}
is stabilised by each of its arc stabilisers in
G
, that is,
T^{\prime\prime}
is a
G
-quasiblock subtree; since
T^{\prime\prime}
also contains the axis of
g
, it follows that
T^{\prime}\subseteq T^{\prime\prime}
, and hence
T^{\prime}=T^{\prime\prime}
. This completes the proof of (ii) and (iii).
∎

We can now prove the result about RIO subgroups from the introduction.

Proof of Theorem
1.13
.

It is clear that no element of
H
acting as a translation can be contained in a compact subgroup; from this fact, it easily follows that the three cases given are mutually exclusive.
Note that since arc stabilisers are open,
G
must be locally compact.
By
[
15
, Proposition 4.9]
, it follows that every compactly generated open subgroup of
H
is an intersection of open subgroups of
G
.

Let us first consider the case that
H
is compactly generated. If
H
has bounded action, then by Theorem
2.5
there is a vertex
v
such that
|H:H_{v}|\leq 2
, and we see that (i) or (ii) holds. If
H
has unbounded action, then
H
acts with translation by Corollary
2.7
, containing a translation
g
say. Then
\mathrm{Res}_{G}(g)\leq H
, since
H
is an intersection of open subgroups; by Proposition
4.17
,
it follows that
H
contains an arc stabiliser of
G
.

Now consider the general case. If
H
acts with translation, then by the previous paragraph,
H
contains an arc stabiliser of
G
. Thus
H
is open in
G
, and by Lemma
4.6
,
H
is
(\mathrm{P})
-closed, so (iii) holds.
If
H
acts without translation, then by Lemma
2.4
,
H
has bounded action or fixes an end. If
H
has bounded action, then
|H:H_{v}|\leq 2
for some
v\in VT
, and from there it is clear that (i) or (ii) holds; so we may suppose
H
fixes an end.
Thus,
H
has no edge inversions and so (since it also contains no translations) by Proposition
2.2
every element in
H
fixes a vertex. Hence by Lemma
2.6
, every compactly generated closed subgroup
K
of
H
fixes a vertex. Since
H
also fixes an end, in fact
K
fixes an arc on some ray representing that end, and hence
K
is compact, so (i) holds.
∎

4.6
End stabilisers

We note that property
(\mathrm{P})
is inherited by end stabilisers. Moreover, the end stabiliser is open if and only if it contains arc stabilisers.

Lemma 4.19
.

Let
T
be a tree, let
G
be a
(\mathrm{P})
-closed subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
and let
\xi
be an end of
T
. Then the end stabiliser
G_{\xi}
is
(\mathrm{P})
-closed.

Proof.

Let
H=G_{\xi}
. It is clear that
H
is a closed subgroup of
G
, and hence of
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
. Let
a\in AT
; by property
(\mathrm{P})
, we can write
G_{a}=\mathrm{rist}_{G}(T_{a})\times\mathrm{rist}_{G}(T_{\overline{a}})
. Given
a\in AT
, then
\xi
is an end of
T_{a}
or of
T_{\overline{a}}
, but not both; say
\xi
is an end of
T_{a}
. Then
\mathrm{rist}_{G}(T_{\overline{a}})
fixes
a
and
\xi
, that is,
\mathrm{rist}_{G}(T_{\overline{a}})\leq H_{a}
, so

H_{a}=(\mathrm{rist}_{G}(T_{a})\cap H_{a})\times\mathrm{rist}_{G}(T_{\overline{a}})=\mathrm{rist}_{H}(T_{a})\times\mathrm{rist}_{H}(T_{\overline{a}}).

From this decomposition of the arc stabiliser, we conclude via Theorem
2.18
that
H
is
(\mathrm{P})
-closed.
∎

Proposition 4.20
.

Let
T
be a tree, let
G
be a
(\mathrm{P})
-closed subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
, and let
\xi
be an end of
T
. Then
G_{\xi}
is open in
G
if and only if
G_{a}\leq G_{\xi}
for some
a\in AT
. Moreover, If
G_{\xi}
is open, the arcs
a\in AT
such that
G_{a}\leq G_{\xi}
form the arcs of a subtree
T^{\prime}
, such that
\xi
is an end of
T^{\prime}
.

Proof.

If
G_{\xi}
contains an arc stabiliser, then certainly
G_{\xi}
is open. So we may suppose for the rest of the proof that
G_{\xi}
is open in
G
. In other words, there exist
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}\in VT
such that
\bigcap^{n}_{i=1}G_{v_{i}}\leq G_{\xi}
.

The set
X
of arcs
a\in AT
such that
G_{a}\leq G_{\xi}
is closed under edge reversal, so
X
is the set of arcs of some subgraph
T^{\prime}
of
T
. We claim that
T^{\prime}
is nonempty; more precisely we claim that, given a ray
r
representing
\xi
, then the intersection of
T^{\prime}
with
r
is a subray of
r
. So let
r
be a ray representing
\xi
, given by the sequence of arcs
(a_{1},a_{2},\dots)
, with all arcs pointing towards
\xi
. We see that there exists
k
such that the half-tree
T_{a_{k}}
is disjoint from
\{v_{1},\dots,v_{n}\}
, so
\mathrm{rist}_{G}(T_{a_{k}})
fixes
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
. By our hypothesis about
G_{\xi}
, it follows that
\mathrm{rist}_{G}(T_{a_{k}})
fixes
\xi
. Now let
k^{\prime}
be the least
k^{\prime}\geq 1
such that
\mathrm{rist}_{G}(T_{a_{k^{\prime}}})
fixes
\xi
. Then for
k^{\prime\prime}<k^{\prime}
,
G_{a_{k^{\prime\prime}}}
does not fix
\xi
, but for
k^{\prime\prime}\geq k^{\prime}
we can see that
G_{a_{k^{\prime\prime}}}
does fix
\xi
. Indeed,
G_{a_{k^{\prime\prime}}}=\mathrm{rist}_{G}(T_{a_{k^{\prime\prime}}})\times\mathrm{rist}_{G}(T_{\overline{a_{k^{\prime\prime}}}})
by property
(\mathrm{P})
; we see that
\mathrm{rist}_{G}(T_{\overline{a_{k^{\prime\prime}}}})
fixes
\xi
, since
\xi
is not an end of
T_{\overline{a_{k^{\prime\prime}}}}
; and
T_{a_{k^{\prime\prime}}}
is contained in
T_{a_{k^{\prime}}}
so
\mathrm{rist}_{G}(T_{a_{k^{\prime\prime}}})\leq\mathrm{rist}_{G}(T_{a_{k^{\prime}}})\leq G_{\xi}
.

From the description of the intersection of
T^{\prime}
with every ray representing
\xi
, we conclude that
T^{\prime}
is a subtree of
T
such that
\xi
is an end of
T^{\prime}
.
∎

Definition 4.21
.

Let
T
be a tree, let
G
be a
(\mathrm{P})
-closed subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
, and let
\xi
be an end of
T
. We say that
\xi
is
locally invariant
if
G_{\xi}
is open.

Using the fact that the end stabiliser contains arc stabilisers for arcs on a ray representing that end, we can describe locally invariant ends in the local action diagram.

Definition 4.22
.

Let
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(G(v)))
be a local action diagram. A
locally invariant ray quotient
consists of a sequence of arcs
(b_{1},b_{2},\dots)
, forming a path in
\Gamma
(that is,
t(b_{i})=o(b_{i+1})
), and points
p_{i}\in X_{b_{i}}
and
q_{i}\in X_{\overline{b_{i}}}
such that

\forall i\geq 2:p_{i}\neq q_{i-1}\text{ and }G(o(b_{i}))_{p_{i}}\geq G(o(b_{i}))_{q_{i-1}}.

Suppose we have locally invariant ray quotients
r=((b_{i}),(p_{i}),(q_{i}))
and
r^{\prime}=((b^{\prime}_{i}),(p^{\prime}_{i}),(q^{\prime}_{i}))
. We say
r
and
r^{\prime}
are
ray-equivalent
if
b_{i}=b^{\prime}_{i}
, and for each
i\geq 2
, there is
g_{i}\in G(o(b_{i}))
such that
(g_{i}p_{i},g_{i}q_{i-1})=(p^{\prime}_{i},q^{\prime}_{i-1})
; and
end-equivalent
if
r
and
r^{\prime}
can be made ray-equivalent by shifting the indices (where we delete all entries with negative index). A
locally invariant end quotient
is an end-equivalence class of locally invariant ray quotients.

Proposition 4.23
.

Let
T
be a tree and let
G
be a
(\mathrm{P})
-closed subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
. Then the
G
-orbits of locally invariant ends for
G
are in one-to-one correspondence with the locally invariant end quotients of the local action diagram of
G
.

Proof.

Let
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(G(v)))
be the local action diagram of
G
, with quotient map
\pi:T\rightarrow\Gamma
, and suppose that
G
is the universal group given by a
\Delta
-colouring
\mathcal{L}
.

Let
\xi
be a locally invariant end of
T
for the action of
G
. By Proposition
4.20
, there is some ray
(a_{1},a_{2},\dots)
in
T
representing
\xi
such that
G_{a_{i}}
fixes
\xi
for all
i\geq 1
. Letting
b_{i}=\pi(a_{i})
, we have a sequence of arcs
(b_{1},b_{2},\dots)
, forming a path in
\Gamma
, and also points
p_{i}=\mathcal{L}(a_{i})\in X_{b_{i}}
and
q_{i}=\mathcal{L}(\overline{a_{i}})\in X_{\overline{b_{i}}}
. Given
i\geq 2
, the definition of the colouring ensures that
p_{i}\neq q_{i-1}
; the fact that
G_{a_{i-1}}=G_{\overline{a_{i-1}}}
fixes
\xi
ensures that
G_{\overline{a_{i-1}}}
fixes the arc
a_{i}
, so in the local action,
G(o(b_{i}))_{q_{i-1}}
fixes
p_{i}
. Thus
r=((b_{i}),(p_{i}),(q_{i}))
is a locally invariant ray quotient. Consider now how
r
depends on the choices we have made (keeping the colouring fixed) and what happens if we move
\xi
using an element of
G
. Given
g\in G
, the end
g\xi
is represented by the ray
(a^{\prime}_{1},a^{\prime}_{2},\dots)
where
a^{\prime}_{i}=ga_{i}
; we can then perform the same procedure as before to obtain a locally invariant ray quotient
r^{\prime}=((b^{\prime}_{i}),(p^{\prime}_{i}),(q^{\prime}_{i}))
. We have

b^{\prime}_{i}=\pi(ga_{i})=\pi(a_{i})=b_{i},

and the sequences
(p_{i})
and
(q_{i})
are changed according to the local action of
g
, that is,

p^{\prime}_{i}=\mathcal{L}(ga_{i})=\sigma_{\mathcal{L},o(a_{i})}(g)p_{i};\;\quad q^{\prime}_{i}=\mathcal{L}(g\overline{a_{i}})=\sigma_{\mathcal{L},o(\overline{a_{i}})}(g)q_{i}=\sigma_{\mathcal{L},o(a_{i+1})}(g)q_{i}.

In particular, note that for
i\geq 2
,
p_{i}
and
q_{i-1}
are moved by the same element
g_{i}:=\sigma_{\mathcal{L},o(a_{i})}(g)
of
G(o(b(i)))
. So
r
and
r^{\prime}
are ray-equivalent. The choice of where to start the ray
(a_{1},a_{2},\dots)
was arbitrary, but any two choices would be shift-tail equivalent, so regardless of the choices made, the end-equivalence class of
r^{\prime}
is uniquely determined by the orbit
G\xi
. Thus each
G
-orbit of locally invariant ends produces a unique locally invariant end quotient; write
[r(\xi)]
for the end-equivalence class of locally invariant ray quotients obtained from
\xi
.

Conversely, suppose that
r=((b_{i}),(p_{i}),(q_{i}))
is a locally invariant ray quotient of
\Delta
; we aim to produce a ray
(a_{1},a_{2},\dots)
representing an end
\xi
such that
\pi(a_{i})=b_{i}
for all
i
and
r\in[r(\xi)]
. Start with an arc
a_{1}
such that
\mathcal{L}(a_{1})=p_{1}
. Once we have chosen the arc
a_{i}
, let
q^{\prime}_{i}=\mathcal{L}(\overline{a_{i}})
. Then
q^{\prime}_{i}
belongs to the same
G(o(b_{i+1}))
-orbit as
q_{i}
, that is,
q^{\prime}_{i}=g_{i+1}q_{i}
for some
g_{i+1}\in G(o(b_{i+1}))
. There is then an arc
a_{i+1}
with
o(a_{i+1})=t(a_{i})
such that
\mathcal{L}(a_{i+1})=g_{i+1}p_{i+1}
. Since
q_{i}\neq p_{i+1}
, the arcs
a_{i+1}
and
\overline{a_{i}}
have different colours and are therefore distinct, ensuring that
(a_{1},a_{2},\dots)
does not backtrack. Moreover,
g_{i+1}p_{i+1}
is in the same
G(o(b_{i+1}))
-orbit as
p_{i+1}
, so
\pi(a_{i+1})=b_{i+1}
. Now let
i\geq 2
and consider the action of the arc stabiliser
G_{a_{i-1}}=G_{\overline{a_{i-1}}}
on
o^{-1}(o(a_{i}))
. We see that
G_{a_{i-1}}
has local action at
o(a_{i})
given by
G(o(b_{i})_{g_{i}q_{i-1}})=g_{i}G(o(b_{i}))_{q_{i-1}}g^{-1}_{i}
. From the definition of a locally invariant ray quotient, we see that
g_{i}G(o(b_{i}))_{q_{i-1}}g^{-1}_{i}
also fixes
g_{i}p_{i}
, so
G_{a_{i-1}}
fixes
a_{i}
. Thus we have an ascending sequence of open subgroups

G_{a_{1}}\leq G_{a_{2}}\leq\dots

of
G
, from which we see that
G_{a_{1}}
fixes the ray spanned by the arcs
(a_{1},a_{2},\dots)
. Thus
G
has a locally invariant end
\xi
represented by this ray. From the colours of the arcs
a_{i}
and
\overline{a_{i}}
, we see that the locally invariant ray quotient obtained by applying the procedure in the previous paragraph is ray-equivalent to
r
; in particular,
r\in[r(\xi)]
.

So we have a surjective map map
G\xi\mapsto[r(\xi)]
from
G
-orbits of locally invariant ends of the action on
T
to locally invariant end quotients in
\Delta
. It remains to check that
G\xi\mapsto[r(\xi)]
is injective. Consider two rays
(a_{1},a_{2},\dots)
and
(a^{\prime}_{1},a^{\prime}_{2},\dots)
, representing ends fixed respectively by
G_{a_{i}}
and by
G_{a^{\prime}_{i}}
for all
i
, giving rise to locally invariant ray quotients
r=((b_{i}),(p_{i}),(q_{i}))
and
r^{\prime}=((b^{\prime}_{i}),(p^{\prime}_{i}),(q^{\prime}_{i}))
respectively, such that
p_{i}=\mathcal{L}(a_{i})
,
q_{i}=\mathcal{L}(\overline{a_{i}})
, and similarly for
(a^{\prime}_{1},a^{\prime}_{2},\dots)
and
r^{\prime}
. Suppose that
r
and
r^{\prime}
are end-equivalent. After shifting indices on the rays, we may assume that
r
and
r^{\prime}
are ray-equivalent; say
h_{i}\in G(o(b_{i}))
is such that
(p^{\prime}_{i},q^{\prime}_{i-1})=(h_{i}p_{i},h_{i}q_{i-1})
. Note in particular that
b_{i}=\pi(a_{i})
and
b^{\prime}_{i}=\pi(a^{\prime}_{i})
, so the fact that
r
and
r^{\prime}
are equivalent implies that
\pi(a_{i})=\pi(a^{\prime}_{i})
. We use
r
and
r^{\prime}
to produce rays representing locally invariant ends of
T
as in the previous paragraph. Notice that we can use the initial arc
a_{1}
for the ray obtained from
r
and
a^{\prime}_{1}
for the ray obtained from
r^{\prime}
, and from there we can choose the permutations
g_{i}\in G(o(b_{i}))
along the way to be trivial, with the result that the ray we produce from
r
is just
(a_{1},a_{2},\dots)
, and the ray we produce from
r^{\prime}
is
(a^{\prime}_{1},a^{\prime}_{2},\dots)
. In particular, we see that for
i\geq 2
,

\mathcal{L}(\overline{a^{\prime}_{i-1}})=q^{\prime}_{i-1}=h_{i}q_{i-1};\quad\mathcal{L}(a^{\prime}_{i})=p^{\prime}_{i}=h_{i}p_{i}.

Given how the colouring is defined, we see that there is
k_{i}\in G
, with local action
h_{i}
at
o(a_{i})
, such that
k_{i}\overline{a_{i-1}}=\overline{a^{\prime}_{i-1}}
and
k_{i}a_{i}=a^{\prime}_{i}
. By repeatedly using the decomposition of arc stabilisers as a product of rigid stabilisers, there is then
l_{j}\in G
such that
l_{j}a_{i}=a^{\prime}_{i}
for all
j\leq i
, and then since
G
is closed, there is
l\in G
such that
la_{i}=a^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i
. In particular, the ends represented by
(a_{1},a_{2},\dots)
and
(a^{\prime}_{1},a^{\prime}_{2},\dots)
are in the same
G
-orbit, showing that the map
G\xi\mapsto[r(\xi)]
is injective. This completes the proof that
G\xi\mapsto[r(\xi)]
is a one-to-one correspondence between
G
-orbits of locally invariant ends of the action on
T
and locally invariant end quotients in
\Delta
.
∎

Corollary 4.24
.

Let
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(G(v)))
be a local action diagram. Suppose that every infinite path in
\Gamma
passes through some
v\in VT
such that
G(v)
is closed and the point stabilisers of
G(v)
are pairwise incomparable. Then
\mathbf{U}(\Delta)
has no locally invariant ends.

We now have all the ingredients to generalise the theorems
[
4
, Theorem A and Theorem 3.9]
of Caprace–De Medts, as stated in the introduction.

Proof of Theorem
1.11
.

Note that, since
G
is nondiscrete, its open subgroup
G^{+}
is nontrivial. Let
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(G(v)))
be the local action diagram of
G
.

Assume (i), that is, every proper open subgroup of
G
has bounded action on
T
and point stabilisers in G are pairwise incomparable.
Now
G
cannot be horocyclic or focal, since such groups fix an end and thus contain comparable point stabilisers. Examining Table
1
we see that
G
, preserving no proper subtree of
T
, must act with translation. By Lemma
2.12
,
G^{+}
has unbounded action, so
G=G^{+}
. Since
G
is generated by arc stabilisers, it is parity-preserving; in particular,
G
acts without inversion, so every bounded subgroup of
G
fixes a vertex. In particular, given a proper open subgroup
H
of
G
, then
H\leq G_{v}
for some
v\in VT
. On the other hand, given any proper subgroup
H
of
G
, if
G_{v}\leq H<G
for some
v\in VT
, then
H
is open so
H\leq G_{v^{\prime}}
for some
v^{\prime}\in VT
. Hence
G_{v}\leq G_{v^{\prime}}
which is impossible.
Since
G
itself does not fix a vertex, the maximal proper open subgroups of
G
are thus exactly the vertex stabilisers, and all vertex stabilisers are distinct (abstractly) maximal proper subgroups of
G
. This completes the proof that (i) implies (ii). Moreover,
G
is generated by any two distinct cosets of a vertex stabiliser, so by Lemma
2.13
,
G
