has finitely many orbits on
VT\sqcup AT
. The action of
G
cannot be horocyclic or focal; since
G
does not preserve any proper subtree, the only possibility is that the action of
G
is geometrically dense. Hence
G
is simple by Theorem
2.22
.

Assume (ii). Since
G_{v}
and
G_{w}
are distinct maximal subgroups of
G
, we have
G=\langle G_{v},G_{w}\rangle
. Corollary
2.31
ensures that
\Gamma
is a tree, so
|V\Gamma|\geq 2
. Since
G
is generated by vertex stabilisers, it acts without inversion. Let
a
be the arc
(v,w)
. From the fact that
G
is generated by
G_{v}
and
G_{w}
, we see that
\bigcup_{g\in G}\{ga,g\overline{a}\}
is the set of arcs of a connected subgraph of
T
; since
G
does not preserve any proper subtree, in fact
AT=\bigcup_{g\in G}\{ga,g\overline{a}\}
. In particular, we see that
Gv
and
Gw
are the only
G
-orbits on
VT
, so
\{Gv,Gw\}
is the natural bipartition of
VT
. The fact that
G_{v}
and
G_{w}
are both maximal then ensures that
G
acts primitively on both
Gv
and
Gw
, so (iii) holds. We also see by Theorem
2.28
that
G=G_{v}\ast_{G_{(v,w)}}G_{w}
.

We now claim that (iii) and (iv) are equivalent. Let
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(G(v)))
be the local action diagram of
(T,G)
. If either (iii) or (iv) holds, it is clear that
|V\Gamma|=2
and that
|o^{-1}(v)|=1
for all
v\in V\Gamma
; the only possibility is that
\Gamma
consists of a single undirected edge with two distinct endpoints
v
and
w
. In particular, we deduce that
G
is the universal group
\mathbf{U}(G(v),G(w))
defined in
[
19
]
. By
[
19
, Theorem 26]
,
G
acts primitively on both parts of the natural bipartition if and only if
G(v)
and
G(w)
are both primitive but not regular; this establishes the equivalence of (iii) and (iv).

Finally, suppose (iii) and (iv) hold and suppose that
H
is an open subgroup of
G
with unbounded action; we aim to show
G=H
.
Recall Theorem
3.10
and note that the groups
G(v)
, being primitive but not regular, have pairwise incomparable point stabilisers. By Corollary
4.24
, for any end
\xi
of
T
the end stabiliser
G_{\xi}
is not open, and thus
H\neq H_{\xi}=H\cap G_{\xi}
, in other words,
H
does not fix any end of
T
. In particular,
H
cannot be horocyclic, and hence
H
acts with translation.
Let
h\in H
be a translation. Then by Proposition
4.17
there is a smallest invariant subtree
T^{\prime}
for the action of
O=\mathrm{Res}_{G}(h)\langle h\rangle
on
T
such that
T^{\prime}
is spanned by the axes of the translations of
O
, so in particular
T^{\prime}
is leafless; and
T^{\prime}
is the unique smallest
G
-quasiblock subtree of
T
containing the axis of
h
. We claim that
T^{\prime}=T
. Indeed, for each vertex
v
of
T^{\prime}
, because
T^{\prime}
is leafless, there are at least two arcs in
o^{-1}(v)
that lie in
T^{\prime}
. By Lemma
4.16
, a quasiblock of
G(v)
must be either all of
X_{v}
or a single point, so it follows then that
T^{\prime}
contains all arcs in
o^{-1}(v)
. Our claim is established. Applying Proposition
4.17
(ii) we conclude that
O\geq G^{+}
.

We have shown that
T
is the smallest invariant subtree of
H
and that
H\geq G^{+}
. Now given
v\in V\Gamma
, since
G(v)
is primitive but not regular,
G(v)
is generated by point stabilisers, and hence
H
contains all the vertex stabilisers of
G
. Since the
G
-orbits on
VT
are the natural parts of the bipartition,
\Gamma=G\backslash T
is a single undirected edge with distinct endpoints; by Corollary
2.31
,
G
is generated by vertex stabilisers, so
G=H
. Since each group
G(v)
is transitive, it follows that for any vertex
w
of
T
, the stabiliser
G_{w}
fixes no vertex in
VT\smallsetminus\{w\}
, and hence point stabilisers in
G
are pairwise incomparable. Thus (iv) implies (i), and the cycle of implications is complete.
∎

5
Invariant structures

Let
G
be a group acting on a tree
T
. In this section, we describe how certain kinds of
G
-invariant structure in
T
can be detected from the local action diagram of the action.

5.1
Invariant partial orientations

Recall that a
partial orientation
of a graph
\Gamma
is a subset
O
of
A\Gamma
such that for each
a\in A\Gamma
, if
a\in O
then
\overline{a}\not\in O
. Every
G
-invariant partial orientation of
T
gives rise to a partial orientation of
G\backslash T
, and conversely. In particular, the local action diagram provides enough information to give a list of the
G
-invariant partial orientations of
T
.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
\Gamma
be a graph, let
G\leq\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma)
, let
\Gamma^{\prime}=(G\backslash\Gamma)
and let
\pi=\pi_{(\Gamma,G)}
. Then a subset
O
of
A\Gamma
is a
G
-invariant partial orientation of
\Gamma
if and only if
O=\pi^{-1}(O^{\prime})
for some partial orientation
O^{\prime}
of
\Gamma^{\prime}
. Moreover,
O
is a full orientation of
\Gamma
if and only if
O^{\prime}
is a full orientation of
\Gamma^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Suppose
O
is a
G
-invariant partial orientation of
\Gamma
. Since
O
consists of arcs and is
G
-invariant, we have
O=\pi^{-1}(O^{\prime})
for some subset
O^{\prime}
of
A\Gamma
. Suppose
O^{\prime}
is not a partial orientation, that is, there is
a\in O^{\prime}
such that also
\overline{a}\in O^{\prime}
. Let
b\in\pi^{-1}(a)
. Then

\pi(\overline{b})=\overline{\pi(b)}=\overline{a}\in O^{\prime},

so
\overline{b}\in O
contradicting the assumption that
O
is a partial orientation. Thus every
G
-invariant partial orientation
O
of
\Gamma
arises as
\pi^{-1}(O^{\prime})
where
O^{\prime}
is a partial orientation of
\Gamma^{\prime}
.

Conversely, suppose
O^{\prime}
is a partial orientation of
\Gamma^{\prime}
and let
O=\pi^{-1}(O^{\prime})
. Then certainly
O
is a
G
-invariant set of arcs of
\Gamma
; moreover, given
a\in O
, then
\pi(a)\in O^{\prime}
, and hence

\pi(\overline{a})=\overline{\pi(a)}\not\in O^{\prime},

so
\overline{a}\not\in O
. Thus
O
is a partial orientation of
\Gamma
.

If
O^{\prime}
is an orientation of
\Gamma^{\prime}
, then for all
a\in A\Gamma
, either
\pi(a)\in O^{\prime}
, in which case
a\in O
, or else
\pi(\overline{a})=\overline{\pi(a)}\in O^{\prime}
, in which case
\overline{a}\in O
; thus in this case,
O
is an orientation of
\Gamma
. Conversely if
O^{\prime}
is not an orientation of
\Gamma^{\prime}
, say
O^{\prime}\cap\{a,\overline{a}\}=\emptyset
for
a\in A\Gamma^{\prime}
, then for each
b\in\pi^{-1}(a)
, neither
b
nor its reverse is contained in
O
, so
O
is not an orientation of
\Gamma
.
∎

More interesting is to determine, given a partial orientation
O
of the local action diagram, what kind of invariant structure is being described in the tree. Given Theorem
2.22
, partial orientations of
T
that determine subtrees or ends are of particular interest. Our goal in the rest of this subsection is to use partial orientations to characterise the existence of invariant subtrees or ends in terms of the local action diagram.

Definition 5.2
.

Say that a partial orientation
O
of a graph
\Gamma
is
confluent
if for every vertex
v\in V\Gamma
, we have
|o^{-1}(v)\cap O|\leq 1
. A
strongly confluent partial orientation
(scopo) is a confluent partial orientation such that in addition, for all
v\in V\Gamma
, we have

|o^{-1}(v)\cap O|=1\quad\Rightarrow\quad\forall a\in o^{-1}(v),\,|\{a,\overline{a}\}\cap O|=1.

In words: if
O
includes any arc originating at
v
, then
O
is a full orientation of the edges incident with
v
.

Since the quotient map
\pi_{(T,G)}
is locally surjective and the strongly confluent property is defined using local information, we can easily identify the
G
-invariant scopos of the tree from the local action diagram.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
T
be a tree, let
G\leq\mathrm{Aut}(T)
, let
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(G(v)))
be the associated local action diagram and let
\pi=\pi_{(T,G)}
. Let
O
be a partial orientation of
\Gamma
. Then the preimage
\pi^{-1}(O)
is confluent (resp. strongly confluent) on
T
if and only if
O
is confluent (resp. strongly confluent) on
\Gamma
and
|X_{a}|=1
for all
a\in O
. In particular, the
G
-invariant scopos of
T
are precisely the preimages
\pi^{-1}(O)
of those scopos
O
of
\Gamma
that satisfy
|X_{a}|=1
for all
a\in O
.

Proof.

Let
v\in VT
. We can calculate the size of
o^{-1}(v)\cap\pi^{-1}(O)
as follows:

|o^{-1}(v)\cap\pi^{-1}(O)|=\sum\{|X_{a}|:a\in o^{-1}(\pi(v))\cap O\}.

In particular, we see that
|o^{-1}(v)\cap\pi^{-1}(O)|\leq 1
if and only if
|o^{-1}(\pi(v))\cap O|\leq 1
and
|X_{a}|=1
for all
a\in o^{-1}(\pi(v))\cap O
. This establishes that
\pi^{-1}(O)
is confluent if and only if
O
is confluent and
|X_{a}|=1
for all
a\in O
.

Now suppose
O
and
\pi^{-1}(O)
are both confluent and that
|X_{a}|=1
for all
a\in O
. We see that

o^{-1}(v)\cap\pi^{-1}(O)\neq\emptyset\Leftrightarrow o^{-1}(\pi(v))\cap O\neq\emptyset.

If
o^{-1}(v)\cap\pi^{-1}(O)
is empty, we do not need to check the strong confluence condition at
v
or
\pi(v)
, so let us assume that
o^{-1}(v)\cap\pi^{-1}(O)
is nonempty. Then for
O
to be strongly confluent, it must induce a full orientation of the edges of
\Gamma
incident with
\pi(v)
. In fact, since
\pi
is locally surjective, this is equivalent to the condition that
\pi^{-1}(O)
induces a full orientation of the edges of
T
incident with
v
. Thus
O
is strongly confluent if and only if
\pi^{-1}(O)
is strongly confluent.

It now follows from Lemma
5.1
that
the
G
-invariant scopos of
T
are precisely the preimages
\pi^{-1}(O)
of those scopos
O
of
\Gamma
that satisfy
|X_{a}|=1
for all
a\in O
.
∎

Accordingly, given a local action diagram
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(G(v)))
, we define a
(strongly) confluent partial orientation

O
of
\Delta
to be a (strongly) confluent partial orientation of
\Gamma
such that
|X_{a}|=1
for all
a\in O
. For
G\leq\mathrm{Aut}(T)
with associated local action diagram
\Delta
, the
G
-invariant scopos of
T
are thus precisely preimages
\pi^{-1}(O)
of scopos
O
of
\Delta
.

As we shall see, scopos of a graph only occur in a few special forms, and in the tree case they correspond exactly to subtrees and ends.

Given a connected graph
\Gamma
, a directed path
(v_{0},\dots,v_{n})
of length
n\geq 2
is
backtracking
if
v_{i}=v_{i+2}
for some
0\leq i\leq n-2
; directed paths of length
n=0,1
are always non-backtracking. For an induced subgraph
\Gamma^{\prime}
of
\Gamma
, a
projecting path
from
v\in V\Gamma
to
\Gamma^{\prime}
is a directed non-backtracking path
(v_{0},\dots,v_{n})
of some finite length
n\geq 0
, such that
v=v_{0}
and such that
v_{n}\in V\Gamma^{\prime}
and
v_{i}\not\in V\Gamma^{\prime}
for
i<n
. We say a nonempty induced subgraph
\Gamma^{\prime}
is a
cotree
of
\Gamma
if for every
v\in V\Gamma\smallsetminus V\Gamma^{\prime}
there is exactly one projecting path to
\Gamma^{\prime}
, including the choice of arcs (that is, in the projecting path
(v_{0},\dots,v_{n})
we require there to be only one arc of
\Gamma
from
v_{i}
to
v_{i+1}
for
0\leq i<n
). Note that, because there can be only one such path, a cotree of a connected graph is connected.

A
cycle graph
is a finite connected graph in which all vertices have degree
2
. As conventions can differ here, we emphasise that the cycle graph of order
1
consists of a vertex with a loop, but edge-reversal is nontrivial on the loop; the cycle graph of order
2
consists of two vertices with two edges between them. A
cyclic orientation
of a cycle graph
\Gamma
is an orientation that includes exactly one element of
o^{-1}(v)
for each
v\in V\Gamma
. One sees that each cycle graph admits two cyclic orientations, both of which are scopos. We say a graph is
acyclic
if it has no cycle subgraphs. In particular, trees are precisely the orientable acyclic connected graphs.

In any connected graph other than a tree, there is a smallest cotree (which could be the whole graph), and cotrees are connected induced subgraphs that contain the smallest cotree.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
\Gamma
be a connected graph. Let
F
be the set of vertices
v\in V\Gamma
such that
v
belongs to a cycle subgraph or
o^{-1}(v)
contains a loop. Let
\Gamma^{\prime}
be the subgraph induced by the union of all simple paths (including empty paths) with endpoints in
F
. Then
\Gamma^{\prime}
is empty if and only if
\Gamma
is a tree; otherwise,
\Gamma^{\prime}
is connected. In either case, given a nonempty connected induced subgraph
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
of
\Gamma
, then
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
is a cotree if and only if
\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
.

Proof.

We see that
\Gamma^{\prime}
is empty if and only if
\Gamma
has no loops and no cycle subgraphs. Since
\Gamma
is connected, this is exactly the case when
\Gamma
is a tree. Now suppose
\Gamma^{\prime}
is nonempty. Given a simple path
p=(v_{0},\dots,v_{n})
in
\Gamma
such that
v_{0},v_{n}\in V\Gamma^{\prime}
, we see that
v_{0}
and
v_{n}
lie on simple paths with endpoints
a_{0},z_{0}\in F
, respectively
a_{n},z_{n}\in F
, and then
p
is contained in some simple path whose endpoints are a subset of
\{a_{0},z_{0},a_{n},z_{n}\}
, so
p
is contained in
\Gamma^{\prime}
. In particular,
\Gamma^{\prime}
is connected.

Let
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
be a nonempty connected induced subgraph of
\Gamma
. Suppose first that
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
is a cotree of
\Gamma
. From the uniqueness of projecting paths, we see that
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
must contain all loops and cycle subgraphs of
\Gamma
, so
F\subseteq V\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
. Moreover, given
v,w\in V\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
, a simple path
p
from
v
to
w
, and a vertex
x
on this path, then
x
cannot have a unique projecting path unless
x\in V\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
. Since
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
is an induced subgraph, we conclude that
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
contains
\Gamma^{\prime}
.

Conversely, let
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
be a nonempty connected induced subgraph of
\Gamma
that contains
\Gamma^{\prime}
, and let
v\in V\Gamma\smallsetminus V\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
. Then
v
certainly has a projecting path to
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
; suppose for a contradiction that it has two distinct projecting paths
p=(v_{0},\dots,v_{m})
and
p^{\prime}=(v^{\prime}_{0},\dots,v^{\prime}_{n})
, and suppose that
m
is the minimum length for which this occurs. Since
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
contains all cycle subgraphs and
m
is minimal, we see that there are no multiple edges between
v_{i}
and
v_{i+1}
for
0\leq i<m
or between
v^{\prime}_{i}
and
v^{\prime}_{i+1}
for
0\leq i<n
. We also see that
p
is simple and there are no vertices other than endpoints shared by
p
and
p^{\prime}
. We can thus replace
p^{\prime}
with a simple path that is still distinct from
p
. Now we obtain a cycle graph
C
that is the union of the undirected paths specified by
p
and
p^{\prime}
, with a shortest path in
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
from
v_{m}
to
v^{\prime}_{n}
. But then
C\subseteq\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
, a contradiction. From this contradiction we conclude that
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
is a cotree.
∎

In particular, any nonempty proper subtree of a tree is a cotree.

Definition 5.5
.

We now define three kinds of scopos of the connected graph
\Gamma
.

(a)

Given a cotree
z
of
\Gamma
, the associated partial orientation
O_{z}
consists of all arcs
a
such that
o(a)\not\in Vz
and
a
lies on the projecting path from
o(a)
to
z
. (In particular,
O_{\Gamma}=\emptyset
.)

(b)

Suppose
z
is a cycle graph equipped with one of its two cyclic orientations, such that
z
occurs as a cotree of
\Gamma
. Then the associated partial orientation
O^{+}_{z}
is the union of
O_{z}
with the cyclic orientation of
z
.

(c)

If
\Gamma
is a tree and we are given an end
z
of
\Gamma
, then for each arc
a\in A\Gamma
, exactly one of
a
and
\overline{a}
is directed towards
\xi
, that is, it belongs to a ray in the equivalence class
z
. The set
O_{z}
is then defined to be those arcs in
A\Gamma
that are directed towards
z
, and is thus an orientation of
\Gamma
.

Here are some observations on these partial orientations:

(i)

The associated partial orientations of type (
a
), (
b
) and (
c
) are all strongly confluent.

(ii)

The partial orientations of types (
b
) and (
c
) are in fact full orientations of
\Gamma
; a partial orientation of type (
a
) is full if and only if
z
consists of a single vertex with no edges.

(iii)

If
z
is a cotree or end of
\Gamma
and
G
is a group of automorphisms of
\Gamma
, then
z
is
G
-invariant if and only if
O_{z}
is
G
-invariant.

In light of Lemma
5.3
, given a local action diagram
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(G(v)))
we define a
cotree of
\Delta
to be a cotree of
\Gamma^{\prime}
of
\Gamma
such that
|X_{a}|=1
for all
a\in O_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
, and an
invariant end of
\Delta
, in the case that
\Gamma
is a tree, to be an end
\xi
of
\Gamma
such that
|X_{a}|=1
for all
a\in O_{\xi}
. In each case we ensure that
O_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
, respectively
O_{\xi}
is a scopo of
\Delta
.

Our next goal is to show that the types (
a
)–(
c
) actually account for all scopos of connected graphs, so in particular, in the case of trees they correspond to subtrees and ends.

A confluent partial orientation
O
of a graph
\Gamma
defines a map
f_{O}
on
V\Gamma
, as follows: if
o^{-1}(v)\cap O=\{a\}
we set
f_{O}(v)=t(a)
, and if
o^{-1}(v)\cap O=\emptyset
we set
f_{O}(v)=v
. The
attractor

K(O)
of
O
is then defined to consist of the following:

(i)

All vertices of
\Gamma
belonging to periodic orbits of
f_{O}
;

(ii)

All ends of
\Gamma
defined by an aperiodic orbit
(v,f_{O}(v),f^{2}_{O}(v),\dots)
.

Thus each
v\in V\Gamma
defines a nonempty finite subset
z_{O}(v)
of the attractor: if
(v,f_{O}(v),f^{2}_{O}(v),\dots)
is eventually periodic then
z_{O}(v)
is the associated periodic orbit, whereas if
(v,f_{O}(v),f^{2}_{O}(v),\dots)
is aperiodic then
z_{O}(v)
is the associated end. We then have
K(O)=\bigcup_{v\in V\Gamma}z_{O}(v)
.

Attractors of scopos are of a special form, which allows us to recognise the types (a)–(c).

Theorem 5.6
.

Let
\Gamma
be a connected graph, let
O
be a scopo on
\Gamma
and let
K
be the attractor of
O
. Then exactly one of the following occurs:

(a)

There is a cotree
\Gamma^{\prime}
of
\Gamma
such that
V\Gamma^{\prime}=K
and
O=O_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
;

(b)

There is a cotree
\Gamma^{\prime}
of
\Gamma
forming a cycle graph such that
V\Gamma^{\prime}=K
and
O=O^{+}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
for one of the cyclic orientations of
\Gamma^{\prime}
;

(c)

There is an end
\xi
of
\Gamma
such that
K=\{\xi\}
,
\Gamma
is a tree and
O=O_{\xi}
.

Most of the proof will consist of the next two lemmas.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
\Gamma
be a graph and let
O
be a scopo of
\Gamma
.

(i)

If
\Gamma^{\prime}
is a subgraph of
\Gamma
, then
O\cap A\Gamma^{\prime}
is a scopo of
\Gamma^{\prime}
.

(ii)

If
\Gamma
is a cycle graph, then
O
is either empty or it is one of the two cyclic orientations of
\Gamma
.

Proof.

(i) It is clear that any subset of
O
is a confluent partial orientation. We also see that

o^{-1}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(v)\cap O=\{a\}\Rightarrow o^{-1}_{\Gamma}(v)\cap O=\{a\}\Rightarrow t^{-1}_{\Gamma}(v)\subseteq O\cup\{\overline{a}\}\Rightarrow t^{-1}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(v)\subseteq O\cup\{\overline{a}\},

which ensures that
O\cap A\Gamma^{\prime}
is strongly confluent on
\Gamma^{\prime}
.

(ii) It is easy to see that the two cyclic orientations of
\Gamma
are strongly confluent.
Conversely, suppose that
O
is nonempty, that is, there exists
a\in O
. Then the strong confluence condition means that we must also have
s(a)\in O
, where
s(a)
is the unique element of
t^{-1}(o(a))\smallsetminus\{\overline{a}\}
. We then have
s^{n}(a)\in O
for all
n\geq 0
, and since
\Gamma
is finite, eventually the sequence repeats; without loss of generality,
s^{k}(a)=a
. The sequence of arcs
a,s^{k-1}(a),s^{k-2}(a),\dots,s(a)
then defines a directed path from
o(a)
to
o(a)
without backtracking; since
\Gamma
is a cycle graph, we conclude that
O=\{a,s^{k-1}(a),s^{k-2}(a),\dots,s(a)\}
and that
O
is a cyclic orientation of
\Gamma
.
∎

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
\Gamma
be a connected graph and let
O
be a scopo of
\Gamma
.

(i)

The attractor
K(O)
contains the vertices of every cycle subgraph of
\Gamma
and the endpoint of every nonorientable loop of
\Gamma
.

(ii)

For every simple path
(v_{0},\dots,v_{n})
in
\Gamma
such that

O\cap\bigcup^{n}_{i=0}o^{-1}(v_{i})\neq\emptyset,

we have
f_{O}(v_{0})=v_{1}
or
f_{O}(v_{n})=v_{n-1}
(or both). Consequently, if
(v_{0},\dots,v_{n})
is a simple path such that
\{v_{0},v_{n}\}\subseteq K
, then also
v_{1},\dots,v_{n-1}\in K
.

(iii)

Suppose there exist
v,w\in V\Gamma
such that
z_{O}(v)\neq z_{O}(w)
. Then
K(O)
consists exactly of those
v\in V\Gamma
such that
o^{-1}(v)\cap O=\emptyset
.

Proof.

(i) Let
v
be the endpoint of a loop, that is, there is
a\in A\Gamma
such that
o(a)=t(a)=v
. If no arc of
O
originates at
v
, then
f_{O}(v)=v
. Otherwise we see that
O
must contain one of
a
or
\overline{a}
; we thus end up with an arc in
O
originating at
v
that also terminates at
v
, so
f_{O}(v)=v
. In either case, we see that
v\in K(O)
.

Let
\Gamma^{\prime}
be a cycle subgraph of
\Gamma
of order
\geq 2
. By Lemma
5.7
, the restriction
O^{\prime}:=O\cap A\Gamma^{\prime}
is either empty or one of the two cyclic orientations of
\Gamma^{\prime}
. If
O^{\prime}
is a cyclic orientation of
\Gamma^{\prime}
, we immediately see that
\Gamma^{\prime}
is a periodic orbit of
f_{O}
, so
V\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq K(O)
. If instead
O^{\prime}
is empty, then for each
v\in V\Gamma^{\prime}
,
O
is missing at least two of the arcs of
\Gamma
that terminate at
v
, and hence
O
is disjoint from
o^{-1}_{\Gamma}(v)
; this means
f_{O}(v)=v
, so
v\in K(O)
.

(ii)
We consider a simple path
(v_{0},\dots,v_{n})
and
a\in o^{-1}(v_{i})\cap O
for some
0\leq i\leq n
. If
a
is an arc from
v_{0}
to
v_{1}
, we have
f_{O}(v_{0})=v_{1}
, and similarly if
a
is an arc from
v_{n}
to
v_{n-1}
we have
f_{O}(v_{n-1})
; so let us assume neither is the case. Then
v_{i}
has at least one neighbour other than
t(a)
in the path; by symmetry we may assume this neighbour is
v_{i-1}
. Then by strong confluence,
O
must include all arcs from
v_{i-1}
to
v_{i}
, then all arcs from
v_{i-2}
to
v_{i-1}
, and so on, up to the arcs from
v_{0}
to
v_{1}
; hence
f_{O}(v_{0})=v_{1}
.

We now claim that if
(v_{0},\dots,v_{n})
is a simple path whose endpoints are in
K
, then all vertices of the path are in
K
. Suppose not, and suppose
(v_{0},\dots,v_{n})
is a counterexample of minimal length. Then
n\geq 2
and
K\cap\{v_{0},\dots,v_{n}\}=\{v_{0},v_{n}\}
. Since
f_{O}
cannot leave its attractor, we have
f_{O}(v_{0})\neq v_{1}
and
f_{O}(v_{n})\neq v_{n-1}
. But then by the previous paragraph,
O
is disjoint from
o^{-1}(v_{i})
for all
i
, which means that
f_{O}(v_{i})=v_{i}
and hence
v_{i}\in K
for all
i
, a contradiction.

(iii)
Let
v_{i}=f^{i}_{O}(v)
and let
w_{i}=f^{i}_{O}(w)
. Choose
i,j\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
in such a way that the distance
d(v_{i},w_{j})
is minimised; note that
v_{i}\neq w_{j}
, so
d(v_{i},w_{j})>0
. Let
p
be a path of minimal length from
v_{i}
to
w_{j}
. Then by the minimality of
d(v_{i},w_{j})
, neither
f_{O}(v_{i})
nor
f_{O}(w_{j})
is an interior vertex of the path. By part (ii) it follows that
O
is disjoint from
o^{-1}(v_{i})
and
o^{-1}(w_{j})
. In particular,
v_{i}
and
w_{j}
are both fixed by
f_{O}
; hence
z_{O}(v)=\{v_{i}\}
and
z_{O}(w)=\{w_{j}\}
. We then see by the same argument that given any
w^{\prime}\in V\Gamma
, then
z_{O}(w^{\prime})=\{w^{\prime\prime}\}
for some
w^{\prime\prime}
such that
o^{-1}(w^{\prime\prime})\cap O=\emptyset
. Conversely, if
w^{\prime\prime}
is any vertex of
\Gamma
such that
o^{-1}(w^{\prime\prime})\cap O=\emptyset
, then
w^{\prime\prime}
is fixed by
f_{O}
, so
w^{\prime\prime}\in K(O)
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
5.6
.

Suppose that
K
contains a vertex of
\Gamma
. Then by Lemma
5.8
(iii), we see that
K
consists solely of vertices of
\Gamma
, and by parts (i) and (ii) of Lemma
5.8
, together with Lemma
5.4
, we see that
K
is the set of vertices of a cotree
\Gamma^{\prime}
of
\Gamma
. There are then two possibilities. If
K
consists of those
v\in V\Gamma
such that
o^{-1}(v)\cap O=\emptyset
, then case (a) holds. Otherwise, by Lemma
5.8
(iii),
\Gamma^{\prime}
is a cycle graph and
O\cap A\Gamma^{\prime}
is a cyclic orientation of
\Gamma^{\prime}
, and we see that case (b) holds.

The remaining possibility is that
K
does not contain any vertex of
\Gamma
. Then by Lemma
5.8
(i),
\Gamma
is a tree; by Lemma
5.8
(iii), we have
K=\{\xi\}
for a unique end
\xi
of
\Gamma
. It is then clear that case (c) holds.
∎

Theorem
1.4
is a summary of the precise correspondence described in this chapter. For completeness, we give an explicit proof of the theorem below.

Proof of Theorem
1.4
.

Let
G
be a group acting on a tree
T
, with associated local action diagram
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(G(v)))
.
Let
z
be an end of
T
and let
z^{\prime}
be a nonempty proper subtree of
T
. Note that
z^{\prime}
is a cotree of
T
. Thus, using Definition
5.5
, we have scopos
O_{z}
of type (
c
) and
O_{z^{\prime}}
of type (
a
).

If
z
(resp.
z^{\prime}
) is
G
-invariant, then
O_{z}
(resp.
O_{z^{\prime}}
) is
G
-invariant by observation (
iii
) of Definition
5.5
.
As noted above, the
G
-invariant scopos of
T
are precisely preimages under
\pi
of scopos of the local action diagram
\Delta
. Clearly, if
z^{\prime}=T
then
O_{z^{\prime}}
is empty, and is the preimage under
\pi
of the empty scopo of
\Delta
.

On the other hand, given a scopo
O
of the local action diagram
\Delta
, the preimage
\pi^{-1}(O)
is a
G
-invariant scopo of
T
. Because
T
is a tree, Theorem
5.6
guarantees that the
G
-invariant scopos
\pi^{-1}(O)
of
T
are of type (
a
) or (
c
). That is,
\pi^{-1}(O)=O_{z}
, where
z
is either a cotree or an end of
T
. A cotree is a connected induced subgraph, so
z
is either a subtree or an end of
T
. Since
O_{z}
is
G
-invariant, by observation (
iii
) of Definition
5.5
we have that
z
is
G
-invariant.
∎

5.2
Tits’ theorem revisited

Theorem
5.6
immediately provides a characterisation of geometrically dense action in terms of the local action diagram
(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(G(v)))
: specifically, it should be
irreducible
, meaning that the only scopo of
\Delta
is the empty one. (In fact, we only need to know
\Gamma
and the colour sets
X_{a}
; the additional information provided by the groups
G(v)
is not needed.)

Corollary 5.9
.

Let
T
be a tree, let
G\leq\mathrm{Aut}(T)
and let
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(G(v)))
be the associated local action diagram. Then
G
is geometrically dense if and only if
\Delta
is irreducible.

Proof.

Suppose
G
is not geometrically dense. Then either
G
fixes an end or it leaves a proper nonempty subtree of
T
invariant. Recalling that such a subtree of
T
is a cotree, we see that in either case, using parts (
a
), (
c
) and (
iii
) of Definition
5.5
, there is a scopo
O_{z}
of
T
that is
G
-invariant. Hence by Lemma
5.3
,
\Delta
is not irreducible.

On the other hand, if
\Delta
is not irreducible, then
\Delta
has a nonempty scopo
O
. By Lemma
5.3
,
\pi^{-1}(O)
is a
G
-invariant scopo of
T
. By Theorem
5.6
, there is an end or cotree
z
of
T
such that
\pi^{-1}(O)=O_{z}
, and by observation (
iii
) of Definition
5.5
we have that
z
is
G
-invariant. Thus,
G
is not geometrically dense on
T
.
∎

We can now derive Theorem
1.8
.

Proof of Theorem
1.8
.

Let us first consider the case that the action of
G
on
T
is not parity-preserving. In that case, the parity-preserving subgroup
H
of
G
is an open normal subgroup of index
2
. If
G
is cyclic of order
2
, we see that
G
is generated by a single edge inversion and hence the stabiliser of any vertex is trivial, so (i) is false; otherwise
H
is nontrivial, so (i) is false because
G
is not simple. Given a parity-reversing
g\in G
, then
g
is parity-reversing on any
G
-invariant subtree
T^{\prime}
of
T
, meaning that the quotient graph of the action is not bipartite, and in particular is not a tree; thus (ii) is also false. Hence, for the rest of the proof we may assume that
G
is parity-preserving, and in particular inversion-free.

Suppose that (i) holds. Since
G
is simple, a lineal or focal action of
G
is ruled out by Lemma
2.10
, and a bounded or horocyclic action is ruled out since
G
acts with translation. Thus the action is of general type, so there is a unique smallest
G
-invariant subtree
T^{\prime}
, which has infinite diameter, and the action of
G
on
T^{\prime}
is geometrically dense. In particular,
G
acts nontrivially, hence faithfully on
T^{\prime}
. The action of
G
on
T^{\prime}
is
(\mathrm{P})
-closed by Lemma
2.19
. From now on we focus on the action
(T^{\prime},G)
and define subgroups of
G
relative to this action. Let
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(G(v)))
be the local action diagram of the faithful and
(\mathrm{P})
-closed action
(T^{\prime},G)
, then
G=\mathbf{U}(\Delta)
by Theorem
3.10
. Since the action is geometrically dense,
\Delta
is irreducible by Corollary
5.9
. Our hypotheses ensure that all arc stabilisers in
G
are nontrivial. In particular,
G^{+}
is nontrivial; since
G
is simple it follows that
G=G^{+}
. For each
v\in VT^{\prime}
, the vertex stabiliser
G_{v}
has closed action
G(\pi_{(T^{\prime},G)}(v))
on
o_{T^{\prime}}^{-1}(v)
, by Lemma
2.25
and the fact that
G
is
(\mathrm{P})
-closed. By Theorem
4.12
and the description of
\Delta^{+}
in Definition
4.10
, it follows from
G=G^{+}
that
G(v)
is generated by point stabilisers. We also see that
G
is generated by vertex stabilisers, so
\Gamma
is a tree by Corollary
2.31
. If all local actions
G(v)
in
\Delta
were trivial, then any vertex stabiliser in
(T^{\prime},G)
, and thus in
(T,G)
, would be trivial — a contradiction. Thus (i) implies (ii).

For the remainder of the proof we suppose that (ii) holds. The action of
G
on
T^{\prime}
is faithful by assumption, and closed because
(T,G)
is strongly closed;
(T^{\prime},G)
is
(\mathrm{P})
-closed by Lemma
2.19
; and it is geometrically dense by Corollary
5.9
. The fact that
G
has a geometrically dense action on an infinite subtree ensures that the original action is not bounded or horocyclic, so
G
acts on
T
with translation; moreover (as noted in Section
2.2
) this geometrically dense action on
T^{\prime}
ensures that
T^{\prime}
must be leafless, and hence unbounded.
Let
v\in V\Gamma
be such that
G(v)\neq\{1\}
. Since we are assuming that
G(v)
is generated by point stabilisers, there is a nontrivial point stabiliser in
G(v)
, which implies that there is a nontrivial arc stabiliser
G_{a}
for some
a\in AT^{\prime}
. By property
(\mathrm{P})
, the pointwise stabiliser of one of the half-trees of
T^{\prime}
defined by
a
, say
T^{\prime}_{a}
, acts nontrivially on
T^{\prime}
.
Since
G
has geometrically dense action on
T^{\prime}
we know by Lemma
2.3
that for every
b\in AT^{\prime}
, the half-tree
T^{\prime}_{b}
is contained in a
G
-translate of
T^{\prime}_{a}
, so in fact the pointwise stabiliser of
T^{\prime}_{b}
also acts nontrivially on
T^{\prime}
. Applying property
(\mathrm{P})
again, this time to the action on
T
, we find that the pointwise stabiliser of
T_{b}
is nontrivial; in turn, since
T^{\prime}
is unbounded, any finite set of vertices of
T
is contained in
T_{b}
for some
b\in AT^{\prime}
. Hence, there is no finite set of vertices of
T
whose pointwise stabiliser is trivial.

Define
G^{+}
with respect to the closed and faithful action
(T^{\prime},G)
. We have seen that arc stabilisers in
(T^{\prime},G)
are nontrivial, so
G^{+}
is nontrivial and
G^{+}
is simple by Theorem
2.22
.
The action
(T^{\prime},G)
is
(\mathrm{P})
-closed, so
G=\mathbf{U}(\Delta)
.
The fact that
G(v)
is closed and generated by point stabilisers for every
v\in V\Gamma
implies, by Theorem
4.12
, that
G^{+}
and
G
have precisely the same local action at
v
, for all vertices
v\in VT^{\prime}
. Thus, for any arc
a\in VT^{\prime}
lying in
o^{-1}(v)

we have that for all
g\in G_{v}
there is
g^{\prime}\in(G^{+})_{v}
such that
ga=g^{\prime}a
; hence
g\in(G^{+})_{v}
.
From this it follows that
G_{v}=(G^{+})_{v}
. Therefore

R:=\langle G_{v}\mid v\in VT^{\prime}\rangle=\langle(G^{+})_{v}\mid v\in VT^{\prime}\rangle\leq G^{+}\leq R,

so in particular
R
is simple.
Now
(T^{\prime},G)
acts without inversion because
(T,G)
is inversion-free, and so by Theorem
2.27
we have
G=R
because
\Gamma=G\backslash T^{\prime}
is a tree. Thus
G
is simple.

Finally we note that in (ii), since
G
is a
(\mathrm{P})
-closed subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(T^{\prime})
with local action diagram
\Delta
, we have
G=\mathbf{U}(\Delta)
by Theorem
3.10
.
∎

The next proposition gives an alternative characterisation of when the local action diagram
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(G(v)))
is irreducible. We say
\Delta
is a
focal cycle
if
\Gamma
is a cycle graph admitting a cyclic orientation
O
, such that
|X_{a}|=1
for all
a\in O
. For each end
\xi
of
\Gamma
, say
\xi
is a
horocyclic end
of
\Delta
if
\Gamma
is a tree and
|X_{a}|=1
for every
a\in O_{\xi}
. Recall that for
a\in A\Gamma
the graph
\Gamma\smallsetminus\{a\}
is obtained from
\Gamma
by removing arcs
a,\overline{a}
. If
\Gamma\smallsetminus\{a\}
is not connected, and
\Gamma_{a}
is the connected component of
\Gamma\smallsetminus\{a\}
containing
t(a)
, we say that
\Gamma_{a}
is a
stray half-tree
of
\Delta
if
\Gamma_{a}
is a tree containing no leaves of
\Gamma
, and in
\Gamma_{a}
, we have
|X_{b}|=1
for all arcs
b
oriented towards
t(a)
. A
stray leaf
of
\Delta
is a leaf
v
such that
|X_{v}|=1
.

Proposition 5.10
.

Let
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(G(v)))
be a local action diagram. Then
\Delta
is irreducible if and only if
\Delta
is not a focal cycle and has no horocyclic ends, no stray half-trees and no stray leaves.

Proof.

Firstly, we see that
\Delta
is a focal cycle if and only if there is scopo of type (
b
) forming an orientation of
\Gamma
.
Indeed, if
\Delta
is a focal cycle then the cyclic orientation of
\Gamma
gives rise to a scopo of type (
b
), as described in Definition
5.5
. On the other hand, suppose
\Delta
admits a scopo
O
of type (
b
) that forms an orientation of
\Gamma
. By Theorem
5.6
(
b
) there is a cotree
\Gamma^{\prime}
of
\Gamma
that forms a cycle graph and
O=O_{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{+}
for one of the cyclic orientations for
\Gamma^{\prime}
. Since
O
is in fact an orientation of
\Gamma
(not just a partial orientation) it follows immediately that
\Gamma
must be a cycle graph.

Secondly, we note that the horocyclic ends correspond to the scopos of
\Delta
of type (
c
).
From these two observations we may thus, henceforth, assume
\Delta
is not a focal cycle and has no horocyclic ends. In this case, we see from Theorem
5.6
, that
\Delta
is irreducible if and only if there is no nonempty scopo of type (
a
) or (
b
); in turn, by Lemma
5.3
, no such scopo exists if and only if
\Delta
has no proper cotree (see Definition
5.5
).

To establish our proposition, we need only prove that
\Delta
has no proper cotree if and only if it has no stray leaves and no stray half-tree. Given a leaf
v
of
\Gamma
, if
|X_{v}|=1
, then
\Gamma\smallsetminus\{v\}
is a proper cotree of
\Delta
, whereas if
|X_{v}|>1
we see that
v
must be included in every cotree of
\Delta
. If
\Gamma_{a}
is a stray half-tree defined by the arc
a
, then
\Gamma\smallsetminus V\Gamma_{a}
is a proper cotree of
\Delta
.

Now we assume there are no stray half-trees or stray leaves, and suppose for a contradiction that
\Gamma^{\prime}
is a proper cotree of
\Delta
.
Then:
(i) the partial orientation
O_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
consists of all arcs
b
such that
o(b)\not\in V\Gamma^{\prime}
and
b
lies on the projecting path from
o(b)
to
\Gamma^{\prime}
;
(ii)
|X_{b}|=1
for all arcs
b\in O_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
; and
(iii) there is an arc
a\in A\Gamma
such that
o(a)\in V\Gamma^{\prime}
and
t(a)\not\in V\Gamma^{\prime}
and
\Gamma\smallsetminus\{a\}
is not connected.
Let
\Gamma_{a}
be the connected component of
\Gamma\smallsetminus\{a\}
containing
t(a)
. Then we see that
\Gamma_{a}
is disjoint from
\Gamma^{\prime}
and is hence a tree. Since there are no stray leaves, we see that
\Gamma_{a}
has no leaves of
\Gamma
; thus
\Gamma_{a}
has no leaves except possibly
t(a)
, and
t(a)
is not a leaf of
\Gamma
, so
|V\Gamma_{a}|\geq 2
. We also see that every arc
b
of
\Gamma_{a}
oriented towards
t(a)
belongs to a projecting path, so
|X_{b}|=1
. But then
\Gamma_{a}
is a stray half-tree, a contradiction. This contradiction shows that
\Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma
and hence that
\Delta
is irreducible.
∎

Corollary
1.5
now follows immediately from Corollary
5.9
and Proposition
5.10
together with Tits’ Theorem (Theorem
2.22
). The proof of Corollary
1.6
is also straightforward.

Proof of Corollary
1.6
.

By hypothesis, the underlying graph
\Gamma
of the local action diagram
\Delta
is finite and not a cycle graph. We immediately see that
\Delta
is not a focal cycle. Moreover, since
\Gamma
is finite, it has no ends and contains no leafless subtree, so
\Delta
cannot have any horocyclic end or stray half-tree. Following Proposition
5.10
, the sole remaining obstacle to the irreducibility of
\Delta
is if it has a stray leaf, which is exactly the case that
\Gamma
has a leaf such that
|X_{v}|=1
. Thus
\Delta
is irreducible if and only if
|X_{v}|>1
for every leaf of
\Gamma
. The remaining conclusion follows by Tits’ theorem.
∎

As stated, Theorem
2.22
leaves open the question of whether or not the group
G^{+}
generated by the arc stabilisers is simple or trivial. In fact, this distinction is easy to detect in the local action diagram.

Say that a local action diagram
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{v}),(G(v)))
is
free
if for all
v\in V\Gamma
,
G(v)
acts freely on
X_{v}
.

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
T
be a tree, let
G\leq\mathrm{Aut}(T)
and let
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{v}),(G(v)))
be the associated local action diagram. Then
G^{+}
is trivial if and only if
\Delta
is free.

Proof.

Let
\pi=\pi_{(T,G)}
. Suppose that
G^{+}
is trivial. Then for all
a\in A\Gamma
,
G_{a}
is trivial. Let
v\in VT
. Then the action of
G_{v}
on
o^{-1}(v)
is free, since the set of stabilisers of this action is exactly
\{G_{a}\mid a\in o^{-1}(v)\}
. Thus
G(\pi(v))
acts freely on
X_{\pi(v)}
.

Conversely, suppose that for all
v\in V\Gamma
,
G(v)
acts freely on
X_{v}
. Let
a\in AT
and let
g\in G_{a}
. Suppose
g\neq 1
: then there is some arc
b\not\in\{a,\overline{a}\}
such that
b
is directed away from
a
,
g
fixes
w=o(b)
, but
g
does not fix
b
. Then the action of
G_{w}
on
o^{-1}(w)
corresponds as a permutation group to the action of
G(\pi(w))
on
X_{\pi(w)}
; that is, the action is free modulo kernel. If
w\in\{o(a),t(a)\}
, then clearly
g
fixes an element of
o^{-1}(w)
; otherwise,
g
fixes the unique arc in
o^{-1}(w)
in the direction of
\{o(a),t(a)\}
. Thus
g
is an element of
G_{w}
fixing some element of
o^{-1}(w)
; since the action of
G_{w}
on
o^{-1}(w)
is free modulo kernel, we conclude that
gb=b
, contradicting the choice of
b
. Thus in fact
g=1
, showing that
G_{a}
is trivial. Since this holds for all
a\in AT
, we conclude that
G^{+}
is trivial.
∎

For a more detailed analysis of the case when the action is not geometrically dense, recall the possible types of action of a group
G
acting on a tree
T
given in Theorem
2.5
. We can recognise these cases from the local action diagram
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(G(v)))
as follows.

(Fixed vertex)

If
G
fixes a vertex of
T
, then
\Gamma
is a tree. If
\Gamma
is a tree, the fixed vertices of
G
on
T
(if there are any) correspond to cotrees
\Gamma^{\prime}
of
\Delta
consisting of a single vertex of
\Gamma
with no edges.

(Inversion)

G
preserves a unique undirected edge (and includes a reversal of that edge) if and only if there is a cotree
\Gamma^{\prime}
of
\Delta
consisting of a single vertex and a single nonorientable loop
a
with
|X_{a}|=1
.

(Lineal)

G
fixes exactly two ends and translates the axis between them if and only if there is a cotree
\Gamma^{\prime}
of
\Delta
that is a cycle graph, such that additionally
|X_{a}|=1
for all
a\in A\Gamma^{\prime}
.

(Horocyclic)

Assuming that
G
does not fix any vertices, then it is easy to see that
G
fixes a unique end while not including any translations exactly in following situation:
\Gamma
is a tree, and there is a unique horocyclic end of
\Gamma
. To see why the horocyclic end must be unique, suppose
G
fixes a unique end of
T
while not including any translations and
\Gamma
is a tree with multiple horocyclic ends. Then for any two distinct horocyclic ends
\xi
and
\xi^{\prime}
, we find that the line
L
from
\xi
to
\xi^{\prime}
is a cotree of
\Delta
such that
|X_{a}|=1
for all
a\in AL
, and hence
G
fixes pointwise the preimage of
L
in
T
.

(Focal)

The following characterises the situation where
G
fixes a unique end and includes a translation towards this end: There is a cotree
\Gamma^{\prime}
of
\Delta
that is a cycle graph, and a cyclic orientation
O^{\prime}
of
\Gamma^{\prime}
, such that
|X_{a}|=1
for
a\in O^{\prime}
, but
|X_{a}|\geq 2
for some
a\in A\Gamma^{\prime}\smallsetminus O^{\prime}
.

In particular, the case where
G
has focal action and no proper invariant subtree corresponds exactly to the case where
\Delta
is a focal cycle such that the colour set of some arc is nontrivial.
Indeed, as in the proof of Theorem
1.4
we note that proper invariant subtrees of
(T,G)
are cotrees and have a corresponding
G
-invariant scopo; these
G
-invariant scopos arise as the preimages of nonempty scopos of
\Delta
. If
\Delta\neq\Gamma^{\prime}
then
\Gamma^{\prime}
is a proper cotree and gives rise to a proper invariant subtree in
(T,G)
; if
\Delta=\Gamma^{\prime}
then
\Delta
admits no proper cotree or end, therefore no scopo of type (a) or (c), and thus there is no proper invariant subtree of
(T,G)
.

(General type)

In the remaining case, the unique minimal subtree
T^{\prime}
on which
G
acts geometrically densely corresponds to the unique smallest cotree
\Gamma^{\prime}
of
\Delta
, where
\Gamma^{\prime}
is not of the special form indicating a bounded, lineal or focal action, and
\Delta
does not have any horocyclic ends. The action of
G
on
T
is geometrically dense if and only if
\Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma
.

In all cases except when
G
fixes a vertex, there are at most two invariant ends, and the invariant ends are easily identified. If
G
fixes a vertex
v
of
T
, then the invariant ends correspond to rays starting at
v
that are fixed pointwise by
G
, so they are accounted for by invariant subtrees. We also note that for lineal and focal actions, there is a unique smallest invariant subtree
T^{\prime}
spanned by all axes of translation of
G
; the only distinction from general type is the existence of one or two fixed ends of this subtree.

Note also the following: if
\Gamma
is a tree, then the action has a fixed vertex, or is horocyclic or of general type; in particular, by Corollary
2.31
, if
G
is generated by vertex stabilisers then the action must be of one of these types. If
\Gamma
is not a tree, then the possibilities are: inversion (in which case
\Gamma
is a tree plus a single nonorientable loop), lineal, focal and general type.

We also have the following correspondence between types of scopo given by
O\mapsto\pi_{(T,G)}(O)
:

(i)

Invariant subtrees of
T
, or equivalently, invariant scopos of
T
of type (a), correspond to scopos of
\Delta
of type (a).

(ii)

Inversion and general type actions do not have invariant ends. Otherwise, there are two kinds of invariant end to consider:

(1)

If
\Gamma
is a tree, the action could have a fixed vertex or be horocyclic, without translation. In this case invariant ends of
T
correspond to scopos of
\Delta
of type (c).

(2)

If
\Gamma
is not a tree, the action could be lineal or focal with a translation towards an invariant end. In this case invariant ends of
T
correspond to scopos of
\Delta
of type (b), with the order of the associated cycle graph in
\Gamma
corresponding to the minimal translation length of a translation towards the fixed end.

5.3
Quotient trees

Given a group
G
acting on a tree
T
, a
quotient tree
of the action is a surjective graph homomorphism
\theta:T\rightarrow T^{\prime}
, such that the fibres of
\theta
on vertices and arcs form a system of imprimitivity for
G
, together with the induced action of
G
on
T^{\prime}
. Our methods are not sufficient to classify quotient trees in general, even if the original action is
(\mathrm{P})
-closed. However, we can say something in the case that
\theta
is locally surjective.

Given
a\in AT
, say that
\theta:T\rightarrow T^{\prime}

backtracks at
a
, or
a
is a
backtracking for
\theta
, if there exists
b\in AT
such that

o(a)\neq o(b);t(a)=t(b);\theta(a)=\theta(b).

Of course a graph homomorphism
\theta
between trees with backtracking is not injective; conversely if
\theta
is not injective then
\theta(v)=\theta(w)
for some distinct vertices
v,w
in
T
, and examining the image under
\theta
of
[v,w]
we see that at some point backtracking must occur. Thus a graph homomorphism between trees is injective if and only if it has no backtracking.
Say that
a\in AT
is
pre-backtracking
for
\theta
if there is a backtracking arc
a^{\prime}
such that
T_{a^{\prime}}\subseteq T_{a}
.

Note that
a\in AT
cannot be (pre-)backtracking if
t(a)
is a leaf, since there are no arcs
b\neq a
such that
t(a)=t(b)
and no half-trees contained in
T_{a}=\{t(a)\}
other than
T_{a}
itself. Moreover, if
\theta
is locally surjective then the image of every leaf is a leaf, but potentially non-leaves of
T
can be mapped to leaves of
T^{\prime}
.

The locally surjective property is particularly useful for restricting the possible images of half-trees.

Lemma 5.12
.

Let
T
and
T^{\prime}
be trees and let
\theta:T\rightarrow T^{\prime}
be a locally surjective graph homomorphism.

(i)

Given
a^{\prime}\in AT^{\prime}
, then
T^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}}\subseteq\theta(T_{a})
, for all
a\in\theta^{-1}(a^{\prime})
.

(ii)

If
a\in AT
is pre-backtracking for
\theta
then
\theta(T_{a})=T^{\prime}
.

Proof.

(i)
We see that
T^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}}
is the smallest subgraph
\Gamma
of
T^{\prime}
with the properties that
t(a^{\prime})\in V\Gamma
and
o^{-1}_{T^{\prime}}(t(a^{\prime}))\smallsetminus\{a^{\prime},\overline{a^{\prime}}\}\subseteq A\Gamma
, and for all
v^{\prime}\in V\Gamma\smallsetminus\{t(a^{\prime})\}
we have
o^{-1}_{T^{\prime}}(v^{\prime})\subseteq A\Gamma
. Consider now the graph
\Gamma=\theta(T_{a})
where
a\in\theta^{-1}(a^{\prime})
. Then
t(a^{\prime})=\theta(t(a))\in V\Gamma
, and since
\theta
is locally surjective, we have

o^{-1}_{T^{\prime}}(t(a^{\prime}))\smallsetminus\{a^{\prime},\overline{a^{\prime}}\}\subseteq\theta(o^{-1}(t(a))\smallsetminus\{a,\overline{a}\})\subseteq A\Gamma.

Furthermore, given a vertex
v^{\prime}\in V\Gamma\smallsetminus\{t(a^{\prime})\}
, then there is
v\in VT_{a}\smallsetminus\{t(a)\}
such that
\theta(v)=v^{\prime}
. We then have
o^{-1}_{T}(v)\subseteq T_{a}
and hence, by local surjectivity,
o^{-1}_{T^{\prime}}(v^{\prime})\subseteq A\Gamma
. Thus
T^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}}\subseteq\Gamma
.

(ii)
Since the half-tree defined by a pre-backtracking arc contains a half-tree defined by a backtracking one, we may assume that
a
is backtracking. Let
v=t(a)
and
b\in AT
such that

o(a)\neq o(b);t(a)=t(b);\theta(a)=\theta(b).

Now
T^{\prime}=T^{\prime}_{\theta(b)}\cup T^{\prime}_{\theta(\overline{b})}\cup\{\theta(b),\theta(\overline{b})\}
. Since
a\in\theta^{-1}(\theta(b))
, by (i) we have

T^{\prime}_{\theta(b)}\subseteq\theta(T_{a})
.
Since
\overline{b}\in\theta^{-1}(\theta(\overline{b}))
and
T_{\overline{b}}\subseteq T_{a}
, by (i) we have

T^{\prime}_{\theta(\overline{b})}\subseteq\theta(T_{\overline{b}})\subseteq\theta(T_{a})
. Since
b,\overline{b}\in AT_{a}
we have
\theta(b),\theta(\overline{b})\in\theta(T_{a})
. Thus
T^{\prime}\subseteq\theta(T_{a})
and (ii) follows.
∎

Suppose now that we have an action
(T,G)
on a tree and
\theta:T\rightarrow T^{\prime}
is a quotient tree that is locally surjective. There are a few ways in which
\theta
can be ‘almost injective’, which are useful to distinguish.

Lemma 5.13
.

Let
(T,G)
be an action on a tree and let
\theta:T\rightarrow T^{\prime}
be a quotient tree that is locally surjective. Then exactly one of the following holds.

(a)

There is a
G
-invariant subtree
T_{0}
of
T
with at least two vertices, such that no arc in
T_{0}
is pre-backtracking for
\theta
and the restriction of
\theta
to
T_{0}
is injective.

(b)

There is a
G
-invariant end
\xi
of
T
, such that
a\in AT
is pre-backtracking for
\theta
if and only if
a
is oriented towards
\xi
.

(c)

There is a
G
-invariant subtree
T_{0}
of
T
such that every
a\in AT_{0}
is pre-backtracking for
\theta
, and for all arcs
a\in AT\smallsetminus AT_{0}
, then
a
is pre-backtracking for
\theta
if and only if
a
is oriented towards
T_{0}
.

Proof.

Let
B
be the set of pre-backtracking arcs for
\theta
, let
B^{*}
be the set of
a\in AT
such that
\{a,\overline{a}\}\subseteq B
and let
C
be the set of vertices
v\in VT
such that
o^{-1}(v)\cap B=\emptyset
.

We see that
B
,
B^{*}
and
C
are
G
-invariant. Suppose
B^{*}
is nonempty. Then we see that
B^{*}
is convex in
AT
, that is, it is the set of arcs of a
G
-invariant subtree
T_{0}
of
T
. Given
a\in AT\smallsetminus AT_{0}
, if
a
is oriented towards
T_{0}
then
a\in B
, but since
a\not\in B^{*}
we have
\overline{a}\not\in B
. Thus (c) is satisfied. From now on we may assume that
B^{*}
is empty, in other words
B
is a full or partial orientation (possibly empty).

Given distinct vertices
v,v^{\prime}\in C
and a third vertex
w
on the path from
v
to
v^{\prime}
, then any arc
b\in o^{-1}(w)
satisfies
T_{b}\subseteq T_{a}
or
T_{b}\subseteq T_{a^{\prime}}
, or both, where
a
and
a^{\prime}
are arcs directed towards
w
in
o^{-1}(v)
and
o^{-1}(v^{\prime})
respectively. Since neither
a
nor
a^{\prime}
is pre-backtracking,
b
cannot be pre-backtracking. Thus
C
is convex as a subset of
VT
, so it spans a
G
-invariant subtree
T_{0}
of
T
. On
T_{0}
, the restriction of
\theta
is injective, because there is no backtracking. Thus as soon as
|C|\geq 2
, then (a) is satisfied.

Consider next the case that
C=\{v\}
. If
VT=\{v\}
then we are in case (c), so we may suppose this is not the case. Suppose
v
is a leaf. Then for the unique arc
a\in o^{-1}(v)
, we see that
a
is not pre-backtracking, and
\overline{a}
is not pre-backtracking because it terminates in a leaf, but also
T_{a}
contains all half-trees of
T
apart from
T_{\overline{a}}
. This means
B=\emptyset
, so
C=VT
, a contradiction. Now suppose
|o^{-1}(v)|\geq 2
. Since
v\in C
, none of the arcs oriented away from
v
are pre-backtracking. Therefore, the fact that
C
contains none of the neighbours of
v
ensures that every
a\in t^{-1}(v)
is pre-backtracking, and from there we see that every arc oriented towards
v
is pre-backtracking. Thus (c) holds with
VT_{0}=\{v\}
.

From now on we may assume that
C
is empty, that is,
o^{-1}(v)\cap B
is nonempty for every
v\in VT
. Starting from some vertex
w_{0}
, we choose a path of vertices
(w_{0},w_{1},\dots)
so that
(w_{i},w_{i+1})\in B
for each
i\geq 0
. The sequence of vertices obtained does not backtrack, since
B^{*}
is empty, and hence does not repeat, since
T
is a tree. Thus
(w_{0},w_{1},\dots)
is a ray representing some end
\xi
, such that all arcs on the ray oriented towards
\xi
are pre-backtracking. It then follows that in fact, every arc pointing towards
\xi
is pre-backtracking, that is,
O_{\xi}\subseteq B
. Since
B
is a partial orientation we deduce that in fact
B=O_{\xi}
, and since
B
is
G
-invariant, so is
\xi
. Thus (b) holds.

The three cases are mutually exclusive, as follows: (a) is the unique case in which there are undirected edges
\{a,\overline{a}\}
disjoint from
B
and (b) is the unique case in which
B=O_{\xi}
for an end
\xi
of
T
.
∎

By analogy with the usual analysis of actions on trees, we can regard cases (a) and (b) (and case (c) if
|VT_{0}|=1
) of Lemma
5.13
as degenerate cases of quotient trees; in general there could be many such quotient trees arising from systems of imprimitivity on sets of vertices at a given distance from the invariant tree, or horospheres around the invariant end. In case (c), it makes sense to focus on the restriction of
\theta
to a map from
T_{0}
to
\theta(T_{0})
, considered as a quotient tree of the action
(T_{0},G)
. So by analogy with the definition of a geometrically dense action, we will say that
\theta
is
densely noninjective
if
|VT|>1
and case (c) of Lemma
5.13
holds with
T=T_{0}
, that is, every arc is pre-backtracking. In particular, if
\theta
is densely noninjective, then
T
must be an infinite leafless tree, since an arc terminating at a leaf cannot be pre-backtracking. On the other hand, if
(T,G)
is geometrically dense, then it follows from Lemma
5.13
that every locally surjective, noninjective quotient tree is densely noninjective. We then have the following consequence of the previous two lemmas.

Proposition 5.14
.

Let
(T,G)
be an action on a tree and let
\theta:T\rightarrow T^{\prime}
be a quotient tree, with
G
acting on
T^{\prime}
via its induced action. Suppose that
\theta
is locally surjective and densely noninjective. Then
G^{++}
acts trivially on
T^{\prime}
; in particular,
\theta
factors through the locally surjective quotient tree
\pi_{+}:=\pi_{(T,G^{++})}
.

Proof.

By our hypotheses and Lemma
5.13
, every
a\in AT
is pre-backtracking for
\theta
, so by Lemma
5.12
, we have
\theta(T_{a})=T^{\prime}
for every half-tree
T_{a}
of
T
. In particular, the rigid stabiliser of
T_{\overline{a}}
must act trivially on
T^{\prime}
. Thus
G^{++}
acts trivially on
T^{\prime}
, that is, for all
g\in G^{++}
and
e\in VT\sqcup AT
, we have
\theta(e)=\theta(ge)
. Thus
\theta
factors through
\pi_{+}
. Since
G^{++}
is normal in
G
, it is clear that
G^{++}\backslash T
is equipped with an action of
G
such that
\pi_{+}
is
G
-equivariant. We see that
G^{++}\backslash T
is a tree by Corollary
2.31
, so
\pi_{+}
is a quotient tree for
G
. By Lemma
2.1
,
\pi_{+}
is locally surjective.
∎

If
(T,G)
is an action on a tree with property
(\mathrm{P})
, then (see Remark
2.24
) we have
G^{++}=G^{+}
, and so to describe the locally surjective, densely noninjective quotient trees of the action, it is enough to describe the locally surjective quotient trees of the action
(G^{+}\backslash T,G/G^{+})
, starting with
(G^{+}\backslash T,G/G^{+})
itself, which we have already described in Theorem
4.12
and Corollary
4.14
. In general there could be further quotient trees of
(G^{+}\backslash T,G/G^{+})
; however, from this point on, since we have trivial arc stabilisers, the local action diagram approach effectively reduces to a well-known special case of classical Bass–Serre theory, so it is unlikely that the methods developed in this article will give new insights. We therefore leave any further investigation to the interested reader.

6
The group topology

Our definitions ensure that whenever
G\leq\mathrm{Aut}(T)
is such that
G=G^{(\mathrm{P})}
, then
G
is a closed subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
in the permutation topology. In particular, it follows that
G
is a non-Archimedean topological group in its own right.

There are natural characterisations of when
G^{(\mathrm{P})}
is Polish or locally compact as a subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
.

6.1
Polish
(\mathrm{P})
-closed groups

Here is a characterisation of Polish
(\mathrm{P})
-closed groups. For clarity, we note that the word ‘countable’ here is understood to allow finite sets as well as countably infinite sets.

Let
X
be a set, let
G
act by permutations on
X
and let
Y\subseteq X
be
G
-invariant. We say that the action of
G
on
Y
is
strongly faithful (relative to
X
)
if for all
x\in X
, there exists a finite subset
\{y_{1},\dots,y_{n}\}
of
Y
such that
\bigcap^{n}_{i=1}G_{y_{i}}
fixes
x
.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
X
be a set and let
G
be a closed subgroup of
\mathrm{Sym}(X)
. Then
G
is Polish if and only if there is a countable subset
Y
of
X
on which
G
acts strongly faithfully. Moreover, if
Y
is a countable strongly faithful set for
G
, then the induced homomorphism
\theta:G\rightarrow\mathrm{Sym}(Y)
is a closed topological embedding.

Proof.

We note first that
\mathrm{Sym}(X)
is a non-Archimedean topological group, that is, it has a base of neighbourhoods of the identity consisting of open subgroups. It follows that any subgroup of
\mathrm{Sym}(X)
with the subspace topology is also non-Archimedean.

Suppose
Y=\{y_{0},y_{1},\dots\}
is a countable strongly faithful set for
G
and let
\theta:G\rightarrow\mathrm{Sym}(Y)
be the natural homomorphism. Then
\theta
is clearly injective and continuous. To show that
\theta
is a closed map (i.e. the image of every closed set is closed), we need to show that given a net
(g_{i})_{i\in I}
in
G
such that
\theta(g_{i})
converges to the identity, then
(g_{i})
converges to the identity. Indeed, since
\theta(g_{i})\rightarrow 1
as
i\rightarrow\infty
, we see that for all
n\geq 0
, there exists
i_{n}
such that
g_{i}
fixes
y_{0},\dots,y_{n}
for all
i>i_{n}
, so
g_{i}\in G_{n}:=\bigcap^{n}_{i=0}G_{y_{i}}
. Since the groups
G_{n}
are a base of neighbourhoods of the identity, it follows that
g_{i}\rightarrow 1
as
i\rightarrow\infty
. Thus
\theta
is a closed topological embedding as claimed.

In particular, we see from the previous paragraph that if a countable strongly faithful set
Y
exists for
G
, then
G
is isomorphic to a closed subgroup of the Polish group
\mathrm{Sym}(Y)
; thus
G
is Polish.

Conversely, suppose that
G
is Polish. In particular,
G
is separable, so all open subgroups of
G
have countable index; thus
G
has countable orbits on
X
. Since
G
is non-Archimedean and metrisable, there is a countable set of open subgroups of
G
forming a base of neighbourhoods of the identity; recalling the standard base of topology for
\mathrm{Sym}(X)
, in fact there is a sequence
(y_{i})_{i\geq 0}
of points such that
\{G_{n}\mid n\geq 0\}
is a base of neighbourhoods of the identity, where

G_{n}=\bigcap^{n}_{i=0}G_{y_{i}}.

Given
x\in X
, we then see that
G_{n}\leq G_{x}
for some
n
, showing that the action of
G
on
Y
is strongly faithful, where
Y=\{gy_{i}\mid g\in G,i\geq 0\}
.
∎

Proposition 6.2
.

Let
T
be a tree and let
G\leq\mathrm{Aut}(T)
be
(\mathrm{P})
-closed. Then the following are equivalent.

(i)

The permutation topology on
G
is Polish.

(ii)

There is a countable
G
-invariant subtree
T^{\prime}
of
T
on which
G
acts strongly faithfully relative to
T
.

(iii)

There is a countable
G
-invariant subtree
T^{\prime}
of
T
on which
G
acts faithfully and such that for each
v\in VT^{\prime}
, the action of
G_{v}
on
o^{-1}_{T^{\prime}}(v)
is strongly faithful relative to
o^{-1}_{T}(v)
.

(iv)

The local action diagram
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(G(v)))
for
(T,G)
is such that all the colour sets
X_{a}
are countable and the permutation groups
G(v)
are Polish, and there exists a countable cotree
\Gamma^{\prime}
of
\Delta
such that
G(v)=\{1\}
whenever
v\in V\Gamma\smallsetminus V\Gamma^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Suppose (i) holds. Then by Lemma
6.1
, there is a countable set
Y
of vertices on which
G
acts strongly faithfully relative to
VT
. We then see that the unique smallest subtree
T^{\prime}
of
T
containing
Y
is countable; by the construction,
T^{\prime}
is
G
-invariant and
G
acts strongly faithfully on
VT^{\prime}
relative to
VT
. Thus (i) implies (ii).

Suppose (ii) holds; let
v\in VT^{\prime}
and let
a\in o^{-1}(v)
such that
a\not\in AT^{\prime}
. Since
G
acts strongly faithfully on
T^{\prime}
, there are vertices
w_{1},\dots,w_{n}
in
T^{\prime}
such that any element of
G
that fixes
w_{1},\dots,w_{n}
also fixes
t(a)
. For each
w_{i}
, let
a_{i}
be the first arc on the shortest directed path from
v
to
w_{i}
, and let
g\in G_{v}
be such that
g
fixes
a_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n
. Using property
(\mathrm{P})
and induction on
n
, we see that there is
g^{\prime}\in G
such that
g^{\prime}
fixes each of the half-trees
T_{a_{i}}
pointwise, but has the same action as
g
on the vertices outside of
\bigcup^{n}_{i=1}T_{a_{i}}
. In particular,
g^{\prime}
fixes
w_{1},\dots,w_{n}
, so
g^{\prime}
also fixes
t(a)
; since
a\not\in AT^{\prime}
, we see that
t(a)
is not contained in
\bigcup^{n}_{i=1}T_{a_{i}}
, from which it follows that
g
also fixes
t(a)
, and hence
g
fixes
a
. This proves that relative to the action on
o^{-1}_{T}(v)
, the action of
G_{v}
on
o^{-1}_{T^{\prime}}(v)
is strongly faithful. Thus (ii) implies (iii).

Suppose (iii) holds. Then every vertex stabiliser in
G
also has countable orbits on
T
, ensuring that the colour sets
X_{a}
are countable. The countable
G
-invariant subtree
T^{\prime}
gives rise to a countable cotree
\Gamma^{\prime}
. Consider
v_{0}\in VT\smallsetminus VT^{\prime}
. Then there is a unique shortest path
(v_{0},\dots,v_{n})
from
v_{0}
to
T^{\prime}
; in particular,
G_{v_{0}}
fixes the arc
a
from
v_{0}
to
v_{1}
. Then
T^{\prime}
is contained in the half-tree
T_{a}
, so
G_{a}
acts faithfully on
T_{a}
. By property
(\mathrm{P})
, it follows that the action of
G_{a}
on
T_{\overline{a}}
is trivial; in particular, since
G_{v_{0}}
also fixes
a
, the action of
G_{v_{0}}
on
T_{\overline{a}}
is trivial. Since
T_{\overline{a}}
contains all neighbours of
v_{0}
other than
v_{1}
, we conclude that
G_{v_{0}}
fixes
o^{-1}(v_{0})
pointwise. Thus
G(\pi(v_{0}))=\{1\}
and we see that
|X_{a}|=1
whenever
a\in o^{-1}(\pi(v_{0}))
. Now consider
v\in VT^{\prime}
; we see that the condition that the action of
G_{v}
on
o^{-1}_{T^{\prime}}(v)
is strongly faithful relative to
o^{-1}_{T}(v)
translates exactly to the condition that for the action of
G(\pi(v))
on
X_{\pi(v)}
, we have a strongly faithful action on the subset

Y=\bigsqcup_{a\in o^{-1}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}(\pi(v))}X_{a}

of
X
. We observe that
Y
is countable;
G(\pi(v))
is closed by definition, and hence Polish by Lemma
6.1
; in particular,
G(\pi(v))
has countable orbits, so
|X_{a}|\leq\aleph_{0}
for all
a\in o^{-1}(\pi(v))
. This completes the proof that (iii) implies (iv).

Suppose (iv) holds. Consider the construction of a
\Delta
-tree
T
starting from a root vertex
()
mapping to a base vertex
v_{0}\in V\Gamma^{\prime}
. The cotree
\Gamma^{\prime}
then gives rises to a
G
-invariant subtree
T^{\prime}
of
T
; the fact that both
\Gamma^{\prime}
and the colour sets
X_{a}
are countable ensures that
T^{\prime}
is countable. For each
v\in VT^{\prime}
, we add a countable union of
G_{v}
-orbits of neighbours of
v
to
T^{\prime}
to produce a new tree
T^{\prime\prime}
, such that the action of
G_{v}
on
o^{-1}_{T^{\prime\prime}}(v)
is strongly faithful relative to
o^{-1}_{T}(v)
; this is possible by Lemma
6.1
since
G(\pi(v))
is Polish, and we see that it can be done in such a way that
T^{\prime\prime}
is
G
-invariant. Now let
v_{0}\in VT\smallsetminus VT^{\prime\prime}
and let
(v_{0},\dots,v_{n})
be the shortest path from
v_{0}
to
T^{\prime}
. Let
a
be the arc from
v_{n}
to
v_{n-1}
. Our choice of
T^{\prime\prime}
ensures that there are arcs
a_{1},\dots,a_{m}\in o^{-1}_{T^{\prime\prime}}(v_{n})
such that given
g\in G_{v_{n}}
that fixes
a_{1},\dots,a_{m}
, or equivalently, given
g\in G
that fixes
t(a_{1}),\dots,t(a_{m}),v_{n}
, then
g
fixes
a
, and hence
g
fixes
v_{n-1}
. In turn, using the fact that the local actions for vertices outside of
\Gamma^{\prime}
are trivial, we see that
G_{v_{n-1}}
fixes each of the vertices
v_{0},\dots,v_{n-2}
. We have thus obtained a finite subset
\{t(a_{1}),\dots,t(a_{m}),v_{n}\}
of
VT^{\prime\prime}
whose pointwise stabiliser also fixes
v_{0}
. Since
v_{0}\in VT\smallsetminus VT^{\prime\prime}
was arbitrary, we conclude that
G
acts strongly faithfully on
T^{\prime\prime}
. It then follows by Lemma
6.1
that
G
is Polish. Thus (iv) implies (i) and the cycle of implications is complete.
∎

6.2
Locally compact
(\mathrm{P})
-closed groups

Here is the characterisation of local compactness of the
(\mathrm{P})
-closure.

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
T
be a tree and let
G\leq\mathrm{Aut}(T)
. Suppose that there is a unique minimal
G
-invariant subtree
T^{\prime}
, such that
|VT^{\prime}|\geq 3
. Then the following are equivalent.

(i)

The
(\mathrm{P})
-closure of
G
is locally compact.

(ii)

For all
a\in AT^{\prime}
, the stabiliser of
a
in the
(\mathrm{P})
-closure of
G
is compact.

(iii)

Let
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(G(v)))
be the local action diagram for
(T,G)
and let
\Gamma^{\prime}
be the unique smallest cotree of
\Delta
. Then for all
a\in A\Gamma
such that
\overline{a}\not\in O_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
, every
G(o(a))
-stabiliser of every point in
X_{a}
has finite orbits on
X_{v}
.

Proof.

Lemma
2.11
implies that
T^{\prime}
is the union of the axes of translation of
G
. Let
\Gamma=G\backslash T
and let
\pi:T\rightarrow\Gamma
be the quotient map.

Suppose that (i) holds. Then there is some finite set
B
of vertices of
T
, such that the pointwise stabiliser
H
of
B
in
G^{(\mathrm{P})}
is compact. Let
a\in AT^{\prime}
. Then
a
belongs to the axis of some translation
h\in G
; we can choose
h
so that
a
is oriented towards the repelling end of
h
. Then the half-trees
T_{h^{n}a}
form an increasing family whose union is
T
; thus there is some
n
such that
T_{h^{n}a}
contains
B
and
t(h^{n}a)\not\in B
.

Set
g=h^{n}
and consider the stabiliser of
ga
; we have

G^{(\mathrm{P})}_{ga}=\mathrm{rist}_{G}(T_{ga})\times\mathrm{rist}_{G}(T_{\overline{ga}}).

Our choice of
g
ensures that
B\subseteq T_{ga}
; thus
\mathrm{rist}_{G}(T_{\overline{ga}})\leq H
, so
\mathrm{rist}_{G}(T_{\overline{ga}})
is compact. In particular,
G^{(\mathrm{P})}_{ga}
has finite orbits on
T_{\overline{ga}}
. After conjugating by
g^{-1}
we see that
G^{(\mathrm{P})}_{a}
has finite orbits on the half-tree
T_{\overline{a}}
. A similar argument using
\overline{a}
in place of
a
shows that
G^{(\mathrm{P})}_{a}=G^{(\mathrm{P})}_{\overline{a}}
also has finite orbits on the complementary half-tree
T_{a}
, so
a
satisfies (ii). Thus (i) implies (ii). It is immediately clear that (ii) implies (i), so (i) and (ii) are equivalent.

Suppose (ii) holds; note that the unique smallest cotree
\Gamma^{\prime}
of the local action diagram is the image of
T^{\prime}
. Let
v\in VT
and
a\in o^{-1}(v)
such that
\overline{\pi(a)}\not\in O_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
. If
\pi(a)\not\in O_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
, then
\pi(a)\in A\Gamma^{\prime}
and it is clear from (ii) that
G_{a}
has finite orbits on
VT
. Otherwise, the fact that
\pi(a)\in O_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
ensures that
a
points towards
T^{\prime}
. By property
(\mathrm{P})
the action of
G^{(\mathrm{P})}_{a}
on
T_{\overline{a}}
is then the same as the action of the pointwise fixator of
T_{a}
on
T_{\overline{a}}
; since
T^{\prime}\subseteq T_{a}
, it follows from (ii) that
G_{a}
has finite orbits on
T_{\overline{a}}
, and in particular on
o^{-1}(v)
. It follows that in the local action diagram, the action of
G(\pi(v))
on
X_{v}
is such that every point stabiliser has finite orbits. Thus (ii) implies (iii).

Suppose (iii) holds. We can regard
G^{(\mathrm{P})}
as the universal group of
\Delta
and we see that
\Gamma^{\prime}=\pi(T^{\prime})
.

Consider a path
(v_{0},\dots,v_{n})
in
VT
; let
a_{i}
be the arc from
v_{i-1}
to
v_{i}
and suppose that
a_{1}\in AT^{\prime}
. We then see that each arc
a_{i}
is either contained in
T^{\prime}
or points away from it, so for all
i
we have
\pi(a_{i})\not\in O_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
. Thus for each
i\geq 0
, the stabiliser in
G(v_{i})
of any point in
X_{\overline{\pi(a_{i})}}
has finite orbits on
X_{\pi(v_{i})}
. Translating this information back to the tree: in the action of the stabiliser in
G^{(\mathrm{P})}
of
\overline{a_{i}}
, or equivalently of
a_{i}
, the orbit of
v_{i+1}
is finite. We conclude that in the action of
G^{(\mathrm{P})}_{a_{1}}
, the orbit of
v_{n}
is finite. Given the freedom of choice of the path
(v_{0},\dots,v_{n})
, we conclude that
H=G^{(\mathrm{P})}_{a_{1}}
has finite orbits on the half-tree
T_{a_{1}}
; by replacing
a_{1}
with
\overline{a_{1}}
, a similar argument shows that
H
has finite orbits on the complementary half-tree
T_{\overline{a_{1}}}
. Since
H
is also closed in
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
, it is compact. Moreover,
a_{1}
can be chosen to be any arc of
AT^{\prime}
; thus every stabiliser in
G^{(\mathrm{P})}
of an arc of
T^{\prime}
is compact. Thus (iii) implies (ii), completing the proof that all three statements are equivalent.
∎

Corollary 6.4
.

Let
T
be a tree and let
G
be a closed subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
that acts with translation and let
G^{(\mathrm{P})}
be the
(\mathrm{P})
-closure of
G
acting on
T
. Suppose that
G^{(\mathrm{P})}
is locally compact, and let
T^{\prime}
be a
G
-invariant subtree of
T
. Then the kernel of the action of
G
on
T^{\prime}
is compact, and
G
and
G^{(\mathrm{P})}
are both strongly closed.

Proof.

Since
G
acts with translation we are in the situation of Proposition
6.3
: there is a unique
G
-invariant subtree
T^{\prime\prime}
, such that
|VT^{\prime\prime}|\geq 3
. In particular,
T^{\prime\prime}\subseteq T^{\prime}
. Since
G^{(\mathrm{P})}
is locally compact, it follows from Proposition
6.3
that
G^{(\mathrm{P})}
has a compact arc stabiliser, for some arc
a\in AT^{\prime\prime}\subseteq AT
; since
G
is closed, the subgroup
G_{a}=G\cap G^{(\mathrm{P})}_{a}
is also compact. Thus by Lemma
2.20
both
G
and
G^{(\mathrm{P})}
are strongly closed as subgroups of
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
.

Let
K
be the kernel of the action of
G
on
T^{\prime}
. Then
K
is a closed subgroup of
G_{a}
, so it is compact.
∎

When
G
is locally compact, it is natural to ask if
G
is compactly generated. As in Proposition
6.3
, for simplicity we will avoid the case when
G
fixes a vertex or preserves an undirected edge. Excluding these degenerate cases, compact generation of
G
is easily seen to be equivalent to compact generation of
G^{(\mathrm{P})}
, so the question of whether or not
G
is compactly generated can be reduced to the local action diagram.

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
T
be a tree and let
G\leq\mathrm{Aut}(T)
be closed with unbounded action. Let
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(G(v)))
be the local action diagram. Suppose that there exists
a\in AT
for which
G_{a}
is compact. Then
G
and
G^{(\mathrm{P})}
are locally compact, and the following are equivalent.

(i)

G
is compactly generated;

(ii)

G^{(\mathrm{P})}
is compactly generated;

(iii)

there is a unique smallest
G
-invariant subtree
T^{\prime}
such that
G
has finitely many orbits on
VT^{\prime}\sqcup AT^{\prime}
and
G_{v}
is compactly generated for each
v\in VT^{\prime}
;

(iv)

there is a unique smallest cotree
\Gamma^{\prime}
of
\Delta
such that
\Gamma^{\prime}
is finite and
G(v)
is compactly generated for each
v\in V\Gamma^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Since
G_{a}
is open in
G
, we see that
G
is locally compact. We also see that
G_{a}
has finite orbits on
VT
; since
G^{(\mathrm{P})}_{a}
is closed and has the same orbits it follows that
G^{(\mathrm{P})}_{a}
is compact, and hence
G^{(\mathrm{P})}
is locally compact. Since
G^{(\mathrm{P})}
has the same orbits on
AT
as
G
does, we have
G^{(\mathrm{P})}=GG^{(\mathrm{P})}_{a}
, so
G
is cocompact in
G^{(\mathrm{P})}
. Thus
G
is compactly generated if and only if
G^{(\mathrm{P})}
is compactly generated, that is, (i) and (ii) are equivalent.

Suppose that the action is horocyclic with unique fixed end
\xi
. We see that
G
preserves every horoball around
\xi
; in particular, there is no minimal
G
-invariant subtree, so (iii) is false. For the same reason every cotree of
\Delta
is infinite, so (iv) is false. We also see that (i) and (ii) are false by Corollary
2.7
. So if the action is horocyclic then (i)–(iv) are all false, which is consistent with them being equivalent.

For the remainder of the proof we may suppose that the action is not horocyclic. It follows that
G
acts with translation, and hence there is a unique smallest
G
-invariant subtree
T^{\prime}
of
T
, which is the union of the axes of translations in
T
.

Suppose (i) holds. Since
G
is compactly generated, it is generated by finitely many cosets of any vertex stabiliser; hence by Lemma
2.13
,
G
has finitely many orbits on
VT^{\prime}\sqcup AT^{\prime}
. The rest of the proof that (i) implies (iii) is
[
7
, Proposition 4.1]
, however for clarity we give a more elementary proof using Bass–Serre theory.

Write
\Gamma^{i}
for the quotient graph of the action of
G
on the inversion-free subdivision of
T^{\prime}
. Since
G^{(\mathrm{P})}
is locally compact, Proposition
6.3
implies that
G^{(\mathrm{P})}_{e}
is compact for all
e\in AT^{\prime}
, and hence
G
acts on
T^{\prime}
with compact kernel; let
G^{*}
be the group defined by this action. Recall the decomposition of
G^{*}
given by Theorem
2.28
:

\frac{F(E)\ast\Asterisk_{v\in VT^{*}}G^{*}_{v}}{\langle\langle s_{a}\tau_{a}(g)s_{\overline{a}}\tau_{\overline{a}}(g)^{-1}\;(a\in E,g\in G_{a}),\;s_{a}s_{\overline{a}}\;(a\in E),\;s_{a}\;(a\in AT^{*})\rangle\rangle}.

where now
T^{*}
is a lift in
T^{\prime}
of a maximal subtree of
\Gamma^{i}
and
E
is a lift of the arcs of
\Gamma^{i}
such that every arc in
E
is incident with
T^{*}
. We remark that this decomposition is also well-behaved with respect to the topology of
G^{*}
, and hence of
G
, since the factors are amalgamated along open subgroups.

We claim that
G_{v}
is compactly generated for all
v\in VT^{\prime}
; it is enough to show that
G^{*}_{v}
is compactly generated for each
v\in VT^{*}
. Fix
v\in VT^{*}
. We have a compactly generated open subgroup
H_{0}
of
G^{*}_{v}
generated by
\tau_{a}(G^{*}_{a})
for all
a\in E
such that
t(\pi_{(T^{\prime},G)}(a))=\pi_{(T^{\prime},G)}(v)
. In particular, in the expression for
G^{*}
, every element of
G^{*}_{v}
that is amalgamated with other vertex groups is contained in
H_{0}
.

Now write
G^{*}_{v}
as a directed union
\bigcup_{i\in I}H_{i}
, where each
H_{i}
is compactly generated and
0
is the least element of
I
, and recall the normal form theorem for graphs of groups (Theorem
2.30
). Let
K_{i}
be the set of elements of
G^{*}
expressible as a reduced word (including the empty word), such that all letters taken from
G^{*}_{v}
belong to
H_{i}
. Then
G^{*}=\bigcup_{i\in I}K_{i}
; since
G^{*}
is compactly generated and
H_{0}
is open in
G^{*}
, in fact
G^{*}=K_{i}
for some
i
. In particular, every
g\in G^{*}_{v}
is expressible as a reduced word using
F(E)
,
H_{i}
and
G^{*}_{v^{\prime}}
for vertices
v^{\prime}\in VT^{*}
other than
v
. Given the reduction rules for words in a graph of groups, we conclude that
g\in H_{i}
. Thus
G^{*}_{v}=H_{i}
, showing that
G^{*}_{v}
is compactly generated as required. This completes the proof that (i) implies (iii).

Suppose now that (iii) holds. Then
G^{*}_{v}
is also compactly generated for each vertex
v
of the inversion-free subdivision of
T^{\prime}
, and hence we see from the free product decomposition that
G^{*}
is compactly generated. Since
G
acts on
T^{\prime}
with compact kernel it follows that
G
is also compactly generated. Thus (iii) implies (i) and hence (i), (ii) and (iii) are equivalent.

For (iv), note that
\Gamma^{\prime}
is finite if and only if
G
has finitely many orbits on
VT^{\prime}\sqcup AT^{\prime}
. Since
G
is locally compact, we know by Proposition
6.3
that
G^{(\mathrm{P})}
has compact arc stabilisers for all arcs
AT^{\prime}
; since
G
is closed the groups
G_{a}=G\cap G^{(\mathrm{P})}_{a}
are compact for all
a\in AT^{\prime}
. Using Lemma
2.20
we deduce that, given
v\in V\Gamma^{\prime}
and the chosen representative
v^{*}
of
\pi^{-1}_{(T^{\prime},G)}(v)
, the local action map
\theta:G_{v^{*}}\rightarrow G(v)
is a continuous closed surjective map with compact kernel. It follows that
G_{v^{*}}
is compactly generated if and only if
G(v)
is compactly generated, and in particular, (iii) and (iv) are equivalent. This completes the proof that (i)–(iv) are equivalent.
∎

6.3
Proofs of theorems from the introduction

We can now deduce Theorem
1.9
and Corollary
1.10
as special cases of the previous propositions.

Proof of Theorem
1.9
.

Let
T
be the defining tree of
\mathbf{U}(\Delta)
.

Suppose (i) holds. Then by Proposition
6.3
, since
\mathbf{U}(\Delta)
is locally compact and the action is geometrically dense, all arc stabilisers of
\mathbf{U}(\Delta)
acting on
T
are compact. By Proposition
6.5
and the fact that
\mathbf{U}(\Delta)
is compactly generated, we see that
\Gamma
is finite and
G(v)
is compactly generated for all
v\in V\Gamma
. The fact that arc stabilisers are compact implies that each of the groups
G(v)
is subdegree-finite. Finally,
\Delta
is irreducible by Theorem
1.4
. Thus (i) implies (ii).

Conversely, suppose (ii) holds. Since
\Delta
is irreducible,
(T,\mathbf{U}(\Delta))
is geometrically dense by Theorem
1.4
. Since the local actions are all closed and subdegree-finite,
\mathbf{U}(\Delta)
has compact arc stabilisers and hence is locally compact. We see that
\mathbf{U}(\Delta)
is compactly generated by Proposition
6.5
. Thus (ii) implies (i).

Suppose (i) and (ii) hold. Taking
v\in V\Gamma
, then as a permutation group,
G(v)=\overline{G(v)}
has finitely many orbits and is a compactly generated locally compact group; it follows that
X_{v}
is countable. Since
\Gamma
is finite we conclude that
T
is countable. Thus
\mathrm{Sym}(VT\sqcup AT)
is Polish, and hence its closed subgroup
\mathbf{U}(\Delta)
is Polish.
∎

Proof of Corollary
1.10
.

As in the proof of Theorem
1.8
we may assume that the action of
G
on
T
is parity-preserving, and in particular inversion-free: if
G
is not parity-preserving, then (i) and (ii) are both false. (The case
G=\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
is incompatible with (i) since groups in
\mathscr{S}
are not discrete, otherwise the argument is the same as in the proof of Theorem
1.8
.)

Suppose (i) holds. By hypothesis the action does not fix a vertex, and the fact that
G
is nondiscrete and topologically simple rules out actions that are of lineal or focal type
(by Lemma
2.10
). Corollary
2.7
then rules out a horocyclic action. By process of elimination, the action is of general type, with a unique smallest
G
-invariant subtree
T^{\prime}
on which the action of
G
is faithful and geometrically dense.
In fact the action of
G
on
T^{\prime}
is strongly faithful: the topology of
G
is already
\sigma
-compact, so there is no coarser locally compact group topology on
G
.
Thus (via Lemma
2.19
) we can regard
G
as a
(\mathrm{P})
-closed, hence closed, subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(T^{\prime})
as a topological group.
Since
G
is a closed and geometrically dense subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(T^{\prime})
, we immediately have that
G
is strongly closed on
T^{\prime}
. Furthermore, the group
G^{+}
generated by arc stabilisers is an open normal subgroup, and since
G
is nondiscrete and topologically simple,
G^{+}=G
. Thus in fact by Tits’ Theorem
2.22
,
G
is abstractly simple.
We then conclude by Theorem
1.8
that
\Delta
is irreducible,
\Gamma
is a tree, and each of the groups
G(v)
is closed and generated by point stabilisers. By Theorem
1.9
,
\Gamma
is finite and each of the groups
G(v)
is compactly generated and subdegree-finite. For every leaf
v
of
\Gamma
, we have
X_{v}=X_{a}
for the unique arc
a\in o^{-1}(v)
, and so
X_{v}
is a single
G(v)
orbit. By Corollary
1.6
, for every leaf
v
of
\Gamma
we have
|X_{v}|>1
and hence
G(v)\neq\{1\}
. This completes the proof that (i) implies (ii).

Conversely, suppose that (ii) holds; as in Theorem
1.8
we have
(T^{\prime},G)=(T^{\prime},\mathbf{U}(\Delta))
, so as a topological group we may identify
G
with the closed subgroup
\mathbf{U}(\Delta)
of
\mathrm{Aut}(T^{\prime})
. For every leaf
v
of
\Gamma
, since
G(v)\neq\{1\}
we have
|X_{v}|>1
, and hence
\Delta
is irreducible via Corollary
1.6
.
We deduce via Theorem
1.8
that
G
is a nondiscrete simple group, and it is clear that
G
does not fix any vertex of
T
. By Theorem
1.9
,
\mathbf{U}(\Delta)
is compactly generated and locally compact. Thus
G\in\mathscr{S}
, showing that (ii) implies (i).
∎

7
Examples

7.1
Vertex-transitive actions on trees of small degree

Isomorphism types of local action diagrams can be used to classify isomorphism types of
(\mathrm{P})
-closed groups acting on trees, via the one-to-one correspondence we have developed.
This classification is most useful for families of groups acting on trees where the associated local action diagram is ‘small’.

Let us consider the special case of a vertex-transitive
(\mathrm{P})
-closed group
G
acting on a locally finite tree
T
. The tree is necessarily regular, of some degree
d
; let us write
T=T_{d}
, to indicate that
T
is a regular tree of degree
d
. In the local action diagram
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{a}),G(v))
for
(T,G)
, the graph
\Gamma
has a single vertex
v
; the set
X_{v}
has size
d
and there is a single permutation group
G(v)
