, which is defined on
X_{v}
. The set
\{X_{a}\mid a\in A\Gamma\}
is the partition of
X_{v}
into
G(v)
-orbits. The only remaining piece of information in the local action diagram is the edge-reversal map
r
on
\Gamma
; since there is only one vertex, this can be any permutation of
A\Gamma
whose square is the identity.

Thus, up to conjugacy in
\mathrm{Aut}(T_{d})
, there are only finitely many vertex-transitive
(\mathrm{P})
-closed subgroups of
\mathrm{Aut}(T_{d})
. The relevant conjugacy classes are in one-to-one correspondence with the set
\mathcal{V}_{d}
of equivalence classes of pairs
(H,r)
, where
H
is a subgroup of
\mathrm{Sym}(d)
and
r
is an
orbit pairing
for
H
, meaning a permutation of the set
H\backslash[d]
of
H
-orbits whose square is the identity. Here we say two pairs
(H_{1},r_{1})
and
(H_{2},r_{2})
are equivalent if there is
g\in\mathrm{Sym}(d)
such that
gH_{1}g^{-1}=H_{2}
and the map
g^{\prime}:H_{1}\backslash[d]\rightarrow H_{2}\backslash[d]
induced by
g
satisfies
g^{\prime}r_{1}=r_{2}g^{\prime}
. Write
\mathbf{U}(H,r)
for the subgroup
\mathbf{U}(\Delta)\leq\mathrm{Aut}(T_{d})
, where
\Delta
is the local action diagram associated to
(H,r)
(here
\mathbf{U}(H,r)
should be understood as specified up to conjugacy in
\mathrm{Aut}(T_{d})
). The orbit pairing captures the difference between the number of arc-orbits of
\mathbf{U}(H,r)
and the number of edge-orbits: the arc-orbits of
\mathbf{U}(H,r)
correspond to orbits of
H
, whereas the edge-orbits correspond to orbits of
r
on
H\backslash[d]
. The orbits of
H
fixed by
r
correspond to those arc-orbits of
\mathbf{U}(H,r)
that are closed under the reverse map on
T_{d}
, in other words, those arcs that are reversed by some element of
\mathbf{U}(H,r)
. We see that
\mathbf{U}(H,r)
fixes an end exactly in the following situation:
H
has a fixed point and acts transitively on the remaining points, and the orbit pairing is nontrivial.

Let us first deal with the special case that
H
is a free permutation group, that is, point stabilisers of
H
are trivial. In this case,
\mathbf{U}(H,r)
acts freely on the arcs of
T_{d}
, and it follows by Theorem
2.28
that it can be expressed as a free product of copies of
H
,
\mathbb{Z}
and
C_{2}
with no amalgamation. Specifically, writing
K^{\ast n}
to mean a free product of
n
copies of
K
, we have

\mathbf{U}(H,r)\cong H\ast C^{\ast a}_{2}\ast\mathbb{Z}^{\ast b}

where
a
is the number of fixed points of
r
(in other words, the number of reversible arc orbits of
\mathbf{U}(H,r)
on the tree) and
b
is the number of nontrivial orbits of
r
. In fact, in this case we see that every group acting on
T_{d}
with the same local action diagram as
\mathbf{U}(H,r)
is
\mathrm{Aut}(T_{d})
-conjugate.

For small values of
d
, it is feasible to list the conjugacy classes of vertex-transitive
(\mathrm{P})
-closed subgroups of
\mathrm{Aut}(T_{d})
; we will describe the list for
d\leq 5
. The pairs
(H,r)
given below should be understood as being taken up to equivalence. Where it is unambiguous we will indicate the orbit pairing simply by the size of the paired orbits, so for instance
[12,22]
indicates an orbit pairing where an orbit of size
1
is paired with an orbit of size
2
, another orbit of size
2
is paired with a third orbit of size
2
, and all other orbits are fixed by
r
.
This notation is especially convenient when
d\leq 5
, as in this case, for any
H\leq\mathrm{Sym}(d)
, all the orbits of
H
of the same size lie in a single orbit of the normaliser of
H
. For brevity we will write
(H,\mathrm{id})
as
(H)
.
Write
S_{n}:=\mathrm{Sym}(n)
,
A_{n}=\mathrm{Alt}(n)
,
C_{n}
for a cyclic group of order
n
and
D_{n}
for a dihedral group of order
n
. We first recall the conjugacy classes of subgroups of
S_{d}
.

In
S_{0}
and
S_{1}
:
there is only the trivial group
1
.

In
S_{2}
:
there are two subgroups, namely
1
and
S_{2}
itself. Henceforth,
S_{2}
is the group of order
2
acting as a local action at a vertex of degree
2
, whereas
C_{2}
(without further decoration) will represent an edge-reversing involution.

In
S_{3}
:
there are four conjugacy classes of subgroup, namely:
1
; one class of subgroup of order
2
that we denote as
C_{2+1}
; the alternating group
A_{3}=C_{3}
; and
S_{3}
itself.

In
S_{4}
:
there are 11 conjugacy classes of subgroup, namely: the trivial group
1
; two classes
C^{-}_{2}
and
C^{+}_{2}
of subgroup of order
2
(acting with two and zero fixed points respectively); one class each of cyclic subgroups
C_{3+1}
and
C_{4}
of orders
3
and
4
; two classes
V^{-}
and
V^{+}
of the Klein
4
-group (the plus sign denoting the regular action, and the minus sign the faithful intransitive action); one class of point stabilisers
S_{3+1}
; one class of the dihedral group
D_{8}
of order
8
; the alternating group
A_{4}
; and the symmetric group itself
S_{4}
.

In
S_{5}
:
there are 19 conjugacy classes of subgroup of
S_{5}
. These are as follows. There are
11
classes of subgroup that fix a point (corresponding to conjugacy classes of subgroup of
S_{4}
). There are three classes of subgroup with orbit partition
(3,2)
, as follows: cyclic group
C_{3+2}
of order
6
; ‘twisted
S_{3}
’, viz.
S^{*}_{3}=\langle(1,2,3),(1,2)(4,5)\rangle
; direct product
S_{3+2}=S_{3}\times S_{2}
. There are five classes of transitive subgroup, as follows: cyclic group
C_{5}
of order
5
; dihedral group
D_{10}
of order
10
; general affine group
GA(1,5)
, a group of order
20
; alternating group
A_{5}
;
S_{5}
itself.

The number of conjugacy classes of
Burger–Mozes
subgroup in
\mathrm{Aut}(T_{d})
is thus
1,1,2,4,11,19
for
d=0,1,2,3,4,5
respectively. However, due to nontrivial orbit pairings, the total number of conjugacy classes of
(\mathrm{P})
-closed subgroup in
\mathrm{Aut}(T_{d})
is larger for
d\geq 2
. Indeed, for
d=2,3,4,5
there are, respectively, a total of
3,6,19,40
conjugacy classes (see Tables
2
and
3
).

Given
G=\mathbf{U}(H,r)
, we can determine the quotient
G/G^{+}
as the fundamental group of a graph of groups with trivial edge groups by passing to the inversion-free subdivision of the action, as in Corollary
4.14
. In particular,
G/G^{+}
can easily be written as a free product with no amalgamation.

In the tables below, a blank entry means a repeat of the previous entry. Note that the same group may appear several times, but with different actions on the tree. ‘l.p.c.’ stands for ‘local prime content’, in other words, the primes
p
such that the
p
-Sylow subgroup of a compact open subgroup of
\mathbf{U}(H,r)
is infinite; in the present context, the local prime content is empty if and only if
\mathbf{U}(H,r)
is discrete.

5

We should remember that the list of groups we have obtained so far is up to equivalence of action on the tree, not up to isomorphism as groups. For example, the group
\mathbb{Z}\ast C_{2}
appears as a vertex-transitive
(\mathrm{P})
-closed subgroup of both
\mathrm{Aut}(T_{3})
and
\mathrm{Aut}(T_{4})
, but clearly the actions are not equivalent. It is not clear what group isomorphisms could exist between the nondiscrete groups in the list (that is, all the groups listed such that the local action is not free).

Recall the class
\mathscr{S}
of t.d.l.c. groups that are compactly generated, nondiscrete and topologically simple. If
G
is a vertex-transitive
(\mathrm{P})
-closed subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(T_{d})
for
d\in\mathbb{N}
, then
G^{+}
is compactly generated if and only if
G^{+}\backslash T
is finite, which means that
G/G^{+}
must be finite;
G^{+}
is nondiscrete if and only if the local prime content is nonempty.
Applying Corollary
1.10
, we see that of the 70 entries in the tables, only seven have
G^{+}\in\mathscr{S}
, namely the Burger–Mozes groups
\mathbf{U}(F)
with transitive local action for
F\in\{S_{3},A_{4},S_{4},D_{10},GA(1,5),A_{5},S_{5}\}
. However, in a further 36 cases (those with nontrivial local prime content where
G
does not fix an end),
G^{+}
is nondiscrete and simple, but fails to be compactly generated. The latter simple groups have a complicated structure in general, which may merit further investigation. For instance, by
[
5
, Theorem 1.8]
, in every nondiscrete Burger–Mozes group there is a compactly generated closed subgroup
K
(where without loss of generality
K\leq G^{+}
), and a discrete normal subgroup
D
of
K
, such that
K/D\in\mathscr{S}
. We do not know if any of these 36 noncompactly generated simple groups
G^{+}
are isomorphic to one another as abstract or topological groups.

One motivation for studying vertex-transitive groups acting on trees of small degree is to understand compactly generated t.d.l.c. groups in terms of their degree. The
degree

\deg(G)
of a compactly generated t.d.l.c. group
G
is the smallest degree of a Cayley–Abels graph for
G
. The degree
0
groups are the compact groups and the degree
2
groups are the compact-by-cyclic groups, but even for degree
3
the structure is not well-understood, except that the degree must be larger than the maximum of the local prime content. What can be said in general, given a group
G
acting with kernel
K
on a Cayley–Abels graph
\Gamma
of minimal degree
d
, is that the action lifts to an action of a group
\widetilde{G}
acting vertex-transitively on
T_{d}
, with an associated homomorphism
\theta:\widetilde{G}\rightarrow G/K
with discrete kernel. We can then consider the
(\mathrm{P})
-closure
\widetilde{G}^{(\mathrm{P})}
as a first step towards understanding
\widetilde{G}
and the original group
G
. Both the local action and the orbit pairing for
\widetilde{G}^{(\mathrm{P})}
come from the action of
G
on
\Gamma
.

7.2
Simple vertex-transitive graphs with vertex connectivity one

We illustrate how to use our theory of local action diagrams to describe all automorphism groups of simple vertex-transitive graphs with vertex connectivity one.

Let
\Gamma
be a simple connected graph; we shall call
\Gamma

trivial
if it consists of a single vertex and no edges. Recall that
\Gamma
has
vertex connectivity one
(also
connectivity one
and
1
-connected
) if there is a vertex
v
in
\Gamma
such that the induced graph
\Gamma\smallsetminus\{v\}
(which arises by removing the vertex
v
from
\Gamma
, together with all edges in
\Gamma
containing
v
) is not connected.
Such a vertex
v
is called a
cut vertex
of
\Gamma
. A connected graph with no cut vertices is
2
-connected
(here we consider the complete graph on two vertices
K_{2}
to be
2
-connected, but a single vertex not to be
2
-connected).
If
\Gamma
has connectivity one, then the maximal
2
-connected subgraphs of
\Gamma
are called the
lobes
(also sometimes called
blocks
) of
\Gamma
. For example, the lobes of the infinite
3
-regular tree
T_{3}
are pairs of adjacent vertices together with the edge between them; each lobe of
T_{3}
is thus isomorphic to
K_{2}
.

Vertex-transitive graphs with connectivity one were completely described by H. A. Jung and M. E. Watkins in
[
10
]
. We adopt and extend their notation here.
Suppose
\Gamma
has connectivity one and let
L
be the set of lobes of
\Gamma
, with
\{L_{i}:i\in I\}
denoting the set of isomorphism classes of
L
.
In each
L_{i}
we fix some distinguished element
\hat{\Lambda}_{i}
and for each
\Lambda\in L_{i}
we fix some isomorphism
\theta_{\Lambda}:\hat{\Lambda}_{i}\rightarrow\Lambda
. For each
i\in I
let
\{\hat{\Lambda}_{i}^{(j)}:j\in J_{i}\}
be a decomposition of
V\hat{\Lambda}_{i}
into
\mathrm{Aut}(\hat{\Lambda}_{i})
-orbits. Now for any
\Lambda\in L_{i}
and
j\in J_{i}
, let
\Lambda^{(j)}:=\theta_{\Lambda}(\hat{\Lambda}_{i}^{(j)})
.
If
\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}\in L_{i}
with
\tau:\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda^{\prime}
an isomorphism, then
for each
j\in J_{i}
we have
\tau(\Lambda^{(j)})=\Lambda^{\prime(j)}
.
Thus, for each
i\in I
and
\Lambda\in L_{i}
the set
\{\Lambda^{(j)}:j\in J_{i}\}
is a decomposition of
V\Lambda
into
\mathrm{Aut}(\Lambda)
-orbits.

One can easily verify that any two pairs
(v,\Lambda),(v^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime})
, consisting of vertices
v,v^{\prime}\in V\Gamma
and lobes
\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}
of
\Gamma
with
v\in V\Lambda
and
v^{\prime}\in V\Lambda^{\prime}
, are isomorphic as rooted graphs if and only if there exists
i\in I
and
j\in J_{i}
such that
\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}\in L_{i}
and
v\in\Lambda^{(j)}
and
v^{\prime}\in\Lambda^{\prime(j)}
.

Now for any vertex
v\in V\Gamma
and
i\in I
and
j\in J_{i}
, define
L_{i}^{(j)}(v):=\{\Lambda\in L_{i}:v\in\Lambda^{(j)}\}
and

AL_{i}^{(j)}(v):=\{A\Lambda:\Lambda\in L_{i}^{(j)}(v)\}
and
m_{i}^{(j)}(v):=|L_{i}^{(j)}(v)|
.

In
[
10
, Lemma 3.1, Theorem 3.2 and proof]
Jung and Watkins show the following.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
\Gamma
be a simple connected graph with vertex connectivity one. Then:

(i)

\Gamma
is vertex-transitive if and only if the functions
m_{i}^{(j)}
are constant on
V\Gamma
.

(ii)

If
\Gamma
is vertex-transitive, and
v_{1},v_{2}
(resp.
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}
) are vertices (resp. lobes) of
\Gamma
with
v_{i}\in\Lambda_{i}
for
i=1,2
, then any isomorphism of rooted graphs between
(v_{1},\Lambda_{1})
and
(v_{2},\Lambda_{2})
extends to an automorphism of
\Gamma
.

Now suppose that
\Gamma
is nontrivial, vertex-transitive with connectivity one.
Such a graph is infinite with each vertex
v
lying in at least two lobes.
Notice that

v
lies in precisely
\sum_{i\in I,j\in J_{i}}m_{i}^{(j)}(v)
many lobes.
The graph
\Gamma
is tree-like, and this can be seen by considering the
block-cut-vertex tree
of
\Gamma
(also called a
structure tree
)
T
, defined as follows:
VT:=V\Gamma\sqcup L
and
ET:=\{\{v,\Lambda\}:v\in V\Gamma\text{ and }\Lambda\in L\text{ and }v\in V\Lambda\}
(it is easy to see that such a graph is indeed a tree). We call elements in
V\Gamma\subseteq VT
(resp.
L\subseteq VT
) the
graph vertices
(resp.
lobe vertices
) of
T
.
Every lobe vertex lies between two graph vertices, so the automorphism group
\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma)
acts faithfully on
T
. We can use this action on
T
to describe
\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma)
in the language of local action diagrams.

Let
T
be the structure tree of
\Gamma

and let
G
be the subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
induced by the action of
\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma)
on its structure tree
T
. In (A
1
)–(A
4
) below we determine the associated local action diagram
\Delta
of
G
in its action on
T
; we then equip
T
with the structure of a
\Delta
-tree in the usual way.

(A1)

The underlying graph
\Gamma^{\prime}
of the local action diagram is
G\backslash T
. By Theorem
7.1
, any two isomorphic lobes lie in the same
G
-orbit. Thus the vertex set of
G\backslash T
is
\{v^{*}\}\cup\{L_{i}:i\in I\}
where
v^{*}
is some representative element from
V\Gamma
. For convenience, write
m_{i,j}:=m_{i}^{(j)}(v^{*})
.

(A2)

Again by Theorem
7.1
, the undirected edges in
\Gamma^{\prime}
correspond to orbits of rooted graphs
(v,\Lambda)
, where
\Lambda\in L
and
v\in V\Lambda\subseteq V\Gamma
. As noted above, these orbits correspond precisely to isomorphism classes of rooted graphs, with
(v,\Lambda)
and
(v^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime})
being isomorphic if and only if there exists
i\in I
and
j\in J_{i}
such that
\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}\in L_{i}
and
v\in\Lambda^{(j)}
and
v^{\prime}\in\Lambda^{\prime(j)}
. Choose a representative vertex
v_{i}^{(j)}
from each orbit
\hat{\Lambda}_{i}^{(j)}
. The undirected edges in
\Gamma^{\prime}
between
v^{*}
and each
L_{i}
thus correspond to elements of the set
\{(v_{i}^{(j)},\hat{\Lambda}_{i}):j\in J_{i}\}
.

(A3)

For all
\Gamma^{\prime}
arcs
a\in o^{-1}(v^{*})
there is some
i\in I
and
j\in J_{i}
such that
a
corresponds to
(v_{i}^{(j)},\hat{\Lambda}_{i})
. The colour set
X_{a}
is now easy to picture: it consists of all lobes
\Lambda
in
\Gamma
that contain
v^{*}
and lie in
L_{i}
such that
v^{*}
lies in the orbit
\Lambda^{(j)}
. In our notation,
X_{a}=L_{i}^{(j)}(v^{*})
.
For the reverse arcs, the colour set is
X_{L_{i}}=V\hat{\Lambda}_{i}
, naturally partitioned according to the
\mathrm{Aut}(\hat{\Lambda}_{i})
-orbits on
V\hat{\Lambda}_{i}
.

(A4)

We now describe the vertex groups in
\Gamma^{\prime}
. Clearly, for each lobe vertex
L_{i}
in
V\Gamma^{\prime}
, we have
X_{L_{i}}=V\hat{\Lambda}_{i}
with
G(L_{i})=\mathrm{Aut}(\hat{\Lambda}_{i})
. So, it remains for us to determine
G(v^{*})
for the single graph vertex
v^{*}
in
\Gamma^{\prime}
.
For each
i\in I
and
j\in J_{i}
, observe that
G_{v^{*}}
induces the symmetric group
S_{m_{i,j}}
on
L_{i}^{(j)}(v^{*})
.
As
G
is the automorphism group of
\Gamma
, there are no further restrictions on
G_{v^{*}}
, and thus
G_{v^{*}}
induces
S_{m_{i,j}}
on
X_{a}
.
For arcs
a,b
the actions of
G_{v^{*}}
on disjoint subsets
X_{a},X_{b}
of
X_{v^{*}}
are independent, and so
G(v^{*})
is the direct product,

\prod_{i\in I,j\in J_{i}}S_{m_{i,j}},

acting on
X_{v^{*}}=\bigsqcup X_{a}
, where the
(i,j)
-th component in the above product acts on
X_{a}
for the arc
a
corresponding to
(v_{i}^{(j)},\hat{\Lambda}_{i})
.

We have thus described the local action diagram
\Delta(T,G)
of
G
, and hence also
\mathbf{T}
, the associated
\Delta
-tree structure on
T
. By Theorem
3.10
, the universal group

\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{T},(G(v)))
is the
(\mathrm{P})
-closure of
G
. Being the group induced by
\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma)
acting on
T
, we have that
G
is
(\mathrm{P})
-closed. Hence
G=\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{T},(G(v)))
. The group
\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma)
is thus the restriction of
\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{T},(G(v)))
to the set of graph vertices in
\mathbf{T}
; that is,
\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{T},(G(v)))\big|_{V\Gamma}
.

Now we reverse this process, and show how any local action diagram
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
with the above structure gives rise to the automorphism group of a nontrivial, vertex-transitive graph with connectivity one. We construct
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
as follows.

(B1)

Choose a nonempty set
\{\hat{\Lambda}_{i}:i\in I\}
of pairwise non-isomorphic,
2
-connected graphs with
\{\hat{\Lambda}_{i}^{(j)}:j\in J_{i}\}
a decomposition of
V\hat{\Lambda}_{i}
into
\mathrm{Aut}(\hat{\Lambda}_{i})
-orbits, with
v_{i}^{(j)}
a representative vertex chosen from each orbit. For each
i\in I
and
j\in J_{i}
choose some cardinal
m_{i,j}\geq 1
,
such that if
|I|=1
(say
I=\{i\}
) and
|J_{i}|=1
(say
J_{i}=\{j\}
) then
m_{i,j}>1
; this latter condition is to guarantee that in the connectivity one graph we construct, every vertex lies in at least two lobes.

(B2)

The underlying graph
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
of the local action diagram will be a (possibly multi-edged) star graph, consisting of a central vertex
v^{*}
together with
|I|
other vertices labelled
L_{i}
for
i\in I
.

(B3)

For each
i\in I
we draw some undirected edges between
v^{*}
and the vertex labelled
L_{i}
; we draw precisely one edge for each rooted graph
(v_{i}^{(j)},\hat{\Lambda}_{i})
,
j\in J_{i}
. Recall that undirected edges consist of a pair of arcs, one in each direction; the two arcs in the edge corresponding to
(v_{i}^{(j)},\hat{\Lambda}_{i})
are labelled
(v_{i}^{(j)},\hat{\Lambda}_{i})
.

(B4)

For an arc
a\in o^{-1}(v^{*})
labelled
(v_{i}^{(j)},\hat{\Lambda}_{i})
, choose a colour set
C_{a}
of cardinality
m_{i,j}
. These colour sets should be disjoint, so that if an arc
b\in o^{-1}(v^{*})
differs from
a
then
C_{a}\cap C_{b}=\emptyset
. Set
X_{v^{*}}
to be the union over all such arcs
a
.

For the reverse arcs, we choose colours from the colour set
X_{L_{i}}=V\hat{\Lambda}_{i}
. Recall that the arcs in
o^{-1}(L_{i})
are labelled
(v_{i}^{(j)},\hat{\Lambda}_{i})
,
j\in J_{i}
, so the arcs in
o^{-1}(L_{i})
are in bijective correspondence with the orbits of
\mathrm{Aut}(\hat{\Lambda}_{i})
on
V\hat{\Lambda}_{i}
. The colour set of an arc
a\in o^{-1}(L_{i})
is taken to be the vertices in the
\mathrm{Aut}(\hat{\Lambda}_{i})
-orbit corresponding to
a
.

(B5)

For the vertex
L_{i}
of
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
, let
G(L_{i})
be
\mathrm{Aut}(\hat{\Lambda}_{i})
, viewed as a subgroup of
\mathrm{Sym}(V\hat{\Lambda}_{i})
.
Let
G(v^{*})
be the direct product
\prod_{i\in I,j\in J_{i}}S_{m_{i,j}}\leq\mathrm{Sym}(X_{v^{*}})=\mathrm{Sym}(\bigsqcup X_{a})
,
where each component acts on
X_{a}
for the arc
a\in o^{-1}(v^{*})
corresponding to
(v_{i}^{(j)},\hat{\Lambda}_{i})
. Note that
G(v^{*})
is closed in the permutation topology on
\mathrm{Sym}(X_{v^{*}})
.

We now have a local action diagram
\Delta^{\prime\prime}=(\Gamma^{\prime\prime},(X_{a}),(G(v))
. Let
\mathbf{T^{\prime\prime}}
be a
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
-tree, and let
U:=\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{T^{\prime\prime}},(G(v)))
. We will show that
U
arises from a connectivity one graph.

We construct a simple graph
\Gamma
with connectivity one as follows (see
[
6
]
for a more general free product construction along similar lines).
Let
\Gamma_{0}
be the graph consisting of a single vertex
v^{*}
. For each
i\in I
and
j\in J_{i}
, glue
m_{i,j}
copies of
\hat{\Lambda}_{i}
to
v^{*}
by associating
v^{*}
with the vertex
v_{i}^{(j)}\in V\hat{\Lambda}_{i}
. Call the graph thus obtained
\Gamma_{1}
.
For
n>1
construct
\Gamma_{n}
by, for each vertex
v\in V\Gamma_{n-1}\smallsetminus V\Gamma_{n-2}
, gluing lobes to
v
in the following way. Notice that there is a single lobe of
\Gamma_{n-1}
that contains
v
. This lobe is isomorphic to
\hat{\Lambda}_{i^{\prime}}
for some
i^{\prime}\in I
, with
v
lying in some
\mathrm{Aut}(\hat{\Lambda}_{i^{\prime}})
-orbit
\hat{\Lambda}_{i^{\prime}}^{(j^{\prime})}
. We glue
m_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}-1
new copies of
\hat{\Lambda}_{i^{\prime}}
to
v
by associating
v
with the vertex
v_{i^{\prime}}^{(j^{\prime})}\in V\hat{\Lambda}_{i^{\prime}}
. Then, for all remaining
i,j
, glue
m_{i,j}
new copies of
\hat{\Lambda}_{i}
to
v
by associating
v
with the vertex
v_{i}^{(j)}\in V\hat{\Lambda}_{i}
. The limit of this process as
n\rightarrow\infty
is a graph
\Gamma
which is simple, nontrivial and has connectivity one; any lobe of
\Gamma
is isomorphic to
\hat{\Lambda}_{i}
for some
i\in I
.

Let
T
be the block-cut-vertex tree of
\Gamma
and let
G
be the subgroup of
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
induced by the action of
\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma)
on
T
. We now apply our prior analysis (A
1
)–(A
4
) to obtain the associated local action diagram
\Delta(T,G)
of
G
and the associated
\Delta
-tree structure
\mathbf{T}
on
T
. By construction,
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
and
\Delta
are the same, and so by Lemma
3.7
we can consider
\mathbf{T}
and
\mathbf{T^{\prime\prime}}
to be identical. In particular, the set
V\Gamma\subseteq VT
of graph vertices in
\mathbf{T}
corresponds to
\pi^{-1}(v^{*})
in
\mathbf{T^{\prime\prime}}
.
We thus have
U=\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{T^{\prime\prime}},(G(v)))=\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{T},(G(v)))=G
, and so
\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma)=\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{T^{\prime\prime}},(G(v)))\big|_{\pi^{-1}(v^{*})}
.

We summarise the above in the following theorem.

Theorem 7.2
.

Automorphism groups of simple, nontrivial, vertex-transitive graphs
\Gamma
with vertex connectivity one are precisely the groups
\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{T^{\prime\prime}},(G(v)))\big|_{\pi^{-1}(v^{*})}
where
\mathbf{T^{\prime\prime}}
is a
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
-tree constructed via (B
1
)–(B
5
) above.

7.3
Building more compactly generated simple groups

Recall that Corollary
1.10
provides straightforward conditions that allow us to construct a local action diagram that will yield a group in
\mathscr{S}
. In particular, for each leaf of the local action diagram, we must provide a transitive subdegree-finite permutation group that is nontrivial, compactly generated and generated by point stabilisers. In fact, given a compactly generated t.d.l.c. group
G
, then every compact open subgroup
U
of
G
will give rise to a closed transitive subdegree-finite action of
G
on
G/U
. The only difficulty is in making sure that the action of
G
on
G/U
is generated by point stabilisers. However, this latter condition will hold, for example, whenever
U\neq\{1\}
and
G
does not admit any proper discrete quotient. Indeed, if
H
is the closed normal subgroup of
G
generated by point stabilisers in
G
then
G/H
in its action on the set of
H
-orbits is semi-regular and thus discrete.

As an illustration, we prove Theorem
1.14
, which shows that we can ‘combine’ finitely many groups in
\mathscr{S}
(chosen from
\mathscr{S}
arbitrarily) to make another group in
\mathscr{S}
of the form
\mathbf{U}(\Delta)
for a suitably chosen
\Delta
.

Proof of Theorem
1.14
.

Define
\Delta=(\Gamma,(X_{a}),(G(v)))
as follows:

\Gamma
is a star with central vertex
v_{0}
, leaves
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
, and exactly one arc
a_{i}
from
v_{0}
to
v_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n
.
If
n=1
we set
G(v_{0})=\mathrm{Sym}(3)
acting on
X_{a_{1}}=\{1,2,3\}
. If
n\geq 2
we set
X_{a_{i}}=\{i\}
for
1\leq i\leq n
and let
G(v_{0})
be the trivial group.

For
1\leq i\leq n
, let
G(v_{i})
be
G_{i}
acting by left translation on the left coset space
G_{i}/U_{i}=:X_{\overline{a_{i}}}
.
It is now easy to see that we have defined a valid local action diagram, and taking the action of
\mathbf{U}(\Delta)
on its defining tree, all the conditions of Corollary
1.10
(ii) are immediately apparent. Thus
\mathbf{U}(\Delta)\in\mathscr{S}
.

Let
(T,\pi,\mathcal{L})
be the
\Delta
-tree defining
\mathbf{U}(\Delta)
. For
1\leq i\leq n
let
v^{*}_{i}\in\pi^{-1}(v_{i})
and let
O_{i}=\mathbf{U}(\Delta)_{v^{*}_{i}}
. Then the action of
O_{i}
on
o^{-1}(v^{*}_{i})
is exactly
G(v_{i})
, which is isomorphic to
G_{i}
; indeed, by Proposition
4.8
, the action homomorphism of
O_{i}
on
o^{-1}(v^{*}_{i})
splits, so
O_{i}\cong K_{i}\rtimes G_{i}
where
K_{i}
is the kernel of the action. In turn,
K_{i}
fixes an arc, hence is compact by Proposition
6.3
.
∎

A similar construction of groups in
\mathscr{S}
, this time as automorphism groups of locally finite graphs of connectivity one, can be extracted from Section
7.2
.

Proposition 7.3
.

Let
n
be a positive integer and let
\hat{\Lambda}_{1},\dots,\hat{\Lambda}_{n}
be pairwise non-isomorphic locally finite simple graphs, each of which is
2
-connected. Suppose that for
1\leq i\leq n
, the automorphism group
\mathrm{Aut}(\hat{\Lambda}_{i})
is vertex-transitive and generated by vertex stabilisers. Let
m_{1},\dots,m_{n}
be positive integers, where
m_{1}\geq 3
if
n=1
. Form the simple vertex-transitive graph
\Gamma
of connectivity one as in (B
1
)–(B
5
) of Section
7.2
, so that
\Gamma
has lobe types
\hat{\Lambda}_{1},\dots,\hat{\Lambda}_{n}
and each vertex belongs to
m_{i}
lobes of the
i
-th type. Then
\Gamma
is locally finite and
\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma)\in\mathscr{S}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
7.2
, as a topological group we can identify
\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma)
with
\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{T^{\prime\prime}},(G(v)))
, where
\mathbf{T^{\prime\prime}}
is a
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
-tree and
\Delta^{\prime\prime}=(\Gamma^{\prime\prime},(X_{a}),(G(v))
is constructed as in (B
1
)–(B
5
) of Section
7.2
. It is clear from the construction that
\Gamma
is locally finite, specifically of degree
\sum^{n}_{i=1}m_{i}\deg(\hat{\Lambda}_{i})
. It remains to check that
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
satisfies the conditions of Corollary
1.10
(ii).

Now
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
has
n+1
vertices
v^{*},v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
, where
v^{*}
represents the vertices of
\Gamma
and for
1\leq i\leq n
, the vertex
v_{i}
corresponds to the
i
-th type of lobe. For
1\leq i\leq n
we have
G(v_{i})=\mathrm{Aut}(\hat{\Lambda}_{i})
acting on
V\hat{\Lambda}_{i}
; since this is a transitive action, there is exactly one undirected edge between
v^{*}
and
v_{i}
. There are no other edges in
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
, so
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
is a finite tree. The fact that
\hat{\Lambda}_{i}
is connected and locally finite ensures that
G(v_{i})
has compact point stabilisers, and the fact that
G(v_{i})
acts transitively ensures it is nontrivial and compactly generated (see for example
[
12
, Theorem 1
+
]
). Meanwhile,
G(v^{*})
is the permutation group acting on
\sum^{n}_{i=1}m_{i}
points as a product of symmetric groups, acting independently on orbits of size
m_{i}
: this permutation group is generated by point stabilisers with the sole exception of the case
n=1
and
m_{1}=2
, which we have excluded by hypothesis. Thus the local actions of
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
are all compactly generated, subdegree-finite and generated by point stabilisers. If
n=1
the leaves of
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
are
v^{*}
and
v_{1}
: we have ensured
G(v^{*})
is nontrivial in this case by setting
m_{1}\geq 3
. Otherwise the leaves are
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
, all of which have nontrivial associated local actions. We have now verified all the conditions of Corollary
1.10
(ii), and hence
\mathbf{U}(\mathbf{T^{\prime\prime}},(G(v)))
is as in Corollary
1.10
(i); in particular,
\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma)\in\mathscr{S}
.
∎

8
Questions

In this section we highlight some questions that might shape further research on local action diagrams.

The theory of local action diagrams developed in this paper is a classification of groups acting on trees with property
(\mathrm{P})
. It is plausible that one could develop a companion theory for groups acting on trees with property
(\mathrm{P}_{k})
, using a modified version of the local action action diagram that is built around
k
-arcs rather than arcs.

Question 1
.

Is it possible to classify groups acting on trees with property
(\mathrm{P}_{k})
via a modified version of the local action diagram?

Let
T_{d}
be the regular tree of finite degree
d
and let
N_{d}
be the number of conjugacy classes of vertex-transitive
(\mathrm{P})
-closed subgroups of
\mathrm{Aut}(T_{d})
. As discussed in Section
7.1
,
N_{d}
is the number of
\mathrm{Sym}(d)
-conjugacy classes of pairs
(H,r)
, where
H
is a subgroup of
\mathrm{Sym}(d)
and
r
is a function on the set of
H
-orbits on
[d]
such that
r^{2}=\mathrm{id}
. For comparison, the number of conjugacy classes of Burger–Mozes groups of degree
d
is simply the number
C_{d}:=|\mathrm{Sym}(d)\backslash\mathrm{Sub}(\mathrm{Sym}(d))|
of conjugacy classes of subgroups of
\mathrm{Sym}(d)
. The problem of enumerating the vertex-transitive
(\mathrm{P})
-closed actions on locally finite trees is thus reduced to a fairly natural problem in finite permutation groups. In Appendix
A
we give a GAP (
[
8
]
) implementation due to S. Tornier that can be used to perform these enumerations for small
d
, and it would be very interesting to continue in this vein, both for larger values of
d
and for biregular trees, to build a database of
(\mathrm{P})
-closed actions. Given the limitations on what is practical to compute exactly, it would also be interesting to have some bounds on the growth rates of
N_{d}
and
C_{d}
coming from the theory of finite permutation groups.

Question 2
.

What are the asymptotics of the number
N_{d}
as a function of
d
? How much faster does it grow than
C_{d}
?

In this paper, we provide a method for determining the local action diagrams of subgroups of
(\mathrm{P})
-closed groups, via the notion of local subaction diagrams. However, as noted in Remark
4.5
, this description falls short of a classification in two respects: (i) in general determining all local subaction diagrams of a given local action diagram appears to be intractable, and (ii) local subaction diagrams only permit the classification of
(\mathrm{P})
-closed subgroups of a
(\mathrm{P})
-closed action
(T,G)
up to conjugacy in
\mathrm{Aut}(T)
, rather than up to conjugacy in
G
.

Question 3
.

Is it possible to extend the idea of a local subaction diagram to address limitations (i) and (ii)? Such a structure will need to carry more information than a local subaction diagram, but possibly not significantly more.

Some insight into Question
3
could come from considering the following question, which cannot be meaningfully addressed using local subaction diagrams alone.

Question 4
.

Suppose
G\leq\mathrm{Aut}(T)
is
(\mathrm{P})
-closed. Can one classify the open subgroups of
G
that act with translation using only the local action diagram of
(T,G)
?

A general theme of interesting research would be to continue to identify global properties of
G\leq\mathrm{Aut}(T)
that are completely characterised by properties of the action of
G^{(\mathrm{P})}
on
T
. By the results of Section
3
, such properties can equivalently be described as those properties of
G
characterised by its local action diagram. Such properties might be called
locally determined global properties of
(T,G)
. For example, by Theorem
1.4
, having geometrically dense action is a locally determined global property.

Question 5
.

What are further examples of locally determined global properties of
(T,G)
?

Theorem
1.14
suggests an interesting preorder on
\mathscr{S}
: say that
G_{1}\prec_{OK}G_{2}
if there is an open subgroup
O
of
G_{2}
and a compact normal subgroup
K
of
O
such that
O/K\cong G_{1}
. The theorem shows that
\prec_{OK}
is a directed preorder on
\mathscr{S}
, that is, any finite subset has an upper bound, and moreover, within
\mathscr{S}
, the groups admitting faithful
(\mathrm{P})
-closed actions on trees are cofinal. On the other hand, every element of
\mathscr{S}
is ‘close to the bottom’ in the following sense: given
G\in\mathscr{S}
, there are only
\aleph_{0}
compactly generated open subgroups, each of which has at most finitely many quotients in
\mathscr{S}
, so for each
G\in\mathscr{S}
there are at most
\aleph_{0}
different isomorphism types of
H\in\mathscr{S}
such that
H\prec_{OK}G
. On the other hand, by
[
19
]
,
\mathscr{S}
as a whole has
2^{\aleph_{0}}
isomorphism classes. In particular, writing
\mathscr{S}/OK
for the poset generated by
\prec_{OK}
, it follows that
\mathscr{S}/OK
has infinite ascending chains. We are naturally led to a ‘well-foundedness’ question:

Question 6
.

Does
\mathscr{S}/OK
have infinite descending chains? That is, does there exist a sequence
G_{0},G_{1},\dots
in
\mathscr{S}
such that
G_{i+1}\prec_{OK}G_{i}
but
G_{i}\not\prec_{OK}G_{i+1}
for all
i
?

It would also be interesting to find a nontrivial
\prec_{OK}
-equivalence class, i.e. a set
\mathcal{X}
of two or more pairwise nonisomorphic groups in
\mathscr{S}
such that for any
G,H\in\mathcal{X}
, then
G
can be realised as a quotient with compact kernel of an open subgroup of
H
and
vice versa
.
